# Agent Harness Spec

**Status:** Draft v0.4
**Scope:** Design specification for a git-backed agent harness with branch-per-dispatch context management. v0.4 folds in the `harness` repo design exploration (2026-07): brazen becomes the provider layer (§4), budgets and sandboxed tools join the milestone chain (§12). The 2026-07 workspace-substrate rewrite (§2.2–§2.6, §7, §9.2) lands one repo per workspace, config branches, message-based child returns, and compaction as the sole reorganization — landed by rebase-forward (§2.6), leaving no merge in the system.

---

## 1. Overview

This document specifies an agent harness in which agent context is managed as a git repository. A **workspace** is one git repository holding a config lineage and the agent tree forked from it (§2.1, §2.2); every agent is a branch plus a linked worktree, forked off a ref. Every dispatch creates a branch. A finished agent returns its result as a message — a **reply** to whoever last prompted it, an **obituary** to the agent that dispatched it (§2.6); a root agent's branch persists, and the next user message resumes it in place (§2.4). Any sender — the user or another agent — may deposit a message into any agent's inbox; the message is delivered at the recipient's next step boundary, and a deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver (§2.11). Within a branch, steps land as linear commits; a step that itself dispatches forks a child branch off the commit where the dispatch landed. State flows between components through the filesystem (§3.1); commands between procedures flow through the `litany` CLI (§3.4). There is no third channel — no *divergent* library API, no in-process sidechannel, no resident broker; the command surface has two bindings, an exec'd binary and a linked library port, held to one surface by CI-enforced parity (§3.4) — and no process holds state across its own termination.

The architecture optimizes for four properties:

1. **Inspectability.** The complete state of any agent, at any point in its history, is a git ref. Replay, debugging, and counterfactual forking are first-class operations.
2. **Uniformity.** User-to-agent dispatch, agent-to-child dispatch, and agent self-reflection all use the same primitive: fork a branch off a ref, do work, return a result message (or answer the user, for a root agent). There is no special path for user input. There is likewise no special sender: a message into a running agent (§2.11) is the same deposit whether the user or another agent makes it.
3. **Testability.** Workflow (prompts, sequencing, context assembly rules) is configuration, not code. Experiments are config diffs, measurable against a task suite.
4. **Regenerability.** Any process can die at any time without losing state. Disk is durable; processes are disposable. Components — harness, tool subprocesses, provider adapters, frontends — restart independently because none hold state across their own termination; `litany advance <workspace> <agent>` (§6) is the operation that drives the workflow chain forward, and crash recovery is the same `litany advance` invocation that runs the chain in normal operation. No process is load-bearing; disk is.

### 1.1 Non-goals for v1

The following are explicitly out of scope for v1:

- Multi-tenancy or multi-user isolation. Single user assumed. (Workspace isolation, §2.2, separates *concerns*, not *principals*.)
- Distributed execution. Single machine, single harness process.
- Tool sandboxing. Tools run with user privileges; no capability restriction. (A v1 non-goal only: specced as milestone v1.1, §12.)
- Remote git operations. Workspace repositories are never pushed anywhere.
- Secrets management infrastructure. Env vars injected at tool execution, referenced by name in config. No vault, no rotation.
- Network-filesystem workspaces. A workspace on a network filesystem (NFS and the like) is an unsupported configuration: the executor lock (§2.11) is kernel-local `flock(2)`, advisory-to-broken across hosts, so two executors could silently drive one branch. Keep the workspace on a local disk.

---

## 2. Core Concepts

### 2.1 Terms

All terminology below is load-bearing and is used exclusively in the senses defined here. Terms in `docs/TAXONOMY.md` are the ambient field context; where the taxonomy flags a term as contested, this spec picks one sense and stays in it.

The terms form a containment ladder: a **workspace** (one git repository) holds a **config** lineage and the **agent** tree forked from it; an agent runs a sequence of **steps**; a step is one **model call**, which comprises one or more **attempts**. Each term is defined once here and referenced, not redefined, elsewhere.

> **Ladder transition (nearly complete).** This table is terminology-ladder v2: the structural primitive is the **agent** (not "conversation"), its container is the **workspace** (not "conversation repo"), and the versioned configuration it forks from is a **config**. The workspace-substrate rewrite has landed: §§2.2–2.6, §7, and §9.2 are rewritten in the ladder, §§2.7–2.11 and `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` amended onto it, and the pre-ladder *mechanics* — the `main`-rooted history, merge-back, the frozen-copy bootstrap — are gone everywhere, not merely re-worded. What remains is re-voicing: §3, §5, §6, §8, and the historical notes of §12 still say "conversation" / "conversation repo" / "subagent" in passing prose; read those as *agent* / *workspace* / *child agent*. Where a term's full mechanics land in a later section, this table defines the term and defers the mechanics.

| Term | Meaning | Relationship |
|---|---|---|
| **Workspace** | The isolation boundary: one git repository holding a config lineage and the agent tree forked from it. No mechanism reaches across workspaces — isolation is structural (there is no cross-workspace channel), not a permission check. | The outermost container. One workspace per repository. |
| **Config** | The contents of one configuration commit — souls, tool enablements, capability grants (§4.3), workflow, and manifest. A descriptively-named **config branch** is a config's version lineage; its head commit is the current config. "Multiple configs" means multiple config branches; there is no `main`. | Versioned by branch. An agent's substrate forks off a config branch's head by default. |
| **Agent** | One living instantiation: a goal, a growing context, a step loop, and a termination — the field-consensus sense of an LLM running tools in a loop toward a goal (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §1). Its **substrate** is a branch plus a worktree, forked off a ref (default: the head of a config branch). | The primitive. A *root* agent is forked by a user message; a *child* agent by a dispatch tool call from a parent (§2.5). Parent/child is recorded provenance, not a category — there is no separate "subagent" kind. |
| **Exchange** | The span between a user message and the terminal response that answers it — a UX label over one stretch of a root agent's linear history. Owns no branch, no merge, no lifecycle of its own. | Non-structural. A view over an agent's steps, surfaced because users think in exchanges. |
| **Step** | One model call and the tool calls it emits. | Child of an agent; structurally bounded by the model call, not by tool completion. Lands as linear commits within the agent's branch. |
| **Model call** | One execution of a model to produce output. | Atomic. The defining event of a step; each step has exactly one. |
| **Tool call** | The model's structured request to invoke a named tool. | Emitted by a model call; structural child of its emitting step (even if it resolves temporally during a later step). |
| **API call** | One HTTP request to a provider endpoint. | 1:1 with attempts by construction (§4.4); retries make a model call span several. |
| **Attempt** | One invocation of the provider adapter for a step's model call — exactly one API call by construction (§4.4). | A model call comprises one or more attempts (§2.10); each attempt lands as one segment in the step's `response.json`. |
| **brazen** | The provider adapter project (binary `bz`, crate `brazen`): one stateless binary adapting every provider and wire protocol behind a canonical request/event pipe contract. | The only component that knows provider wire protocols (§4.4); its specs define the canonical vocabulary. |
| **Canonical request / canonical event** | brazen's typed request shape and its `v=1` streaming event vocabulary (brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3). | The adapter wire contract; `response.json` is JSONL of canonical events (§2.3, §4.4). |
| **Dispatch** | The event that spawns a child agent with a goal. Two forms: a user message (spawns a root agent) and a tool call targeting a child (spawns a child agent off the commit where the dispatch landed on the parent branch). | Creates a branch. |
| **Branch** | The git container for an agent. | Every agent has exactly one branch; every dispatch creates one. |
| **Id** | An agent's **identifier**: its full hyphenated descent (§2.3), which is also its branch name, its worktree directory, and its `steps/` / `inbox/` namespace keys. Minted at the fork, unique per workspace. | Exactly one per agent, always present. Purely an identifier — it carries no display semantics, and nothing derives a label from it. |
| **Name** | An agent's **display** discriminator, settled at the dispatch that creates it — supplied by the dispatcher, or **minted** from the embedded wordlist on omission (§2.3, yog bl-aca4): one unbroken word — a minted one is **two words joined in PascalCase** (`PeachHollow`, bl-79a2), which is still one unbroken token and one path component — unique among the workspace's living agents, stored under the agent and immutable thereafter. What an operator or a peer agent *speaks*; `litany message` accepts it in place of an id (§2.11). | One per newly created agent (unnamed is a readable pre-mint state, not a creatable one). Lawfully **recyclable** — retention (§9.2) deletes the ref and frees the name — which is exactly why it is not the id: ids must never collide. |
| **Goal** | The stated objective handed to an agent at dispatch. | One per agent; not rewritten during execution; pinned at the head of context for every model call on the branch (§2.8). |
| **Soul** | The system prompt handed to an agent at dispatch, drawn from the governing config commit's `souls/<role>.md` (§2.2) and overwritten on the branch's dispatch commit. | One per agent; composed into every model call on the branch as the system message. |
| **Message** | Content addressed to an *existing* agent by a sender — the user or any agent. Deposited into the recipient's inbox; delivered at a step boundary as a committed worktree file (§2.11). | The steering primitive. Sender is recorded provenance; the recipient treats every sender uniformly. Unqualified "message" means this primitive; wire-level messages are always written qualified (user message, assistant message). |
| **Inbox** | The per-agent queue directory holding deposited-but-undelivered messages; outside every worktree, namespaced by agent id (§2.11). | Deposit target for messages (§2.11). |
| **Executor** | The process currently driving a branch's step loop — `litany prompt` (root) or a `litany advance` re-entry (§6, the child and revival driver); the process `litany stop` discovers by its executor-lock fd (§2.9, §2.11). `litany dispatch` is *not* an executor — it is writer-shaped (fork plus front-door deposit, §2.5), and the driver its deposit launches is `litany advance`. | At most one per branch, guaranteed by the executor lock (§2.11). Distinct from the *tool executor* (§3.2), a role inside it. |
| **Transcript** | The branch-scoped sequence of committed worktree files under `messages/` recording, in order, everything that entered the agent's context: delivered messages (§2.11), each step's assistant output, each tool call's result (§2.3). | The agent's model-facing history. Entries are immutable — change is append or delete, never edit-in-place. Never process memory, never a step record. |
| **Dispatch entry** | The transcript entry an agent's *opening prompt* landed as — `messages/001-…`, delivered by the step-1 drain from the deposit the dispatch made (§2.11): a root's first user message, a child's dispatch message. | The goal's second projection: the same text `goal.md` carries (§2.8), written by the same dispatch and never rewritten. Not compaction-eligible (§2.7). |
| **Work product** | An in-worktree, non-transcript file — the artifacts an agent edits or writes (code, docs, data) as distinct from its transcript. | Resolves the workspace/worktree-contents collision: the *workspace* is the repo-level boundary; what fills a worktree and is not transcript is a work product. |

**Retired structural terms** (v0.1–v0.3 vocabulary, superseded by the ladder above; may linger in passing prose per the transition note):

- **"conversation" / "conversation repo"** — the former primitive and its repo. The living instantiation is an **agent**; its container is a **workspace**. Where a later section still says "conversation" / "conversation repo," read *agent* / *workspace*.
- **"invocation"** — retired since v0.3 (general-field "model invocation" ≈ model call is acceptable informally, never as a structural unit).
- **"subagent"** — deleted as a *category*. A child agent is an agent; parent/child is provenance (§2.5), not a kind. Never a structural noun of its own.

**Banned usage:**

- **"call"** without a qualifier, *as a term for an interaction with a model, tool, or API* — use model call, tool call, or API call. The ban is on that sense alone, and every other English sense of the word is unaffected; the **programming sense is explicitly sanctioned** (bl-1966), because it is the one that recurs here and it names something else entirely — what one piece of code does to another. *Call site*, *callback*, *callee*, *function call*, *system call*, and the verb *to call a function* therefore stay as they are, tree-wide, and need no qualifier: no reader can mistake `prompt::tool::envelope::render`'s call sites for model calls, and the obvious rewrite is unavailable anyway ("invocation" is retired, above). Stated here so the rule is read, not re-derived.
- **"turn"** — incompatible vendor meanings (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §1).
- **"session"** — underdefined, per-framework overloaded, and colliding with the transport/connection sense (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §3). Stays banned as an interaction-span term; its only sanctioned use here is the POSIX process-session sense — the `setsid` session a detached spawn leads (§2.11).
- **"compression"** — reserved for model-weights quantization; the harness operation is compaction.
- **"exchange"** as a *structural* term — it is the UX span defined in the table (no branch, no merge, no lifecycle); structural claims belong to the underlying agent.
- **"thread"** as an agent-instance term — considered and rejected: the running instance is an **agent** (agent-as-instance already names it), and "thread" is spoken for by §3.1 ("Processes, not threads," the OS-thread sense) and the field's server-side message-container sense (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §3). Its only sanctioned use here is the §3.1 OS-concurrency sense.
- **"agent"** in the *config/profile* sense — "agent" is pinned to the running-instance sense above; the versioned configuration it forks from is a **config**, never an "agent" (contrast the OpenAI-SDK "agent = config object" reading, `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §1).

**Other terms of art not defined in this document or in `docs/TAXONOMY.md` require explicit definition or user approval before use.**

**Cross-document section references name their document (bl-1966).** Section numbers collide across documents, so a reference carrying only a number tells a reader nothing: `docs/DESIGN_MCP_BRIDGE.md` had a §6 of its own *and* cited this document's §6 budgets, unmarked, and §4.4 here cited brazen's §3.1 / §8 / §5.10 with no path to reach them. The rule: **in a document that numbers its own sections, a bare `§N` is that document's own section, and every reference to another document's section names the document** — `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §3, brazen `specs/architecture.md` §8. A document that numbers no sections of its own has nothing to collide with, so its bare `§N` can only be this document's; `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` states that at its head, and every other unnumbered document in the tree reads the same way. The rule is about the reference, not the term, so it binds every document in the tree rather than only the ones with a taxonomy entry. A **quotation** is exempt in both directions: quoted spec prose and quoted program output are reproduced verbatim, so the numbering inside a quotation is the quoted source's. **Compliance:** this document, `docs/DESIGN_MCP_BRIDGE.md`, `docs/TAXONOMY.md`, and `docs/USER_STORIES.md` comply — the last two swept per-reference and given a convention note at their heads (bl-8766). Every other document in the tree numbers no sections of its own and falls under the unnumbered reading above. The rule reaches past the document tree to the **strings the binary prints**: a refusal a user reads is a citation like any other, and `provider adapter "bz" not found (§4.4 …)` gave that reader no way to know which document §4.4 lives in — so every section citation in user-facing output is written `ARCH §N` (bl-da57, swept). Rust doc comments are the unnumbered reading again, not an exception: source comments number no sections of their own, no user reads them, and their bare `§N` stays this document's.

### 2.2 The Workspace

A **workspace** (§2.1) is one git repository: the isolation boundary, holding a config lineage and the agent tree forked from it. It lives at `<data-root>/workspaces/<workspace>/`.

**One repo per workspace; worktrees are the cloning mechanism.** Every agent in a workspace is a branch plus a linked worktree of the *same* repository (§2.3). The alternative — a repository per agent — was considered and rejected: it buys per-agent blast radius at the price of the system's point. Interoperability is the point: inter-agent messaging (§2.11) addresses any agent in the tree, and starting an agent off *any* ref — another agent's historical commit, a parallel fan of N forks off one commit (§2.3) — requires the refs to share one object store. A git worktree already provides all the isolation an agent needs (its own checkout, its own branch); a second repository would add isolation nobody asked for and destroy the forkability everything else relies on.

**Isolation lives at the workspace boundary.** Separate workspaces are separate repositories, and no mechanism reaches across: no cross-workspace messaging, no cross-workspace fork, no shared ref. The no-cross-talk guarantee is structural — there is no channel to misuse — so two workspaces that must not mix (work for two different employers, say) cannot, by construction rather than by permission check. (§1.1's single-user assumption is unchanged: workspaces separate *concerns*, not *principals*.)

**Harness root, split by XDG lifetime.** Installation-global state outside any workspace splits along XDG lifetimes into two homes:

- **config root** — `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/litany` (default `~/.config/litany`). Hand-edited declarations: the global `models.yaml` (§4.2) and the `workflows/` templates from which config commits are authored (below, §6) — seeded with the shipped default, `basic-agentic-loop.yaml`.
- **data root** — `$XDG_DATA_HOME/litany` (default `~/.local/share/litany`). Machine-populated state: the `workspaces/` tree and the `skills/` and `tools/` pools (§3.3).

> **Two senses of "config."** Bare **config** is always the ladder primitive (§2.1): the contents of one configuration commit inside a workspace. **Config root** — always written with the "root" suffix — is the XDG directory above: install-global, outside every workspace. The suffix is the disambiguator; this document never uses bare "config" for the directory, and never "config root" for the primitive.

`LITANY_HOME`, when set and non-empty, is the single override that **collapses both roots to that one directory** — one env var keeps parallel testing, alternate installs, and sandboxed replay isolated. This aligns litany's resolution with brazen's own XDG-style config resolution (§4.4). Where a statement below does not depend on the split, `<harness-root>` names whichever root holds the artifact in question.

**Founding the harness root: `litany prime`.** One verb **founds the installation substrate** — the harness root a fresh install has none of. `litany prime` resolves the config root and data root exactly as every other verb does (the XDG split above, collapsed by `LITANY_HOME`) and lays down what a ready installation carries: the default global `models.yaml` (§4.2), the `tools/` schema pool and the `skills/` pool (§3.3), the `workflows/` template pool holding the shipped default — `basic-agentic-loop.yaml`, the **basic agentic loop** (§6), read out of the very asset `litany new` freezes into `config/default`, so the pool entry and the freeze are one declaration and not two (bl-fcd8) — and the empty `workspaces/` directory. It is **seed-if-absent throughout — it creates what is absent and never clobbers what exists**, so it is idempotent (a second run changes nothing) and safe over a live install: `models.yaml` is hand-edited by contract (§4.2), so a curated one survives, as does any hand-edited or operator-added pool entry. The shipped assets are **embedded in the binary** (the same `include_dir` discipline the config template uses, below), so the binary is self-contained — priming a fresh `LITANY_HOME` never reaches back to a source tree. `prime` is product-less **on stdout** — it has nothing to hand a caller (the one-product convention, §3.4) — but it is not silent: on success it **reports what it founded, on stderr** (bl-7e9e). Three lines: the config root and what lives in it, the data root and what lives in it, and this run's seed-if-absent split — files written because they were absent, files already there and left alone. That split is also the re-run answer, with no second code path: `0 files seeded, N already present` *is* "already founded". The report exists because `prime` is the one verb whose entire effect is on disk, in two XDG directories the invocation does not name and the user did not choose; a silent success left a fresh install unable to distinguish a founded root from a no-op, and unable to find the state it had just created. It is a **confirmation, not a product** — hence stderr, which is also what keeps `prime` composable in a pipeline that reads stdout. Founding done on the way to another verb (`litany new`, below) reports nothing: there the founding is a precondition, not the act asked for, and that verb's stdout carries its own one product. **`make install` invokes `litany prime`** rather than duplicating the seeding, so the verb is the single source of truth for what a ready installation looks like (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`, single source of truth; §3.4 front door). The driving consumer is a nested world: a manager pointing `LITANY_HOME` at a directory it owns seeds it with `LITANY_HOME=<dir> litany prime`, then drives ordinary verbs against the same home.

**Founding is a step of `litany new`, not a precondition on it.** `litany new` reads the data-root pools to author its `descriptions/**` snapshot (§3.3), so it **runs the founding routine above before it authors** — the same routine `prime` is, invoked, not copied. Seed-if-absent makes this free: on a primed install nothing changes, and on an unprimed one the pools exist by the time they are read. This is what keeps an unseeded harness root from being a special case — it is the general path with empty inputs, needing no flag, no ordering rule the operator must know, and no "run `litany prime` first" refusal. Without it `new` exits 0 on a fresh root having authored a config commit with no `descriptions/` at all, and every agent forked off that commit sees an empty toolset — a silent violation of §3.3 descriptions-always. Verbs that operate on an *existing* workspace do not found: a workspace exists only because `new` created it, so its install is founded by construction.

Directory layout:

```
<data-root>/workspaces/<workspace>/
├── repo.git/                     # the workspace repository (bare): config/* and agents/* refs
├── steps/<agent-id>/             # diagnostic records, per agent; outside every worktree (§2.3)
│   ├── driver.log                # this agent's detached drivers' stderr, appended (§2.11)
│   └── NNN/                      # one step record
│       ├── meta.json             # {commit, started_at, …} — branch tip at step-start
│       ├── request.json          # diagnostic; replay rebuilds the wire input from `commit`
│       ├── response.json         # JSONL of canonical events, attempt segments (§4.4)
│       ├── stderr.log            # the adapter's stderr, per attempt; empty on an ordinary run
│       └── tools/<tool-id>/      # input.json, output.json — diagnostic (§2.3, §3.3)
├── inbox/<agent-id>/             # deposited, undelivered messages; outside every worktree (§2.11)
└── agents/<agent-id>/            # one linked worktree per materialized agent (§2.3)
    ├── goal.md                   # branch-scoped — this agent's goal
    ├── soul.md                   # branch-scoped — this agent's system prompt
    ├── name                      # branch-scoped — this agent's display name, supplied or minted (§2.3); empty = unnamed (pre-mint stock only)
    ├── summary/NNN.md            # branch-scoped — this agent's compactions
    ├── messages/NNN-<origin>.*   # branch-scoped — the transcript (§2.3): delivered messages
    │                             #   (NNN-<sender>.md, §2.11), model output
    │                             #   (NNN-<model-id>.json), tool results (NNN-tool.json)
    ├── descriptions/             # tool + skill descriptions (inherited from the config commit)
    ├── skills/                   # loaded skill content (branch-scoped)
    └── …                         # work products (§2.1)
```

**Config branches.** A config branch (`config/<name>`, §2.3) is the version lineage of one config: souls (`souls/<role>.md`), workflow (`workflow.yaml`), context-assembly manifest (`manifest.yaml`), role → model mapping (`providers.yaml`), schema version (`version`), and the `descriptions/**` snapshot (§3.3). Config branches are descriptively named — `config/default`, `config/strict-verifier` — and there is no `main`: no branch in the workspace is a trunk, and none is privileged beyond what its name says. "Multiple configs" means multiple config branches. A workspace's first config commit is an orphan root; further config branches fork from existing ones or start fresh. Authoring a config commit is a user act (the branch advancement invariant, §2.3), harness-assisted: `litany` materializes a checkout, writes the files — from the `<config-root>/workflows/` templates and the data-root pools, including the `descriptions/**` snapshot (§3.3) — and commits. Two verbs cover this one act: `litany new` authors a workspace's *first* config commit (the orphan `config/default` root) as part of creation, and `litany config <workspace> [<name>]` authors every later one (§3.4) — **advancing** the named branch, **forking** a new one off an existing head (`--from <source>`), or starting a fresh **orphan** lineage (`--orphan`). Both refresh the `descriptions/**` snapshot from the data-root pools as part of the pass (§3.3 — its ongoing home); the checkout is materialized, edited (via `$EDITOR` or supplied files), committed, and torn down, and only this act moves a config branch.

**Teardown is structural, and a declined pass is a clean outcome.** The checkout is torn down on **every** exit path — the commit landing, a git decline, an editor failure, a refresh failure, the user saving nothing — because teardown is a drop guard around the checkout, not a step the happy path reaches. A pass therefore never leaves a `.config-author` to wedge the next one, and never leaves a `config/<name>` its own `--from` / `--orphan` created but never committed to: the ref belongs to the guard until the commit lands. A **declined pass** is one whose edit left the tree identical to its origin: there is nothing to commit, so nothing is authored, no branch moves, no ref survives, and the workspace is exactly as it was. It is a *success*, not a failure — authoring is a user act (§2.3) and the user may decline it by saving no change — so `litany config` exits 0 and names the branch that did not move as its one stdout line (the §3.4 one-product convention: empty stdout means a commit landed). Only a *killed* pass (SIGKILL, reboot) can leave the checkout behind, and the next pass clears it before materializing rather than refusing — §2.11's crash philosophy, "everything is on disk, so nothing is lost, only late, and the next touch heals it," applied here: the transient checkout holds no authored history, so healing it costs only the killed pass's unsaved edit.

**The workspace seed and the config-root `template/` override.** The control files `litany new` authors onto the orphan root come from a template **embedded in the binary** at build time (`include_dir` — the discipline `prime`'s assets share, above; the binary never reaches back to a source tree). Policy lives in config, not code: `litany new` resolves its seed set as the **union** of the embedded template files with any same-named file under `<config-root>/template/` **overriding** the embedded one; extra files in the override dir are included too. No new flag and no new verb — absence of the dir is the default and yields exactly the embedded template, and `litany prime` does **not** seed `template/`, because absence *is* the default (the pattern mirrors `models.yaml`: the shipped default covers a fresh install, a user-authored file wins). Under `LITANY_HOME` the config root collapses (above), so the override lives at `$LITANY_HOME/template/` — the shape a nested world uses to birth workspaces already pointed at the provider its host can actually authenticate (bl-e795's motivating case: a host with only a codex credential overriding `providers.yaml`).

**Fork chooses the lineage; resolution follows its tip (operator ruling 2026-09-01, bl-403b — "the conversation should not be pinned: configuration is changeable at any time, on any turn").** An agent forks off a config commit (default: its chosen config branch's head, §2.3), and what the fork settles is *which lineage governs* — never the moment. Control resolves, at **every step boundary**, from the **followed config commit**: the governing lineage's *current tip* (`workspace::current_config` — the governing ancestry query, then the config heads standing over it; exactly one distinct tip is followed, and diverged tips resolve the fork commit itself with a loud per-step notice until `litany retarget` settles the lineage). So a `litany config` edit reaches every running conversation on the lineage at its next step, with no act per agent. This **inverts the pre-ruling default** ("fork is the freeze"), and the inversion is priced honestly rather than hidden. What fork-time pinning bought: (1) *mid-conversation stability* — an agent's policy could not change under it without a per-agent act; the ruling holds that a config edit **is** the intended act, and its reach is the point (the motivating incident: a workspace's roles moved off a dead provider row and every running conversation kept refusing on it, pinned, with no surface saying so). (2) *Branch-only replay* — "which policy governed step N" was derivable from the branch's ancestry alone; it is now a fact about when the step ran. The step record still carries the policy's *effects* byte-for-byte (`request.json`, §2.3), an archive carries the lineage heads as of archiving (§9.2), and recording the resolved commit per step is filed (bl-e4a0). What still pins, each deliberately: a **step in flight** finishes on the config it started with (resolution is per-hop, §6); the **workflow mark** (§6) is a standing per-agent pin that wins over the followed tip; and the dispatch-time tree artifacts — the `descriptions/**` cut, the pinned `goal.md`/`soul.md`/`name` copies — remain the fork's (the descriptions refresh under a moved tip is filed, bl-37cd; the wire soul already follows, §2.3 composes it from resolution, not the tree copy). **`litany retarget` (§3.4) is re-scoped, not deprecated**: with same-lineage advances reaching agents by resolution alone, what retargets is a change of **lineage** — moving an agent onto a different `config/*` line, re-forking its ancestry so the follow derivation follows the target — or the settling of a diverged one; a target the agent already resolves is a clean no-op. "Live update" arrives only at the step boundary, never mid-step; the per-step boundary is what makes the follow safe.

**Retarget is a re-fork, and it is the compaction landing's own two moves (§2.6).** No merge appears anywhere, because §2.3's invariant is unconditional and §2.6 left no merge in the system to imitate:

1. **The base is a re-derived dispatch commit** — not the old one rebased, a *newly minted* one on top of the target config commit, through the fork's own machinery (§2.3 step 2). Everything config-shaped is re-derived there rather than replayed: the §3.3 descriptor cut is taken from the target commit (which is what §3.3 already requires of every fork — "the derivation reads that commit and never the tree it forked off"), the control-file removal is re-performed against the target (so the modify/delete a naive rebase would hit on `providers.yaml` — the new parent carries it, the old dispatch commit deletes it — never arises: the base already has it gone), and `goal.md` / `soul.md` / `name` are re-pinned from the sources the original fork used. The subject is the old one verbatim, because a branch's founding commit is identified by its subject and the checkpoint clock (§2.7) and role derivation (§6) both read it.
2. **The replay** is the agent's own post-dispatch commits, rebased onto the new base. Transcript entries are one immutable file each with monotonic names (§2.3), so this is conflict-free by the same construction compaction relies on, and the same stage-reading decline applies where it is violated (`refs/litany/conflicted/<agent-id>`, branch restored bit-for-bit by `rebase --abort`).

After it, `governing_config` — unchanged, still a pure ancestry query — answers the target commit. No new stored fact anywhere.

**The user act is a ref mark, so the single-writer rule is untouched.** `litany retarget <workspace> <agent-id> [--config <name>]` writes `refs/litany/retarget/<agent-id>` at the target config commit; the agent's **own executor** consumes it and lands it at the next `advance` step boundary, exactly where the compaction landing runs. §2.3 holds verbatim — the branch still advances by one writer, and that writer is still its executor — and ref marks as orthogonal, non-derivable per-agent state are the established pattern (`conflicted`, `budget-exhausted`, `abandoned`, `notify`, `cwd`, the §6 `workflow` mark). **Why this rewrite is legitimate** is §2.6's own argument on the other axis: a compactor's payload is the dispatching branch's own *context*, rewritten on purpose; a retarget's payload is the branch's own *policy*, re-forked on purpose. Same polarity, same landing, same writer. **Timing is semantics, not compromise**: the mark takes effect at the agent's next step, never mid-step — a config governs steps, and a retarget is in practice followed by a message, which *is* that next step.

**Retargeting a child re-parents it off its dispatcher's lineage, and that is priced rather than policed.** A root forks off a config commit, so re-forking it off another is exact. A *child* forks off its dispatcher's tip (§2.5) and reaches the config through it, so a re-fork moves its ancestry onto the target config commit and out from under the dispatcher's — its own tree, transcript and inherited work products all survive verbatim, but the fork point the §2.6 work-product transfer derives (`merge-base(dispatcher, terminal_ref)`) is no longer a commit on the dispatcher's branch, and that child's eventual return is **declined loudly** at `refs/litany/conflicted/<child-id>` instead of applying. Nothing is lost — the result message still delivers and the terminal ref preserves every byte (§2.6) — but the diff does not land. The ordinary operator act is therefore to retarget the **root**: children dispatched after it fork off the retargeted branch and inherit the new config for free. Retargeting a live child stays legal, because a lineage change is just as real there; it costs that child's transfer, and the cost is stated here rather than turned into a refusal.

Every refusal precedes the mark, so a declined retarget leaves no debris at all — the validity-before-fork discipline of the §6 budget gate and the §3.3 descriptor check: the workspace and the agent exist, the named config lineage exists, and the agent's role is granted only tools the target config describes. A target already governing the agent is a **clean no-op**, not an error. The mark is consumed in every outcome — landed, declined, or no-op — because in each the question has been answered, and a surviving mark would re-ask it at every subsequent boundary.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-22a5).** Shipped. The mark's one home is `src/workspace/retarget.rs` (the ref name, the read, the write, the consume); the landing's is `src/prompt/retarget/` — `mod.rs` (the pre-flights `preflight`, the boundary entry `land`) and `base.rs` (the re-derived dispatch commit, minted in a throwaway detached worktree through `dispatch::trim_to_context` and `commit-tree -p <target>`). The replay is **shared with the compaction landing**, not a second copy: `src/prompt/rebase_forward.rs` is the one rebase-forward move, and `compactor::land` and `retarget::land` differ only in the base they mint and the ref a decline marks (the compactor's own tip; the agent's own). `role::founding_sha` gained the root spelling (`step 001: dispatch [<id>]`) alongside the child's, so one derivation founds every branch. The boundary consumption is one line in `dispatch::advance::run`, placed *before* any config resolution so the step it precedes is the first the target governs; an unmarked branch — every branch at every boundary bar one — costs a single ref read. This deletes the v0.3 frozen-copy bootstrap (`cp -r` from a data-root `agents/<profile>/` pool at repo creation): the copy existed to freeze, the fork *is* the freeze, and the profile pool dissolves into config branches — versioned, diffable, and forkable where the pool was none of those. (Endpoints and auth remain brazen's alone (§4.4); the config's `providers.yaml` carries the role → (provider row name, model id) pointers — the whole model binding (§4.2) — and the global `models.yaml` under the config root holds only the optional `adapter:` override.)

**Control is read from the config commit; worktrees hold only context.** The harness reads an agent's control files — `workflow.yaml`, `manifest.yaml`, `providers.yaml`, `souls/`, `version` — from its **followed config commit** (bl-403b): the current tip of its governing lineage — derived from the **governing config commit**, the nearest ancestor of the agent's branch reachable from any `config/*` ref, followed to the one config head standing over it. Derived from ancestry plus the refs, never stored (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth); an agent started by fork-back-in (§2.3) inherits its source's config the same way. The refs that derivation runs against are the branch's **governing lineage**: every `config/*` ref whose history reaches the branch (each contributing one merge-base candidate; a lineage sharing no ancestor contributes none, and the governing commit is the nearest candidate). The lineage is what makes the governing commit a *query* rather than a stored field — and therefore what an archive must carry, refs and all, for a replayed workspace to resolve control at all (§9.2). Reading control from a config commit rather than from the live worktree means an agent cannot edit its own policy — tool side effects land in the worktree, which control resolution never consults, and a config branch advances only by the user's `litany config` (§2.3). What a replayed workspace resolves is the archived lineage's tip (§9.2 — the archive carries the lineage heads as of archiving), and per-step policy provenance is the step record's business (bl-e4a0), not the branch's. The dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2) removes these harness-facing files from the agent's tree once `soul.md` is specialized — **not** because leaving them would compose them into the prompt (no role's manifest entry names a control path, so they would compose nowhere, §5.1), but because a tree copy of a config fact is a *second home* for that fact: it drifts from the governing commit the moment the branch advances, and it is readable by the agent's own `bash`, which is exactly how an agent comes to believe things about its own policy that its wire request contradicts (§3.3 *The fork derives the branch's descriptors from the config commit*). Single source of truth, enforced by removal rather than by trusting nobody looks. `descriptions/**` stays in the tree because it *is* context (§3.3); `steps/` and `inbox/` sit at the workspace root, outside every worktree, namespaced by agent id — physically outside context assembly (§2.3, §2.11).

**Step records are not context.** A **step record** is the per-step on-disk directory at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/`: `meta.json`, `request.json`, `response.json`, `stderr.log`, and any `tools/<tool-id>/` subdirectories the step emitted (full layout in §2.3). The whole `steps/` tree at the workspace root holds these diagnostic / audit artifacts — including the per-agent `steps/<agent-id>/driver.log`, the detached drivers' stderr (§2.11). It sits outside every worktree by construction, which means context assembly (§3.5, §5) is *physically incapable* of including it. The structural placement enforces the rule the worktree-as-context invariant (§5.1) implies: an agent's context — running, retried, or replayed — is assembled from the branch's read-state commit and nothing else. The model-facing history itself is committed worktree data, the **transcript** (§2.1, §2.3); no runtime code path reads step-record *content* at all. `response.json`'s event *framing* — the terminal `end`, `Finish`/`Error` kind, `Usage` — is read for classification and metering (§2.3 Diagnostic-only contract); everything else in this tree is write-only diagnostic.

**Sibling worktrees, not nested.** Agent worktrees are named by their full hyphenated descent (`<a>-<b>-<c>-…`, §2.3) and live as *siblings* under `agents/`, never as subdirectories of their parent's worktree. Git does not permit nested working trees; the flat layout is how the primitive's uniformity survives contact with git mechanics.

**Branch-scoped vs inherited.** `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name` (§2.3), `summary/`, and `messages/` (the transcript, §2.3) are written per-branch, and they never enter any other branch's tree — structurally, in both directions: child-to-parent, no merge-back exists (§2.6) and the work-product transfer filter excludes them (§2.6); parent-to-child, the child's dispatch commit prunes the fork point's dialog (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance* — the compactor, whose subject it is, and a fork-back-in root, whose own conversation it is, are the two principled keepers). `descriptions/` is committed in the config (§3.3) and inherited by every branch via git. `skills/` is branch-scoped (added as skills are loaded; prunable by the compactor). `steps/` and `inbox/` are *not* branch-scoped — they live at the workspace root, shared across the whole tree and namespaced by agent id, so nothing about them ever needs to cross between branches (§2.3).

**Two writers, disjoint by namespace.** The user advances config branches; each agent's executor (§2.11) advances that agent's branch — the branch advancement invariant (§2.3). The user's interactive surface is the UI, which reads the filesystem and issues `litany` verbs (§3.5); agent worktrees are read-only to the user under normal operation. Workspace repositories are never pushed to a remote (§1.1).

### 2.3 Branches and the branch invariants

A **branch** is the git container for a single agent (§2.1). Two invariants govern every ref in the workspace:

> **Every dispatch creates a branch.** Starting an agent *is* creating a branch plus a linked worktree off a ref — there is no other way an agent comes to exist, whether the dispatch is a user message or a child-targeting tool call (§2.5).

> **Branch advancement.** A config branch advances only by user config edits; an agent branch advances only by its executor (§2.11). No branch has a second writer, and nothing merges into either kind — the compaction landing, the one operation that used to merge, is a rebase-forward the receiving agent's own executor lands (§2.6).

The second invariant replaces v0.3's "nothing writes to `main` directly": there is no `main` (§2.2), so trunk protection generalizes into a per-lineage single-writer rule that covers every branch equally.

**Ref namespace.** Config branches live at `config/<name>` (descriptive names, §2.2); agent branches at `agents/<agent-id>`, where the **agent id** is the full hyphenated descent from its root agent (`<a>`, `<a>-<b>`, `<a>-<b>-<c>`, …) — hierarchy encoded in the name, not the filesystem. The prefix is the kind: which advancement rule a ref lives under is derived from its path (`git branch --list 'config/*'` / `'agents/*'`), never recorded anywhere else. Root ids are unique per workspace; an agent's id doubles as its address (§2.11), its worktree directory name (§2.2), and its `steps/` / `inbox/` namespace.

**The name is a committed fact, and the ref namespace stays the only registry.** Every dispatch settles a **name** (§2.1) — supplied via `litany prompt --name` / `litany dispatch --name` and the `dispatch` built-in's exposed `name` input (§2.5), or **minted on omission** (yog bl-aca4, below): a settle-the-name pre-flight at each creation path validates a supplied name against the living agents, and mints an absent one from the embedded wordlist against the *same* living-names scan — one occupied-set derivation, so no fork ends nameless. The name's one home is a `name` file committed on that agent's own dispatch commit, beside `goal.md` (step 2 below). Everything else derives: reading a name is `git show agents/<id>:name`, so the `agents/*` refs remain the workspace's only registry (above) and no root-level index exists to drift (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth); worktree teardown (step 6 below) cannot lose it, because it is a commit; and retention — ref deletion (§9.2) — recycles the name with **no cleanup path at all**: the ref goes, the blob goes, the name is free.

Three rules make the fact total:

- **Every dispatch commit writes the file; empty content means unnamed.** Writing it only for a named agent would make absence a second shape that has to be *established* rather than left alone — a child forks off its parent's tip and inherits the parent's blob, so the commit would have to delete it, and that deletion would then ride the §2.6 work-product transfer back into the parent and unname it. One always-written file dissolves all three cases: like `goal.md` and `soul.md` the name is overwritten in place at the dispatch commit and frozen thereafter (*Goal and soul are pinned files*, below), so it is always a rewrite and never a deletion. The transfer excludes it as branch-scoped context, exactly like `goal.md` (§2.6).
- **Uniqueness is enforced where the fact lives.** Creation refuses a name a living agent already wears — a scan of the `agents/*` refs, which is the same query resolution runs. The refusal is a pre-flight beside the §4.3 role check and the §6 budget gate, so it leaves no branch, no worktree and no inbox behind.
- **Name space and id space are disjoint by construction.** Every id begins with a compact `YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSZ` timestamp, so a name beginning with one is refused at creation, as is a name that is not one whitespace-free path component. Addressing (§2.11) therefore never *guesses* which reading of a needle was meant; the only ambiguity left to refuse is one name worn twice, which creation-time uniqueness cannot see (a fork-back-in off a named commit re-wears it).

- **Omission mints; the mint lives here (yog bl-aca4).** `name` is an exposed parameter on every creation surface, and an omitted one is minted: **two words from the embedded wordlist (`src/workspace/agent_name/mint/words.txt`) joined in PascalCase** — `PeachHollow` — drawn as a pure function over an injected RNG and the living-names occupied set. One RNG draw picks a start index, a wraparound scan discards occupied names, and one full lap proves exhaustion, which errors loudly rather than looping. **Two words, because one does not read as a name** (bl-79a2, operator ruling 2026-08-16): a lone common noun in a conversation row, a `litany list` line or an id's hyphenated descent reads as a word that happens to be there, where the PascalCase pair carries the naming intent in its own shape. The join has no separator, so a minted name stays one unbroken token and one path component, and — carrying no hyphen — can never be misread as two segments of the descent `<a>-<b>-…`. Mechanically this is the **index space widened, not a second draw**: an index into `0..n * (n - 1)` names an ordered pair of *distinct* words (no `PeachPeach`), so the scan, the purity, the one-draw-per-mint property and the exact exhaustion bound are unchanged and no second wordlist exists to drift. The consequence is stated rather than patched: the walk is **not uniform over pairs** — a collision steps to the next second word, not to a fresh random pair — exactly as the one-word mint's walk was not uniform over words. Nothing asks the mint for randomness guarantees; uniqueness is the occupied-set check's, not the generator's. **The pool is approved data, not merely shaped data** (bl-b59c): it is authored for this repository under the crate's own licence — no third-party corpus, so no second licence rides into an MIT package — and every word is concrete, neutral, everyday English, because an agent wears its name in front of an operator and the mint may not put a slur in their mouth. It is sized for review rather than for entropy: a few hundred words, small enough that a human reads the whole list, since the occupied set it must out-size is one workspace's living agents and a collision costs a scan step, not a failure. The approved set is pinned by count *and* digest in the mint's tests, so adding a word is an explicit review event. The mint sits beside the uniqueness check it races (`workspace::agent_name`), because every creation path — the `dispatch` tool, `litany dispatch`, `litany prompt`, the harness-initiated procedure dispatches — must mint on omission, and none passes through yog; yog (which previews the predicted name before firing) draws the *same* function through the crate's mint seam (§3.4). Unnamed thereby stays a **readable** state (pre-mint agents, until retention ages them out) but stops being a creatable one.

Deriving the name *from* the id was considered and rejected: the consumer that mints names does so before an id exists (it previews the name, then dispatches). Making the name *be* the id was rejected on the recyclability line above.

The file is ordinary worktree data, so whether an agent *sees* its own name is an ordinary manifest question (§5.2) and not a mechanism of its own: no shipped role pins `name` today, so it composes into nothing, and surfacing it into assembled context is a tracked follow-on that adds a `pinned` entry, not a code path.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-c8ed, amended by bl-404d).** Shipped. The fact's one home is `src/workspace/agent_name.rs` — the `name` constant, the writer (`settle`), the reader (`read`, a `git show agents/<id>:name`), the creation guard (`require_available`), the addressing reading (`resolve`), and since bl-404d the mint (`agent_name::mint` — the wordlist, the `Rng` trait with its `SplitMix64` production impl, `mint`, and the settle-the-name pre-flight `preflight`) all in one module, so the read, write and mint halves cannot drift. `preflight` runs at both creation pre-flights (`prompt::run` for roots, `child_dispatch::run` for every child — the one fork in the system); `settle` is called from `dispatch::step_commit::trim_to_context` — the single function staging the dispatch commit's trim, reached by both the root's `commit_dispatch` and every child's `subagent::spawn_subagent_branch` — so no fork path can skip it, exactly as with the control-file removal, the descriptor derivation and the unsettled-tool-step prune. `--name` rides `cmd::prompt::Args` and `cmd::dispatch::Args`; the `dispatch` built-in forwards its optional `name` input to `litany dispatch --name`; `cmd::message::run` resolves the recipient before anything downstream sees it, so the deposit, the probe and the §2.10 failed-branch advisory all address one id. The harness-initiated dispatches (compactor, verifier, the verifier's reject re-dispatch) supply no name and so wear minted ones, like any other omission — the one general path, no procedure-child special case.

**Any ref is a legal fork point.** An agent's substrate is a branch plus a worktree **off a ref** (§2.1). Every start is the same operation with a different argument:

- *Fresh start* — fork off a config branch's head (the default). The agent begins with the config's tree and an empty transcript.
- *Child dispatch* — fork off the commit where the dispatch landed on the parent's branch (§2.5). The child inherits the parent's work products in its tree; the parent's dialog is pruned at the dispatch commit (*Fork and inheritance*, below).
- *Fork-back-in* — fork off any historical commit of any agent: counterfactual exploration, continuing from a stopped tip (§2.9), re-running with a different goal. Provenance is the ancestry; no special branch prefix marks a fork (§7.2).
- *Parallel fan* — N forks off one ref are N sibling agents (§2.5).

An agent's governing lineage rides its ancestry, and same-lineage config edits reach it by resolution alone (§2.2 *Fork chooses the lineage*). Moving a running agent onto a *different* lineage is therefore never a graft onto it: it is `litany retarget` (§2.2, §3.4), which re-forks the branch off the target config commit and replays its own history on top — a fifth start in the same primitive rather than a mechanism beside it. The invariant above is untouched, and worth restating because the landing rewrites history: *an agent branch advances only by its executor*. The user writes a **ref mark**, never the branch; the agent's own executor reads that mark at its next step boundary and performs the re-fork, exactly as it performs the compaction landing (§2.6). No branch gains a second writer.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-a693).** All four starts above are reachable from the command surface (§3.4), and they are one argument, not four code paths. `litany prompt <ws> <message>` takes `--config <name>` (fork off `config/<name>`'s head — how a lineage authored by `litany config --from`/`--orphan` becomes startable at all) or `--from <ref>` (fork off any ref: a historical commit of any agent, a stopped tip, a config commit, §7.2); naming neither is `--config default`, the general path with empty inputs, and naming both is declined. `litany dispatch <role> …` takes the same `--from <ref>`, which reaches the fork-point field the §6 verifier gate already used — the child's id, and so its return address, is unchanged (§2.6), while its **governing config commit follows the fork point** as this section's §2.2 rule requires ("an agent started by fork-back-in inherits its source's config the same way"): soul, grant, descriptors, budgets and the role-validity pre-flight are read from the commit the child's own later hops will derive from its branch, so dispatch-time artifacts and step-time resolution cannot answer to two configs. Before this ball the sole fork-point caller was the verifier gate, whose fork point always descends from the dispatcher's own config, so the two readings coincided; `--from` makes them distinguishable, and the derivation — not the dispatcher — decides. One consequence worth naming: a §6 verifier gate over a worker that was started `--from` a *foreign* lineage reads the verifier soul from that foreign config, and fails loudly if the lineage does not define the role — the ordinary control-read decline, not a silent skip (only budget refusals are non-events, §6). That is the same rule applied consistently, not a special case: a gate judges the agent's own config. `prompt`'s two spellings collapse to one ref in one place (`src/prompt/fork_point.rs`); `dispatch` has the single spelling and reaches the same existence guard directly. Both guards are the shared ones (`workspace::require_ref` for a ref, `workspace::require_lineage` for a lineage name — the latter now also `litany config --from`'s decline), so an unresolvable fork point is declined before any branch, worktree or inbox exists. **Fork settles the lineage** (this note predates bl-403b, which replaced the freeze reading with follow-the-tip): `workspace::governing_config` takes a *revision* rather than an agent id, so the fresh root asks the same ancestry derivation of the ref it is about to fork off that an existing agent asks of its own ref (a config head answers itself) — grants and descriptors derive from a config commit of that lineage (its followed tip, since bl-403b), never from the fork point's tree (§3.3, §5.1).

Within a branch, **steps land as linear commits**: when a step's model call completes, the executor commits its assistant output into the transcript (below); each tool call the step emitted commits its result — and any worktree side effects — as it lands (§3.3). Steps are not their own branches. New branches appear only at dispatch boundaries.

> **Historical.** v0.1 committed exchanges directly on `main` without branching; v0.2 introduced `ex/*`/`inv/*` branch prefixes; v0.3 unified both under one primitive, with root history merging to `main` and children merging back to their parents. The workspace substrate (this rewrite) deletes `main` and merge-back together: root agents live on their own branches indefinitely (§2.4), children return results as messages (§2.6), and the only merge left was compaction — itself since replaced by the rebase-forward landing (bl-bc9c, §2.6), which merges nothing.

**Branch lifecycle** (identical for root and child agents, up to step 5's addressee):

1. **Fork.** The dispatch creates `agents/<agent-id>` off the chosen ref and materializes a linked worktree at `<workspace>/agents/<agent-id>/`.
2. **Dispatch commit.** The harness writes `goal.md` and `soul.md` (the chosen role's soul, read from the governing config commit's `souls/<role>.md`, §2.2) and trims the tree to this agent's context: the agent's own `name` is settled over whatever the fork point carried (*The name is a committed fact*, above), the harness-facing control files leave (§2.2 — a no-op when the fork point was not a config commit), the `descriptions/**` descriptors are derived from the same governing config commit, filtered to the chosen role's `tools:` grant — never inherited from the fork point's own already-filtered tree (§3.3 *The fork derives the branch's descriptors from the config commit*, §5.1) — and the inherited **unsettled tool step** leaves: a trailing model-output entry carrying a `tool_use` no committed `tool_result` answers, together with the partial results that would orphan when it goes (§2.5 *The fork lands inside the parent's tool step*). All four are the same act, and each is a no-op for a fork point that carried nothing to change. This is the first commit on the new branch. Goal, soul and name are overwritten in place here and frozen thereafter (§2.3 *Goal and soul are pinned files*).
3. **Work.** The agent runs its step loop. At each step boundary the executor drains the agent's inbox — pending messages land as delivery commits before the model call (§2.11). Assistant output and tool results commit as transcript entries as they land; worktree-modifying tool calls carry their modifications in the same serialized commit discipline (§3.3). Each step's diagnostic record lands at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/NNN/`, outside the worktree. A step that emits a dispatch tool call forks a child branch off the branch tip.
4. **Completion.** A terminal event: final response, stop (§2.9), budget exhaustion (§6).
5. **Return.** Every terminal event deposits — this step is total. The agent deposits its **result message** (§2.6), carrying its epitaph (the pinned manner of ending), its terminal ref, and — iff it spoke — its terminal response. **Whose inbox it lands in is the epitaph's decision** (§2.6 *A reply answers the last prompter; an obituary reports to the dispatcher*): a `final-response` **reply** answers whoever last prompted this agent — for the dispatch step that is the dispatcher, which is why an ordinary child's return reaches its parent — while a `stopped` / `budget-exhausted` / `died` **obituary** is addressed to the dispatcher whoever prompted last, because it states a fact about the tree rather than answering anyone. A reply whose last prompter is the *user* addresses no inbox at all: the answer is read in this agent's own conversation (§2.4, §7.1), which is also why a root agent — with no dispatcher and, ordinarily, a user as its last prompter — deposits nothing and its non-response terminations surface in the exchange (§2.4). Delivery applies the work-product transfer only in the **dispatcher's** inbox (§2.6). Final response, stop (§2.9), and budget exhaustion (§6) each deposit through the agent's own executor with the matching epitaph; an agent that crashed too hard to run its handler (SIGKILL, OOM, panic) has a `died`-epitaph deposit made on its behalf by the §8 sweep. Nothing merges anywhere; the branch simply stops advancing. The return is the opening act of the exit protocol (§2.11): after depositing, the executor releases its lock and — for a final response, never for stopped or budget-exhausted — fires the self-directed launch and the launch that revives *the recipient it just deposited into*, before exiting.
6. **Quiescence, not teardown.** The branch persists as a ref. A quiescent agent's worktree may be torn down and is rematerialized by the next driver (`git worktree add` off the ref) — the worktree is disposable materialization, never state; the inbox directory and the lock it carries (§2.11) persist at the workspace root regardless. A later deposit revives the agent: the deposit starts a driver, and the step loop resumes on the same branch (§2.4, §2.11). Retention and GC are branch deletion plus object pruning (§9.2).

Branching is cheap — local git operations on disk — but it is not per-step. A long-running agent produces many commits on one branch and only forks children when it actually dispatches.

**Executor exclusivity is two existing rules.** Git refuses to check one branch out into two worktrees, so a branch has at most one materialization; the executor lock (§2.11) admits at most one driver per agent, so a materialized worktree has at most one process stepping it. The residual case the git rule cannot see — two processes re-entering one *existing* worktree — is exactly the lock's job. Jointly the two rules are total, and nothing new is needed.

Compaction may run *during* a branch's execution, not only near termination; see §2.6 (the compaction landing) and §2.7.

**The transcript: context has one home.** Everything that enters an agent's context is a committed worktree file. The **transcript** (§2.1) is the branch-scoped sequence under `messages/` that carries the model-facing history itself: each delivered message (§2.11), each step's assistant output, and each tool call's result is one immutable **transcript entry**, committed by the executor as it lands. Nothing context-bearing sits in process memory: the executor holds no message history across steps, and a model call's context — running, retried, or replayed — is assembled the same way, from the read-state commit's tree (§2.10, §5). This closes the gap the diagnostic-only contract (below) used to leave open: assistant text and thinking blocks previously had no readable home outside the diagnostic `response.json`, so a crashed chain could not rebuild its history without either violating the contract or having held that history in RAM — and both arms fail `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Disk-first. Now the history has exactly one home, and it is a commit.

> **Shipped-state note.** The transcript is the shipped harness's sole assembly source (bl-4798 wrote it; bl-26cb re-pointed assembly at it). A settled model call seals its staging entry and commits `messages/NNN-<model-id>.json` (the origin token is the authoring model, below); each resolved tool commits `messages/NNN-tool.json`; each step re-assembles its whole model-facing history by `readdir`+sort of `messages/` in the read-state commit's tree (below, *Origins and wire framing*), with **zero runtime content reads under `steps/`** — the pre-transcript in-memory accumulator and the `tools/<tool-id>/output.json` read path are deleted (the per-tool-call `output.json` is still *written* as the diagnostic raw capture, never read). The on-ramp is now the real one: the initial user message is deposited into the agent's own inbox by the root executor and delivered as `messages/NNN-user.md` by the step-1 inbox drain (bl-cb44, bl-1129, §2.11), the same front door every later message uses — the earlier direct-commit stand-in is retired. The writer's mechanics are specified below (*The transcript writer*).

*Sequence and immutability.* Transcript entries are named `messages/NNN-<origin>.<ext>`; **order lives in the filename**. `NNN` is one zero-padded per-branch counter shared across every origin, assigned at commit time by the executor — the branch's single writer (§2.11 executor lock) — and derived, never stored: the next number is max-present-plus-one from a directory listing. The shared counter is race-free by the same token: every transcript commit, whatever its origin — a delivery commit (§2.11), a step's assistant entry, a tool entry (§3.3, sibling commits serialized) — lands inside the executor lock's critical section, and max-present-plus-one is evaluated there; no writer ever assigns a number outside the lock, so the counter needs no mechanism of its own. Assembly is readdir + sort — no git-log walk, no index, no sidecar order file (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth: order has one home, the name). Entries are immutable: change is append or delete, never edit-in-place. Deletion is the ordinary curation primitive (§5.4), and a deleted entry stays recoverable from git history until compaction squashes it.

*Origins and wire framing.* **Every transcript entry's origin token names its author** — the sender for a delivered `.md` message, the **model id** for model output, `tool` for a tool result. The token is provenance; role framing is a separate axis, derived from the path — the extension and the reserved-token test — not from frontmatter and not by flattening history into one assembled user block:

- `messages/NNN-<sender>.md` — a delivered message (§2.11); composes as user-role content, path as provenance hint (§5.3).
- `messages/NNN-<model-id>.json` — one step's model output: the origin token is the id of the model that produced the entry, as it rode the canonical request (§4.3 role → model mapping). The body is an **API-shaped message object** — the canonical content blocks (text, thinking, `tool_use`) under `content`, exactly as the model call's authoritative content emitted them (§4.4 segment authority), with the provider's token `usage` as their sibling (*Usage rides the entry*, below) — streamed by the executor from the adapter pass it is already consuming — one pass, two sinks, no read-back and no in-RAM history (*The transcript writer*, below) — and committed when the model call settles complete; a retried or paused model call commits only the authoritative content (§2.10, §4.4). Composes verbatim as an assistant-role wire message — the model id is the entry's provenance, the assistant role is its wire shape (any `.json` token but the reserved `tool` composes assistant-side).
- `messages/NNN-tool.json` — one tool call's canonical `tool_result` block (content, `is_error`, and the `tool_use_id` pairing it to its emitting step), committed by the tool executor when the tool resolves (§3.3). Composes as `tool_result` blocks in the following **tool-role** wire message — brazen's canonical `Role::Tool` (§4.4), which each provider protocol projects into its own dialect (the anthropic projection folds it into a `"user"` message; the openai/ollama projections fan it out to `role:"tool"` messages). The dialect fold is the protocol layer's business, never the assembler's: emitting user-role here worked only on the anthropic dialect and was rejected by every other (bl-2e28, the first non-anthropic live wire).

The assembler walks the sequence and groups consecutive same-side entries into alternating wire messages; because tool results commit immediately after their emitting step's assistant entry — the boundary drain (§2.11) is ordered *after* the step's tool entries, so a delivered message can never wedge between a `tool_use` and its `tool_result` — the provider pairing rule — every `tool_use` matched by a `tool_result` in the immediately following wire message (§2.5) — holds by construction. Path-derived framing is forced, not chosen: the wire itself requires role structure (a single flattened user block cannot carry `tool_use`/`tool_result` pairing, and replayed thinking blocks must re-enter under their original role with their original signatures for the request to be bit-identical), and the role's single home is the filename — frontmatter would be a second copy of a fact the path already states. The `.json` entries carry brazen's canonical vocabulary verbatim (§4.4), which is what makes replay bit-for-bit rather than a lossy re-rendering. **Two entry shapes are lawful, and every reader accepts both:** the API-shaped object above (`{"content":[…]}`, plus a `usage` sibling on model output) and a **bare array of the same blocks** — the shape a tool entry carries, and the shape every model entry carried before usage rode along. So the shape has exactly one question and one answer, *where do the blocks live* (`src/prompt/dispatch/entry.rs`, the shape's one home), and an absent `usage` is the general path with empty inputs, never an error (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`) — a pre-usage transcript replays unchanged, with no migration and no version field. **`tool` is the one reserved `.json` origin token** — it, and it alone, marks an entry as a tool result rather than model output; every other `.json` token is a model id. In `.md` sender space `user` stays reserved (§2.11). A model id colliding with a reserved token — a model literally named `tool` — is **declined, not munged** (decline illegal operations): the token is a fact, not a slot to sanitize, and a second spelling would defeat the self-describing transcript. (The provider row is deliberately excluded from the token: routing is brazen's config and the diagnostic `request.json`, and free-form row names would force the filename sanitization this rule refuses.)

*Self-output commits directly; the inbox is the on-ramp for everyone else.* The inbox (§2.11) exists because external senders cannot commit into a branch they do not drive — the queue plus the step-boundary drain is what serializes many writers onto the single executor. The executor needs no such ramp for its own step output: it already holds the lock and already owns the counter, so it delivery-commits assistant output and tool results straight into the same sequence namespace. Routing self-output through its own inbox would add a hop that its own next drain immediately undoes — mechanism without a difference. A *note to self* is a different thing and stays legal: that is a message (content addressed to an agent, §2.1), deposited through the front door like any other (§2.11 "Self-messages"); step output is not addressed to anyone — it is the history itself.

*The transcript writer.* Both sinks are streams off the one adapter pass; nothing is buffered in RAM beyond the block in progress, and nothing is ever read back. Every event appends to the diagnostic `response.json` as it arrives (§4.4, fd held open across attempts). Content and usage additionally stream into the transcript entry *under construction*: a **staging file** at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/staging.json`, appended block-by-block as each content block completes — a block is the smallest unit that exists before it is whole, so the in-progress block's deltas are the only buffering anywhere. Segment authority (§4.4) drives the staging file mechanically: an `Error`-terminated segment truncates it (the discarded attempt's audit home is `response.json`); a `Pause`-terminated segment leaves it accumulating — the pause continuation (§2.10) re-sends the staging file's blocks, the writer reading its own sink, not a diagnostic record; the final `Finish` seals it — closing the `content` array and writing the sealing segment's usage report (*Usage rides the entry*, below) — and the executor renames it into the worktree as `messages/NNN-<model-id>.json` (`NNN` assigned at commit time, above; the origin token is the model that authored the entry) and commits. A model call that never settles complete — retries exhausted, a non-retryable error, a stop — commits nothing: the transcript gains no entry, the branch tip does not move (§2.10), and the stale staging file is debris the step's re-run overwrites (*Crash and recovery*, below). The staging file is the one path under `steps/` that is not a record — it is the entry being authored, homed outside the worktree until it is authoritative, and it *leaves* by rename at settle; the diagnostic-only contract (below) is untouched, because no content is ever read back out of `response.json` or any other step record.

*Usage rides the entry.* **Usage** is brazen's token accounting for one model call (`Usage`, brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3.2): the `input_tokens` / `output_tokens` / `cache_read_tokens` / `cache_write_tokens` counters a provider reports, each optional — an unreported counter is *absent*, never a fabricated `0`. The writer commits it with the output it belongs to, as the entry's `usage` sibling (above), so **a transcript reader states real token counts from the committed bytes alone** — no `steps/` read, no second artifact, no reconstruction. Three rules keep it a fact rather than a derivation:

- **Recorded, never computed.** litany adds nothing, scales nothing, estimates nothing. A provider may state one report in installments — Anthropic's `message_start` carries the input side, its terminal `message_delta` the output side — so the writer records each counter *as the provider last reported it* and omits every counter never reported; the sealed object is what the same call would have returned unstreamed. The fold is over the reported counter names, not a fixed field list, so a counter brazen adds under `v=1` rides through with no code change.
- **Segment authority governs usage exactly as it governs blocks (§4.4).** A discarded `Error` segment's counters are discarded with its blocks — the committed output never carries a superseded attempt's cost — and the report the entry seals is the sealing segment's.
- **Spend metering keeps its own home, because it is a different fact.** §6 budgets and §8 metrics bill *every* attempt segment of `response.json`, discarded ones included, since a failed attempt is billed by the provider. The entry's `usage` answers a narrower question — what this committed output itself cost — so the two never drift into two spellings of one number, and neither is derived from the other. (With no v1 call able to pause, the entry's report and the last segment's are the same bytes.)

*Goal and soul are pinned files, not sequence item zero.* The pinned head — `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, `descriptions/**`, the manifest's `pinned` paths (§5.2) — stays outside the sequence. The **system slot** is the wire home of the first three, composed in that order: the goal block, then the identity line the `name` derives (§2.8), then the role's soul. The two regimes are opposites on every axis: the pinned head is overwritten in place at the dispatch commit (step 2 above) and frozen thereafter — exactly the edit-in-place the sequence bans; the soul composes into the system slot, which is not a wire message at all; and the goal's contract is position-independence (§2.8, pinned at the head of context regardless of sequence position) — the antithesis of a sequence coordinate. Making them "item zero" would trade a clean two-part rule (frozen head + append-only tail, §5.5) for a sequence with one mutable, role-anomalous member.

*Fork and inheritance.* A branch forks with its fork point's whole tree — work products, descriptors, and the dialog (`messages/**`, `summary/**`, `skills/**`) — and its whole commit history. What the dispatch commit then does with the inherited *dialog* turns on whose conversation it is (§2.2 branch-scoped). A **child's** dispatch commit deletes it: assembly is a pure function of the tree (§5.1) and the transcript composes unconditionally (§5.2), so a retained parental dialog would open the child's first model call with instructions addressed to the *parent* — the reproduced runaway (bl-5a36, yog bl-d023): a user's "spawn a subagent …" arrived in each child as an apparently unanswered user message outranking its own deposited goal, and every generation re-dispatched until the operator stopped the tree. A child's opening context is its pinned goal, soul and pins plus what is deposited to it. Two forks keep the dialog on principle: the **compactor** (§2.7), whose subject it is — this is what gives it its view of the dispatching branch's history with no special channel — and a **fork-back-in root** (§7.2), which is re-entering that very conversation; both then append from the inherited maximum (the counter is derived from the surviving listing). Within a retained dialog, the fork still does not keep the parent's unsettled tool step (§2.5): a tool-call dispatch forks from inside its own tool step, so the tail can carry a `tool_use` that will settle on the parent's branch and never on the child's, and the dispatch commit deletes it (step 2 above). All of these are deletions, the sanctioned transcript change — the branch's own record, honest at fork time — not exceptions to inheritance and not filters at read time.

*Return, not merge-back.* Nothing a child appends ever enters its parent's tree, because nothing merges back (§2.6). The child's transcript, summaries, goal, and soul stay on its branch — provenance until GC (§9.2) — and its distilled result travels as the result message plus the work-product transfer (§2.6). Parent and child transcripts are separate lineages sharing a prefix; they cannot collide because they never meet.
