*Crash and recovery.* An entry that never committed never happened: a chain that dies mid-model-call re-enters (§6), finds the read state unchanged, and re-runs the step — the identical shape as a retry (§2.10), with nothing in memory to lose. Running assembly and replay assembly are one code path against one input; "replay" stops being a mode.

**Step on-disk layout.** Each step lives in its own directory at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/` — at the workspace root, *outside every worktree* (§2.2). `<NNN>` is zero-padded 3-digit and 1-indexed, so step dirs sort lexically. `<agent-id>` is the owning agent's id — namespacing this way is what lets every agent in the tree write into a single shared `steps/` tree without filename collision, and why a child's step records never need to cross anywhere: they were never below a worktree to begin with.

Per-step files:

- `meta.json` — `{commit, started_at, ended_at, …}`. The `commit` field is the sha of the branch tip at step-start; it is the **read state** for the step's model call. Replay reproduces the wire input by re-running the context assembler (§5) against this commit's tree — `request.json` is not the source of truth. Step 1's `commit` is the dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2). Step ≥2's `commit` is the prior step's tip, advanced by the commits between them — the prior step's transcript entries (assistant output, tool results), any worktree side effects, and any delivery commits from the boundary drain (§2.11); the harness writes no separate pre-call commit for step ≥2 (§2.10).
- `request.json` — diagnostic snapshot of the wire request the model saw. Written for audit and human inspection only (see Diagnostic-only contract below).
- `response.json` — JSONL of canonical events (one event per line), appended across one or more attempt segments, each segment terminated by its `{"type":"end"}` line; the wire-side streaming/non-streaming distinction collapses inside the adapter before bytes reach disk. See §4.4 for the segment rules and §3.5 for the live-streaming completion signal.
- `stderr.log` — the adapter subprocess's stderr, appended once per attempt across the model call. **Empty on an ordinary run**: brazen speaks every failure in-band on stdout (§4.4), so bytes here mean the adapter failed *outside* that contract — a startup failure (a malformed brazen config, an unreadable credstore) that produced no events at all. That is the one case the on-disk record cannot otherwise distinguish from a mid-stream kill (§2.9): both leave a `response.json` with no terminal `end`. The capture is what tells an operator which one happened, so the half-stream error quotes its tail and names this file (§4.4).
- `tools/<tool-id>/` — per-tool-call records (`input.json`, `output.json`); `<tool-id>` is the `tool_use.id` from the wire (e.g. `toolu_01abc…`). Full contract in §3.3.

One file sits a level up, at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/driver.log`, because it belongs to the agent's *drivers* rather than to any one step: the stderr of every detached driver launched for this agent, appended across launches and across the §6 exec baton (§2.11 *A detached driver's stderr is captured, not discarded*). It is diagnostic under the same contract below, and being non-numeric it is skipped by the derivations that read this directory's entries as step sequence numbers.

**Diagnostic-only contract (request.json, response.json, stderr.log, driver.log, tools/).** Everything under `steps/` is diagnostic; there are **zero runtime content reads** from this tree. The rule is framing-yes / content-no, with no exemptions:

- **request.json: never read at runtime.** Replay (§3.1, §2.10) re-runs the context assembler against `meta.json`'s `commit` tree and re-invokes the adapter; it does not read `request.json`. The file exists for human inspection only.
- **response.json content: never read, by anyone, for anything.** The model output — text, thinking, and tool-call arguments — a step produced has its readable home in the transcript (`messages/NNN-<model-id>.json`, above): the executor writes the adapter stream to both sinks in one pass and never reads it back. Context during execution and context at replay are the same assembly from the read-state commit; no code site reconstructs anything by re-reading a prior step's `response.json`.
- **response.json framing: sanctioned reads.** Classification (§3.5), the harness retry loop (§2.10), and metering (§6, §8) read only the framing tail — the terminal `end`, the last segment's `Finish`/`Error` kind, and `Usage` events — never the content blocks. Workflow advance (§1 #4, §6, "No resident interpreter") stays scoped to workflow-event boundaries: it never picks up mid-history by reading response content.
- **stderr.log: never read at runtime.** The tail quoted in a half-stream error (§4.4) comes from the capture the runner already holds in the same pass that writes the file — the write and the quote are one stream to two sinks, the shape the transcript writer uses (above). No code site opens the file back up.
- **driver.log: never read at runtime.** The launcher opens it, hands the fd to the child, and forgets it; the driver writes through inherited stderr and opens nothing. Nothing in the harness reads it back, and no decision anywhere turns on its contents — it is for the operator, whose alternative was `/dev/null`.
- **tools/<tool-id>/ (input.json, output.json): never read at runtime.** A tool result's runtime home is its transcript entry (`messages/NNN-tool.json`, committed when the tool resolves, §3.3); the emitting `tool_use` block's home is the step's model-output entry. The per-tool-call records remain as the raw capture — full stdout/stderr/exit code with timestamps, the streams still apart and unrendered — for audit and human inspection. (Historical: through v0.7 `output.json` was the runtime source for `tool_result` assembly and `input.json` the replay source for tool framing; the transcript supersedes both reads.)
- **The frontend (§3.5)** may read all of these files in full for user inspection; that is a read-only consumer, not harness state.

This is structural, not advisory. The placement of these files at `<workspace>/steps/`, outside every worktree, makes context assembly (§3.5, §5) physically incapable of including them as model context; the worktree-as-context invariant (§5.1) is untouched, because a framing read (a terminal line, an error kind, a token count) never flows into a prompt. Harness implementations honor the split directly: no code site reads step-record content at runtime — only `response.json`'s framing, and only for classification and metering.

Step records are not committed to git. The workspace repository is bare (§2.2), and `steps/` sits beside it — outside every worktree, untracked by git. Their durability is filesystem durability (atomic write via temp + rename, fsync as needed); their authority for replay is the `commit` sha each `meta.json` records, which *is* a real git commit.

**Silent death is derived.** Git's ref database plus the executor lock is the tracking. An agent branch with no live executor (the §2.11 lock probe) whose latest step's model call **never settled complete** (*The transcript writer*, above) died mid-work — two on-disk shapes say so, and both are dead: `response.json` closed without a terminal `end` (killed or stopped mid-stream, §2.9), or its final attempt segment terminated in an `Error` (retries exhausted, or a non-retryable error, §2.10 — the segment closes with its clean `end`, so absence-of-`end` alone would misread this branch as idle; the classification reads the last segment's terminal, §4.4). Either way no transcript entry committed and the branch cannot advance without a new touch. A child with no live executor and no result deposit (§2.6) stalled without returning. Both classifications derive from refs, the lock probe, inbox listings, and step-record framing — no status field anywhere (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth). The §8 silent-death health metric reads exactly these observations.

### 2.4 Exchanges and reprompt

An **exchange** (§2.1) is a UX span, not a structure: the stretch of a root agent's linear history between a user message and the terminal response that answers it. It owns no branch, no merge, no lifecycle. Users think in exchanges, so the UI surfaces the grouping (§7.1); the architecture does not.

**Reprompt is a message.** A root agent's branch outlives its exchanges. When the user speaks again, the deposit lands in the agent's inbox, a driver starts if none is live (§2.11), the delivery commit lands, and the step loop resumes on the same branch — the same primitive as any other message from any other sender, at any other time. There is no branch-per-exchange, no re-dispatch, no state handoff: the context is already the branch's tree. This dissolves a self-contradiction in the v0.3 shape, where each user message spawned a fresh branch off `main` that "consumed the prior branch's state as context" while root branches never merged — so `main` never actually held the history the next branch was supposed to consume. Neither arm survives: there is no `main` and no branch-per-exchange to root on it.

An exchange interrupted before a terminal response is just a stopped agent (§2.9): the branch stays, and the user moves forward with the same two primitives as ever — a message to resume the same agent, or a fork off any ref for a fresh one (§2.3).

Multiple concurrent exchanges are multiple root agents (§7.3): a *new question* forks a new root agent off a config branch's head; a *course correction* messages the running one. No mechanism beyond the branch invariants and the inbox is required.

### 2.5 Dispatch

A **dispatch** (§2.1) is the event that starts an agent with a goal. Two forms:

- **User-message dispatch.** The user starts a root agent — default fork point: a config branch's head (§2.3).
- **Tool-call dispatch.** A running agent emits a tool call targeting a child. The child forks off the commit where the dispatch landed on the parent's branch, inheriting the parent's work products but never its dialog (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance*). From the child's perspective, its parent is indistinguishable from a user; the two forms are one primitive.

This symmetry is load-bearing:

- The same code path handles user-initiated and agent-initiated dispatches.
- Verifier agents, compactor agents, and adversarial critics are child agents with different goals.
- Parallel exploration (N workers on the same task) is N parallel dispatches from the same step — N sibling forks of one ref (§2.3).

The unification is not ergonomic sugar; it falls out of the substrate. Because starting work is forking a branch and results return through the one message channel (§2.6), any operation shaped as "spawn work with a goal, run, return a result" collapses to the same primitive. The harness does not ship a separate framework for compaction, verification, or auto-parsing of oversized tool output — each is a child agent with a different goal and toolset, dispatched the same way (§3.4's `litany dispatch …`). A new procedure earns its place by collapsing onto this primitive or by introducing one that is genuinely new; it does not sit in parallel with something it almost is. This is the concrete instantiation of the "One obvious path" principle in `docs/PRINCIPLES.md`.

**Every tool returns synchronously.** Provider APIs (Anthropic Messages, OpenAI Responses, Gemini) require each `tool_use` block emitted by a step to be matched by a `tool_result` block in the immediately following wire message (each dialect's own shape, projected by brazen from the canonical tool-role message, §2.3). The harness honors that invariant: a step's next model call is issued only when every tool call it emitted has produced a `tool_result`. Partial-result reprompts are not attempted — they are rejected at the wire.

**Dispatch returns the child's address.** Long-running tools — including dispatch — return immediately. A dispatch's `tool_result` is the **child's address** (its agent id, §2.11), never its result and never a handle to poll: there is nothing to await. The result arrives later, on its own channel, as a **deposit into this agent's inbox** (§2.6, §2.11) — the dispatch message is the child's last prompt until somebody else speaks to it, so the reply comes here (§2.6) — and revival-on-deposit (§2.11) wakes the parent whether it is still stepping or has gone quiescent. Parallelism is expressed by issuing several dispatches in one step; each child deposits its result message as it terminates, and the parent reacts to each at a step boundary as it lands. The per-step "one result per tool_use" shape stays intact; the asynchrony rides on the inbox, not on a handle. Dispatch is a tool like any other: this is how the symmetry between inline tool calls and child agents is preserved without inventing a second control path. The v0.4 `dispatch` built-in — `litany tool dispatch`, input `{role, goal}` plus the exposed `name`, minted on omission (§2.3) — realizes this contract: it spawns the child through `litany dispatch <role>` (§3.4) and emits `<this-agent>-<child>` — the child's agent id, which is also its branch name and its address (§2.3, §2.11).

Tool calls targeting non-dispatch tools commit their records on the emitting branch as part of the step. Dispatch tool calls fork child branches off the commit where the dispatch landed; the `tool_result` the parent step observes is the child's address. When the child terminates, its result message delivers at a step boundary like any deposit (§2.6, §2.11), or revives the parent if it had gone quiescent (§2.4, §2.11) — the parent never blocks, so there is no separate poll or wait to perform. (The merge-era `{status: conflicted}` is retired with the merge protocol; its survivor is the declined work-product transfer, marked git-natively at delivery, §2.6.)

**Dispatch is fork plus front door — never a spawn.** The dispatch primitive is writes and one deposit, with no process supervision anywhere in it. Tool dispatch does three things, inline and synchronously: (1) fork the child branch off the named ref — default: the commit where the dispatch landed on the parent's branch — and land the dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2), which pins `soul.md` plus whatever documents the dispatcher chose to pin — `goal.md` is the customary default structure, not a requirement — and establishes the **workflow appendix**'s one binding, the return address (below, default derived from the id, stored only when overridden); (2) deposit the dispatch message — the tool's one required input — into the new agent's inbox through `litany message` — the same front door every sender uses (§2.11) — so the child's on-ramp *is* the root's on-ramp: a deposit, a driver started by the deposit's own launch machinery, a step-1 drain; (3) return the child's **canonicalized name** — its address (§2.11) — as the `tool_result`. Pinned documents and the deposited message need not be copies of one text: pins carry standing context (a goal, design constraints, non-functional requirements, parallelism warnings), the message says what to do now, and either may exist without restating the other (the shipped tool, with one `goal` input, projects that single text into both — Shipped-state note). Dispatch never spawns an executor and never watches one; the driver the deposit launches is the ordinary one, and the child begins nominally, exactly as any agent begins. There is no child-specific loop and no worker path. The parent/child relationship is nearly nothing — provenance (ancestry plus the id namespace, which sorts and attributes but controls nothing) and one recorded binding — and the step loop knows neither fact.

**The workflow appendix.** What makes a dispatched agent report back is not executor logic keyed on being a child; it is the **workflow appendix**: the per-dispatch tail of the child's workflow — a property of the tool invocation, fixed at the dispatch and composed after the governing config's `workflow.yaml` at read time. A workflow is a flat priority list of *circumstance → action* rules, not a graph: every `advance` re-evaluates the whole list against disk state and runs what matches, and the agent holds no cursor — the workflow has no position (§6). In the epitaph state no config rule matches, so a root simply terminates: advance finds nothing to do. The appendix adds exactly one rule: *in epitaph, result message unsent → send the result message* — epitaph, terminal ref, terminal response iff the agent spoke (§2.6) — *to the address that epitaph names*. The send is itself recorded on the child's branch, and "unsent" is derived from that record — the once-only latch is the child's own history, not a flag beside it — so the advance after the send matches nothing and the terminal state is idempotent. **The appendix's one datum is the return address — the dispatcher's — and its home is single by construction:** it is **derived from the child's id**, since an id already encodes descent (§2.3, an id doubles as its address), so it is computed, never stored (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth). That address is what an **obituary** is sent to (§2.6); a **reply** is not addressed by the appendix at all but by the child's own transcript — whoever last prompted it — which for the dispatch step is this same dispatcher, and which needs no datum here because it is derived from the branch's record (§2.6). Decoupling the *report* target from descent — a watchdog reporting to an operator agent — is an explicit **override**, absent by default; being control rather than context, an override lives outside the worktree (the `inbox/` / `steps/` home, namespaced by id, §2.2), never a worktree-pinned file — which settles the disk-home question against §2.2's worktree-is-context invariant. This is the severability shape (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`): removing the override deletes config and falls back to the derived default, it never edits the core. (The other case the override once had to carry — a critic reporting to a sibling — is no longer one: a sibling that prompts an agent is answered by the reply rule, with no configuration at all.) A user-dispatched root records no dispatch and has no return address, so its obituaries go nowhere and its reply answers the user instead (§2.4). Return totality (§2.3 step 5) is thereby a property of the dispatch primitive — a dispatch cannot fork without an inbox to deposit into — rather than of loop code, and the §8 sweep deposits its `died`-epitaph result to the same derived address. The child's transcript is empty at the fork — its dispatcher's dialog is pruned at the dispatch commit (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance*, bl-5a36) — so the delivered dispatch message opens it user-side and step 1 assembles wire-valid; a fork that retains a dialog (the compactor §2.7, a fork-back-in root §7.2) closes it on the last *settled* step boundary instead (next paragraph: the fork lands mid-tool-step, and the dispatch commit is what makes that true).

**The fork lands inside the parent's tool step.** A tool-call dispatch is executed *as* a tool call: at the instant it forks, the parent's model-output entry carrying the dispatch's own `tool_use` is committed and the answering `messages/NNN-tool.json` is not — it cannot be, because the tool has not returned, and what it will return is the child's address (above). A fork that retains the dialog (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance* — the compactor, a fork-back-in root) therefore inherits precisely what §3.3 already names *tool in progress*: a `tool_use` block with no matching committed `tool_result` entry. That tail is correct on the parent's branch, where it settles moments later, and incorrect on the child's, where nothing will ever answer it — and the pairing rule (above) is validated by the provider, which refuses the whole request. (For every other child the dialog prune removes the whole transcript, tail included, and the question does not arise.)

So **the dispatch commit deletes it** (§2.3 step 2): the trailing model-output entry whose `tool_use` blocks are not all answered, plus every entry after it — the partial results of the same step, which orphan the moment their `tool_use` leaves. This is the only one of the three candidate fixes that keeps the existing invariants intact. Deleting is the sanctioned transcript change (§2.3 — append or delete, never edit-in-place; §5.4), the parent's copy is untouched, and the child's own record ends up saying exactly what happened to it: it inherited a history up to the last settled boundary and was then told what to do. *Filtering at assembly* — skipping a trailing unpaired `tool_use` when composing — is rejected on §2.7's own reasoning about the compactor's inherited blocks: the wire framing is transcript-backed, so the model call would disagree with the branch's record, and assembly would stop being a pure function of the tree (§5.1). *Moving the fork point* past the tool step is rejected because that boundary does not exist yet: the dispatch's `tool_result` is the child's address, so there is no settled tip to fork off until after the fork has happened. The cut costs the child nothing it can act on — the dispatch's arguments are the goal it is pinned with — and, being a deletion, it is recoverable from git history like any other (§5.4). The child's transcript counter is derived from the surviving listing (§2.3), so it simply resumes at the cut.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-4231).** Shipped. The prune is `src/prompt/dispatch/step_commit/unsettled.rs`, called from `step_commit::trim_to_context` — the one function that stages the dispatch commit's trim, reached by both the root's `commit_dispatch` and every child's `subagent::spawn_subagent_branch`, so there is a single home for all three parts (control files, ungranted descriptors, unsettled tool step) and no dispatch path can skip one. It is total and issues no git command when the tail is settled: a root forks off a config commit with no transcript at all, a compactor forks off a checkpoint commit read closing a tool step (§2.7), and a verifier forks off a terminal ref (§6). Before this, the child's first model call was refused outright — `{"type":"error","kind":{"provider":{"status":400}},"message":"No tool output found for function call call_…"}` — which killed the entire agent-initiated dispatch path end to end: every child died on arrival and the parent learned only that "a child returned" with a `died` epitaph. The `litany dispatch` CLI path never had the defect (it is invoked at a step boundary, where the tail is settled), which is why the wire tests passed while the built-in did not.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-5a36, from yog bl-d023).** The child dialog prune is `src/prompt/dispatch/step_commit/inherited.rs` (`prune_inherited_dialog`), called from `subagent::spawn_subagent_branch` — every child dispatch (worker, verifier, compactor-exempted; tool built-in, CLI, and workflow bindings alike), and deliberately *not* a part of `step_commit::trim_to_context`, so the root path (`commit_dispatch`) never runs it and `litany prompt --from` keeps the conversation it exists to resume (§7.2). Before it, a child forked off its parent's tip carried the parent's whole dialog as its own opening context, and the bl-4231 unsettled prune (above) had deleted the parent's `dispatch` `tool_use` — the only evidence in that dialog that the spawn had already happened. The reproduced runaway (yog bl-d023): a user told an agent "spawn a subagent to analyze the files under yog's XDG location"; the child arrived with that instruction as an apparently unanswered user-role message outranking its own deposited goal, obeyed it, and each generation re-dispatched ~3 seconds after arrival until the operator stopped the tree at depth 4. The prune removes `messages/**`, `summary/**` and `skills/**` — the §2.2 branch-scoped set less the three singletons the dispatch commit overwrites — for every role but the compactor, keyed the same way its toolset injection and checkpoint exclusion are (§2.7). Pinned by `step_commit/inherited/tests/fork.rs::{a_child_never_opens_on_its_dispatchers_dialog, a_compactor_keeps_the_dialog_it_exists_to_compact}` and `e2e/prompt_fork_point.rs` (the fork-back-in root still inherits the transcript it resumes).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-c33b).** Children now run. The reshaped dispatch is `src/prompt/child_dispatch.rs` — fork (`subagent::spawn_subagent_branch`: dispatch commit pinning `goal.md` + `soul.md`, config control files removed, §2.3 step 2) then the front door (`inbox::deposit` from the parent + `inbox::probe_and_launch`), returning the child's id. The deposit's probe finds the fresh child quiescent and launches its driver, **`litany advance`** (§6) — the ordinary driver, no child-specific loop and no worker path: the same `advance` hop that drives any agent takes the child from its step-1 drain to its terminal event. At that terminal event `advance` deposits the child's result at the address its epitaph names (`dispatch::result_deposit::recipient`, bl-a96a §2.6 note) — for a reply, the last prompter, which on the dispatch step is this dispatcher; for an obituary, the dispatcher derived from the child's id (`inbox::parent_of`), the shipped realization of the derived default above. The explicit non-default override (watchdog decoupling) is not yet built and needs no new call site when it is, only a lookup ahead of `parent_of`. **`worker.rs` is deleted** — the v0.4 "Phase 1 stops at the dispatch commit, a worker step loop slots on top" path is gone, not extended; `litany dispatch` is no longer a step-loop driver (below, §2.11 Writer/driver totality) but a writer-shaped fork-plus-deposit. The `dispatch` built-in (`litany tool dispatch`) is unchanged: it re-enters `litany dispatch <role>` and re-emits the child's id as its `tool_result`. On the goal-vs-message pin: the tool's single `goal` input is **one input with two projections** — written verbatim to the pinned `goal.md` (§2.8) and deposited as the dispatch message (the step-1 on-ramp) — identical by construction and neither rewritten, so nothing can drift; the fuller shape above (pins carrying standing context *distinct* from a separate "do this now" message) is a capability the single-field tool input does not yet split, deferred without cost.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-fb5c).** Caller-supplied pinned documents reach both start verbs: `litany prompt` and `litany dispatch <role>` take a repeatable `--pin <dest>=<src>` — exact parity, one mechanism (`src/prompt/pinned_doc.rs`) — which freezes `<src>`'s exact bytes at worktree-relative `<dest>` on the dispatch commit, beside `goal.md` and `soul.md`. This is the "whatever documents the dispatcher chose to pin" of the dispatch primitive above, realized for the *caller* (a frontend, an operator): standing context is pinned without rewriting the goal or authoring a config commit. The mechanism is generic — litany owns only the pinning and carries **no filename policy**; which files count as project instructions, their precedence, trust and size are the caller's concerns (yog's, for project-instruction pinning). Validation is construction: a destination must be one collision-free worktree-relative path that neither traverses out (`..`, absolute, `.git`) nor names a harness-owned tree (`goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, the §2.2 control files, `descriptions/**`, `messages/**`, `summary/**`), and every refusal — including an unreadable source — fires in the CLI layer before any branch, ref or inference exists. One refusal is necessarily later: the write itself declines a destination whose existing components — or final path — are symlinks in the freshly forked tree (agent- or template-authored, invisible at CLI time), since writing through one would carry the bytes outside the worktree and leave the commit snapshotting a symlink rather than the pinned bytes. Provenance needs no second copy: the pins are ordinary blobs on the dispatch commit (`git show agents/<id>:<dest>`), ordinary fork inheritance carries them to descendants (§2.2), and whether one composes into assembled context stays a §5.2 manifest question — the caller names a destination the governing manifest's globs see. The model-facing `dispatch` built-in is unchanged (its single `goal` input, bl-c33b note above); harness-initiated dispatches pass the empty set — the general path with empty inputs.

A child need not resolve before its parent's next step, nor before the parent *terminates*: stopping a parent does **not** cascade to its children by default (§2.9). A parent and child are separate agents, not a process hierarchy — so an orphaned child keeps running and, on its own termination, deposits its result into the (now stopped) parent's inbox — its dispatcher, which is both the addressee of its obituary and, unless somebody else has spoken to it since, its last prompter (§2.6) — which **revives** the parent (§2.11): stopping a parent strands nothing. An operator who does want the whole subtree gone asks for it explicitly — `litany stop --stop-children` walks the id namespace (§2.9). A long-lived child that outlives many parent steps, messaging its parent as it goes (§2.11) — a reminder agent, a watchdog, an adversarial critic running alongside — is a legitimate shape, not a stall; a still-running child is live (its executor holds the branch's lock, §2.11), and the §8 silent-death metric counts agents with *no live executor*, never branches still working.

The parent sees those upward messages at its step boundaries (§2.11): if it is still stepping, the deposit drains at the next boundary; if it has gone quiescent after dispatching, the deposit *revives* it (§2.4, §2.11). Because the parent never blocks on a child — it dispatches, terminates its step, and is parked exactly as a root agent is parked awaiting the user — every deposit is seen the moment the parent next steps, whether that step is its own continuation or one the deposit itself starts. A reminder-shaped child needs no polling apparatus to be seen mid-flight: its message either lands at the parent's next boundary or wakes a quiescent parent. Delivery is deferred to a step boundary, never dropped.

**Write paths are assigned.** The harness assigns write paths per tool call and per child agent, so two sibling branches never target the same file. This is a structural guarantee (enforced by the tool executor and the dispatch primitive), not a convention — and it is what makes the work-product transfer (§2.6) conflict-free by construction: sibling children's diffs are disjoint, and a diff that fails to apply indicates a harness defect, not an expected state.

### 2.6 Results and the compaction landing

Merge-back is eliminated. Through v0.3, a finished child was rebased onto the parent's tip, aligned by a `merge=ours` discipline over its branch-scoped context files (`goal.md`, `soul.md`, `summary/**`, `messages/**` — `.gitattributes` drivers, a pre-merge alignment commit, and a `refs/litany/conflicted/*` escape hatch as enforcement), and merged `--no-ff` into the parent. That machinery deletes wholesale, for cause: it was complicated — rebase plus alignment plus driver registration, all to move one summary; it delivered nothing useful — everything the parent should see already traveled through the result-message channel (§2.6), and everything else was precisely what the discipline existed to strip back out; and it broke caching badly — a merge rewrote the parent's tree mid-history, flushing the provider's cached prefix that the append-only transcript exists to keep warm (§5.5). What replaces it is the channel that already existed:

**An agent returns by message.** At completion (§2.3 step 5), the terminating agent deposits a **result message** into the inbox its epitaph addresses (*A reply answers the last prompter*, below) — an ordinary §2.11 deposit: sender-namespaced, create-only, lock-free. **Every result message carries three fields:**

1. **terminal ref** — always. The sha of the child's branch tip at return, from which delivery applies the work-product transfer.
2. **epitaph** — always. The pinned manner of ending: *final response*, *stopped*, *budget-exhausted*, or *died*.
3. **terminal response** — present iff the agent spoke.

On disk this is the ordinary deposit shape (§2.11): `terminal_ref:` and `epitaph:` frontmatter, the terminal response as the body, the body absent exactly when the agent never spoke.

Both the terminal ref and the epitaph are **pinned** in the message so later revival of the child (§2.4) cannot move what the parent was offered: a revived child's live classification flips from `stopped` to `quiescent` (§3.5), but the parent must still see what it was handed — the epitaph is a frozen fact, not the live query §3.5 answers. The epitaph is the **union** over every terminal event, never the exception-set: were it to name only the no-terminal-response cases, it would be a *kind* of message and code would branch on the message's shape, re-opening the special case this whole channel dissolves. As a total field it does the opposite — code branches on the epitaph's **value** (a parent reasons differently about a budget-killed child than a finished one), never on shape. One primitive, one delivery path, one work-product transfer, three fields, one of them sometimes empty. Delivery at the parent's next step boundary is the ordinary delivery commit. The child's transcript, summaries, goal, and soul never appear anywhere in this: they stay on the child's branch, structurally, because nothing merges (§2.3 *Return, not merge-back*).

**A reply answers the last prompter; an obituary reports to the dispatcher.** The recipient is decided by the epitaph's *value*, like everything else about a result message (above) — and the two arms are two different speech acts, not two kinds of message:

- A **reply** (`final-response`) is an answer, and an answer goes to whoever asked. Its address is the **last prompter**: the sender of the newest delivered message in this agent's own transcript (§2.3 — order lives in the filename, the origin token names the sender). Derived, never stored: a recorded "who spoke last" would be a second copy of what `messages/` already says (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth). Two delivered entries are not prompts and are skipped — a **returning child's result message**, which is the answer to a dispatch this agent already made rather than a question put to it (without the skip, every parent would address its own answer to the last child that returned), and **an agent's own note to itself** (§2.11 *Self-messages*), whose answer is the agent's own next step and whose reply would deposit into the very inbox that delivered it. A branch whose whole record of being prompted was squashed by compaction (below) falls back to the id: the dispatch message is the transcript's first entry, so its absence means the record was rewritten, and the id still carries the one sender the branch's own existence records.
- An **obituary** (`stopped`, `budget-exhausted`, `died`) is not an answer at all. It is a structural fact about the tree — *this agent is gone* — and the one party with a standing interest in that is the agent that dispatched it. So it is addressed to the **dispatcher**, whose address is the id's (§2.11), which no rewrite of the transcript can move: a stop, an exhausted ceiling or a death reaches the dispatcher even when the branch was mid-conversation with somebody else.

For the dispatch step the two coincide: the goal arrives as the dispatcher's message (§2.5), so it is the last prompt until somebody else speaks. **The old parent-addressed rule is this rule's first case, not a rule of its own** — one rule replaces two, and with it goes the defect it existed to produce: an operator who typed a question into a child's own conversation had the child's answer deposited into the *parent's* inbox, waking a parent that had asked nothing to relay an answer it did not understand (bl-a96a). Three consequences are the design, not accidents of it:

- **`user` is nobody's inbox.** An operator-prompted reply deposits nothing and is read where it was asked — this agent's own conversation (§2.4, §7.1). That is the same structural no-op a root's deposit already is, so the absent arm stays one case rather than two. The dispatcher of a hijacked child is left **parked** — the ordinary parked state (§2.11 *Undelivered is derived*; a parent waiting on a child is parked exactly as a root is parked awaiting the user, §2.5) — and its recovery is the ordinary primitive: it messages the child, and the child's next reply answers *it*. An operator who speaks into a child's conversation has taken it over, which is what speaking to an agent means (§2.11: every sender is influence-equivalent for the recipient's whole life). Procedure children change nothing here: a compactor or a verifier wears a minted name like any omission-dispatch (§2.3, yog bl-aca4), but *a procedure child is still not an agent an operator speaks to* — nothing invites the address, and speaking to one is the same takeover this rule already covers.
- **Agents can now hold a conversation**, which is the point: a sibling that asked a question gets the answer. An unbounded exchange between two agents is bounded by the same thing that bounds any other work — the §6 `budgets:` ceiling, derived over the tree — and not by a second governor invented for this case.
- **The wake-up follows the deposit.** §2.11's exit protocol revives *the recipient the deposit landed in*, not `parent_of`, or the two would disagree about who the message was for and the deposit would sit undelivered in an inbox nothing was launched at.

**The work-product transfer.** Results that are *files* — code written, docs edited — travel as a diff, not a merge. **The transfer is the dispatcher's, and so are the §6 delivered-child-result bindings**: at delivery, a result message is a lifecycle circumstance **only in the inbox of the agent that dispatched its sender**. Every part of §2.6's return is defined against the fork the dispatcher made — the diff is taken from the child's fork point, which is a commit on the dispatcher's own branch, and §2.5's disjoint-write-path guarantee, the thing that makes the application conflict-free by construction, is a statement about a dispatcher and its own children. An agent under another lineage has neither relationship: diffing across two lineages would drag the *dispatcher's* intervening commits into a tree that never forked from them. So **a reply delivered to a non-dispatcher is an ordinary message** — it lands in the transcript as the message it is, its `epitaph:` and `terminal_ref:` frontmatter model-visible (§2.11), naming how the sender ended and where every byte of its work still lives; nothing is applied to the recipient's tree and no §6 binding fires. Nothing is lost: the terminal ref is the whole work, and an agent that wants it asks for it. For the dispatcher, the transfer proceeds: the executor applies the diff from the child's fork point (its dispatch commit's parent, derived from ancestry) to the terminal sha the message names, **filtered to work products** (§2.1): the child's branch-scoped context paths — `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, `messages/**`, `summary/**`, `skills/**` — are excluded from the application, and so is `descriptions/**` (bl-475a). `descriptions/**` is not branch-scoped — it is config-inherited context (§2.2) — but the dispatch commit prunes it to the child's own role grant (§3.3 "The fork prunes the snapshot to the role's grant"), a harness-driven deletion rather than agent-authored work; excluding it keeps that prune from riding the fork-point→terminal diff back into the parent and shrinking its toolset. The filtered diff lands as one commit at the parent's sequence tail, immediately before the result message's own delivery commit. Its properties are by construction:

- **Append-only and ordered.** The transfer is an ordinary commit landed by the parent's own executor at a step boundary — the same discipline as any delivery (§2.11), serialized by the single executor; two siblings' transfers cannot interleave, and the parent's branch never advances by any hand but its executor's (§2.3).
- **Conflict-free.** Sibling write paths are disjoint and children edit work products from the parent's own fork point (§2.5), so the application is clean unless the write-path guarantee was violated — a harness defect. A diff that fails to apply is **declined loudly**: the result message still delivers (the terminal ref it names preserves every byte of the work on the child's branch), and the failure is marked git-natively at `refs/litany/conflicted/<agent-id>` for operator attention — the same marking pattern as budget exhaustion (§6), surfaced by the UI (§7.1).
- **Declinable.** The transfer is one commit; a parent that decides against the work reverts it — ordinary curation (§5.4), no protocol.

> **Shipped-state note.** The v0.3 merge-back protocol this section retires is **deleted** (bl-4ce8): `src/prompt/merge/` (rebase-then-align-then-merge), the `merge=ours` `.gitattributes` and `merge.ours.driver` scaffold, `MERGE_OURS_PATHS`, and `tests/merge_ours.rs` are gone. The result-message return is shipped as its replacement: `inbox::deposit_result` (the reusable, total deposit — `epitaph:`/`terminal_ref:` frontmatter, body = terminal response iff the agent spoke; §2.11 shape) and, at that time, `inbox::deposit_child_result` (a parent-derivation wrapper, since deleted — the addressing rule moved to the executor's own seam, bl-a96a note below), wired at **every** terminal event the root's `run_exchange` loop reaches today — normal completion (`final-response`), budget exhaustion (`budget-exhausted`, §6), and stop (`stopped`, §2.9, bl-9f53). The stop wiring is the executor-side SIGTERM handler (`src/prompt/dispatch/stop_signal.rs`): a lone async-signal-safe flag store, read at the step loop's check points (before each model call, and immediately after — a stop mid-call surfaces as an `AdapterHalfStream` the loop then treats as a stop, not a failure), breaking to the on-the-way-out deposit (`src/prompt/dispatch/terminal.rs`). The **work-product transfer** (`src/prompt/dispatch/transfer.rs`) is live at delivery: a delivered message carrying `terminal_ref:` applies the fork-point→terminal diff, filtered to work products, as one commit before the delivery commit, and a diff that fails to apply is **declined loudly** at `refs/litany/conflicted/<agent-id>`. **The filter gained `descriptions/**` (bl-475a).** It was omitted at first ship, so a child's dispatch-commit prune of `descriptions/**` to its own role grant (§3.3) rode the transfer back into the parent, deleting entries the parent itself was still granted — observed shrinking a long-lived coordinator's own toolset after its children returned, with a knock-on second symptom (a later sibling's transfer diff no longer applying against the now-divergent parent tree, declined at `refs/litany/conflicted/<agent-id>`). `CONTEXT_EXCLUDES` in `transfer.rs` now carries it alongside the branch-scoped paths. Coverage: `transfer/tests/descriptions.rs::{apply_excludes_descriptions_so_a_childs_role_prune_never_reaches_the_parent, apply_declines_on_a_real_conflict_even_when_the_child_also_pruned_descriptions}`. Children now run (bl-c33b, §2.5 Shipped-state note): `worker.rs` is deleted, `litany dispatch` forks the child and deposits its dispatch message through the front door, and the launched `litany advance` (§6) drives the child through its step loop to a terminal event, where `advance` deposits the child's result into the derived return address. The **deposit** call sites in the root's `run_exchange` still land nothing — a root's reply addresses no inbox — but a *child*'s `advance` terminal now reaches them for real. The stop *handling* around the root's no-op deposit — catch SIGTERM, do not die, deposit-attempt, exit cleanly with the missing-`end` signature on `response.json` untouched — is itself live and proven end-to-end against a real signal (`tests/stop_cli.rs`: `litany stop` on a real root harness; the executor exits 0 rather than dying). `deposit_result` is total and reusable so the §8 sweep (bl-d148, behind the operator verb `litany scan`, §2.11) can deposit `died` epitaphs on a crashed child's behalf. Compaction's own landing was, at this ball, a plain `--no-ff` merge; it has since been replaced by the rebase-forward landing (bl-bc9c, below), leaving **no** merge anywhere in the system.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-a96a, from a live yog session).** *A reply answers the last prompter; an obituary reports to the dispatcher* is shipped, and the parent-addressed rule it replaces is deleted rather than kept beside it. The addressing rule has **one home**, `src/prompt/dispatch/result_deposit.rs`: `recipient(worktree, agent_id, epitaph)` returns the inbox a terminal event addresses, or `None`; `last_prompter` is its transcript derivation — a `readdir` of `messages/`, newest `NNN` first, skipping the agent's own id and any entry whose frontmatter carries a `terminal_ref:` (a return, not a prompt), falling back to `inbox::parent_of` when no prompt survives. `inbox::deposit_child_result` — the old `parent_of` wrapper — is **gone**; `inbox::deposit_result` now takes the recipient it is handed, and the §8 sweep (`scan`) keeps calling it with `parent_of` because a `died` deposit is an obituary. The §2.11 exit protocol was re-pointed with it: `terminal::conclude` derives the recipient once, under the still-held lease (so a rival executor cannot move the tail between the deposit and the wake-up), and `terminal::revive_recipient` — renamed from `revive_parent` — probes and launches *that* address. The transfer/binding scope is one predicate, `child_result::own_result_ref`, read at the drain and at the interpreter alike: a deposit carrying `terminal_ref:` is a §6 delivered-child-result circumstance **iff `parent_of(sender)` is this agent**, and everything else — a sibling's reply included — delivers as an ordinary message. The defect that motivated it, on-disk: an operator typed a keybinding question into a child's own conversation (`messages/027-user.md`, `from: user`); the child answered; the answer was deposited into the parent's inbox as `messages/042-<child-id>.md`, `epitaph: final-response`, and the revived parent spent a step reporting an unrelated answer to the operator. Pinned by `dispatch/result_deposit/tests.rs` (the rule, case by case: the dispatch step, the operator, a sibling, a returning child's result, a self-note, the compacted fallback, and every obituary), `dispatch/drain/tests.rs::{drain_leaves_an_own_childs_result_message_in_the_inbox_for_the_interpreter, drain_delivers_a_reply_from_an_agent_this_one_never_dispatched}`, `child_result/tests/cases.rs::a_reply_from_an_agent_this_one_never_dispatched_is_not_a_child_result`, and end to end on the real child path by `prompt/tests/reply_address.rs::a_reply_goes_to_the_agent_that_prompted_the_step_not_the_dispatcher` (deposit *and* wake-up at the prompter, nothing at the dispatcher).

**Compaction lands by rebase-forward — and nothing merges anywhere.** A compactor (§2.7) forks off the dispatching branch at the **compaction point** `P` — the branch tip at dispatch, or `HEAD~keep_recent` behind it when the workflow retains a recent tail (§6) — and rewrites only what existed at `P`: nominating superseded transcript entries for deletion, landing a new summary. The live agent keeps stepping past `P` the whole time; no quiescence is required and none is imposed. When the compactor returns — and only on a **`final-response`** epitaph: the epitaph is the proof the pass completed, so any other value (`died`, `stopped`, `budget-exhausted`) lands nothing and the result delivers like an ordinary child's (§2.7) — the receiving branch's own executor lands the product at a step boundary, in two moves:

1. **The compaction base.** One ordinary commit whose tree is the tree at `P` with the product applied — the nominated deletions removed, the new `summary/<NNN>.md` added — parented on the **compaction span**'s lower bound: the branch's own founding commit or its previous compaction base, whichever is newer (the same derivation the checkpoint clock reads, §2.7, so the span and the clock cannot disagree). The span — every commit between that bound and `P` — is squashed out of the branch's history. This is the squash §5.4 prices: a deleted entry's blob leaves the branch's reachable history, which is what keeps a transcript-bearing repo from bloating under large committed tool outputs. Nothing is unrecoverable while provenance lives — the compactor's own ref still reaches the entire pre-squash history, until retention deletes it (§9.2).
2. **The replay.** The branch's commits after `P` — appended transcript entries, deliveries, work-product transfers — rebase onto the base in order, and the branch moves to the replayed tip. Every replayed commit's subject survives verbatim; the base commit (`compaction base [<compactor-id>]`) is the checkpoint clock's new origin (§2.7) and the §5.5 rebuild point.

**Zero downtime** is the availability half of the design: the branch never idles (already true of the retired merge-back), and the retained tail — everything after the compaction point — survives verbatim, structurally outside the compactor's view rather than at its discretion. Availability is all it changes: the cache price of compaction is untouched (§5.4, §5.5).

**Replay is conflict-free by construction, and the exceptions have one answer each.** Transcript entries are one immutable file each with monotonic sequence names (§2.3): the live branch only appends filenames the tree at `P` has never seen, so replaying them onto the base cannot collide. Three exception classes exist:

- **A replayed commit rewrites a work product the compaction deleted** — the one overlap the construction admits, a modify/delete. Resolved **live-branch-wins**: the live content is staged, the compaction's deletion of that path is dropped. A dropped deletion is lost compaction, never lost work — the same worst case the compactor's deletion-only toolset already guarantees (§2.7).
- **A conflict git writes markers for** — both sides carry content (concurrent writers on one `summary/**` path is the observed case). Staging that would commit `<<<<<<<` markup into a file §5.2 composes into every subsequent model call on the branch — corrupted context, the one outcome §2.7 promises cannot happen. The landing is **declined loudly**: the rebase aborts (branch and worktree restored bit-for-bit), `refs/litany/conflicted/<compactor-id>` is marked at the compactor's tip — the same escape hatch the work-product transfer uses — and nothing lands. The branch continues uncompacted, which is what any compaction that does not land already means (§2.7); assembly's marker guard (§5.2, bl-c867) stays the defence-in-depth behind the decline.
- **Another compaction landed since `P`** — the point was rebased out of the branch's history, or a landing sits inside the replay span. The pass is **superseded**: nothing lands, nothing is marked (an overtaken pass is not a defect), and the next checkpoint trigger fires afresh. This is also why concurrent compactors need no coordination — whichever lands first wins, and the loser supersedes.

**The landing admits only the compaction product — by construction, not by filter.** A compactor is an ordinary child (§2.7), so its branch grows what every child's grows: the `goal.md`/`soul.md`/`name` its dispatch commit writes, the fork-time prune of `descriptions/**` to its own empty grant (§3.3), and its own transcript under `messages/**`. None of it can cross, because the base is built *additively* from exactly two classes read off the compactor's branch **after its own dispatch commit**: paths deleted there (the `mark_for_deletion` nominations — the dispatch commit's own prunes fall before the boundary and are excluded structurally, the same polarity as the transfer's `descriptions/**` exclude, bl-475a) and `summary/**` paths added there (the `write_summary` product). The retired merge subtracted the dialog from a staged whole-tree merge; the base never stages the compactor's tree at all. The two-channels polarity of the return paths survives unchanged: a *child's* context is contamination the parent must never inherit — its path is a message plus a filtered diff that structurally cannot carry it; a *compactor's* payload is the dispatching branch's own context, rewritten on purpose — its path is the landing, and its own dialog is a child's context like any other.

**History rewrite is bounded and owned.** The landing moves the branch ref non-fast-forward, but the §2.3 advancement invariant is about *who* advances a branch, and no second writer appears: the landing is the receiving branch's own executor's act at a step boundary, like every commit it lands. Commits at or below the span's lower bound — the founding commit, the config ancestry — keep their shas, so role derivation (§6), governing-config resolution (§2.2), and the checkpoint clock (§2.7) read the same homes as before. What changes is provenance shape: a commit inside a squashed span is no longer reachable from the live branch (§7.2 fork-back-in reaches it through the compactor's ref until retention, §9.2), and a child forked from inside the span is unaffected — its own ref holds its history, and its return never needed the parent's: the transfer diffs the child's own fork point to its terminal (above).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-bc9c).** Rebase-forward is shipped as the one landing, replacing the compaction merge wholesale: `src/prompt/compactor/merge/` is deleted and `src/prompt/compactor/land/` is the mechanism — `span` (the compaction point derived from the compactor's dispatch-commit parent; the superseded checks; the span bound from the same `checkpoint::origin` the clock reads), `base` (product classified over `dispatch..tip`; the base minted through a throwaway `--no-checkout` worktree's private index — `read-tree`, `rm --cached`, `restore --staged --source`, `write-tree`, `commit-tree` — never touching the live checkout), and, for the replay, the shared **rebase-forward** move `src/prompt/rebase_forward.rs` (`git rebase --empty=keep --onto <base> <P> <branch>`, the conflict loop reading `ls-files -u` stages: 1+3 resolves live-branch-wins, anything else aborts and declines at `refs/litany/conflicted/<mark-id>`). That module is shared with the retarget landing (§2.2, bl-22a5) rather than copied into it: the two landings differ only in the base they mint and the ref a decline marks. The workflow action is renamed **`land_compaction`**; the retired spelling `compaction_merge` still parses to it — configs are frozen commits (§2.2) and existing workspaces must keep resolving. The base subject is `compaction base [<compactor-id>]`; the checkpoint clock reads it alongside the retired `compaction merge [` subject, so pre-replacement histories keep their origins. Span selection ships as `compaction.intermediate.keep_recent` (§6): the flush forks the compactor off `HEAD~keep_recent`; a due clock whose commits-since-checkpoint have not outgrown the retained tail dispatches nothing (the span is empty); and `keep_recent >= n` under `every_n_commits` is refused at config load, since every landing would re-arm the clock. One defect of the retired merge is fixed structurally rather than patched: the merge imported the compactor's dispatch-commit prune of `descriptions/**` — a deletion, which its filter passed untouched — stripping the dispatching branch's own descriptors at every compaction (the bl-475a symptom on the other channel); the base's `dispatch..tip` boundary excludes fork-time prunes by construction (`land/tests/mod.rs::the_compactors_dialog_and_fork_prunes_never_cross`). The merge-mechanics passages in the §2.7 shipped-state notes (bl-9dbd items 3–5, bl-a9eb items 1 and 4, bl-4fdf) and in the §6 notes (bl-6a3b's `compaction_merge` naming, bl-2d5a's landing description) describe the retired mechanism and stand as history; their invariants — epitaph gating, checkpoint scope, decline on a content conflict, live-branch-wins, dialog-never-crosses — all survive in the landing, pinned by `src/prompt/compactor/land/tests/`.

Step records pass through none of this. They live at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/NNN/` — outside every worktree, shared across the whole tree from the moment they're written (§2.2, §2.3), namespaced by agent id, collision-free with no alignment of any kind.

### 2.7 Compaction

Compaction (not compression — the term is reserved for model-weights quantization in the taxonomy) is the process of producing a signal-preserving, minimal version of a branch's context. A **compactor** is a child agent that performs compaction. It has almost no privileged position in the architecture: compactors are dispatched like any other child agent and run on their own branches; what is unique to them is how their output lands — the compaction landing (§2.6), the rebase-forward. What distinguishes a compactor otherwise is its goal (produce a summary of the dispatching branch's history) and its toolset.

Compactor toolset (v1):

- **`write_summary(content)`** — writes the compacted summary file on the compactor branch.
- **`mark_for_deletion(path)`** — nominates a file on the compactor branch for removal. The harness applies the deletions at commit time.

**What is not the branch's history is not a pass's to shed.** One invariant with three instances, and one predicate decides all of them (`compactor::tools::not_compaction_eligible`). Two are *what the dispatch wrote*: a fact set when the branch was created and never rewritten is not history. The first instance is the **goal**. A goal is set at one dispatch and never rewritten (§2.8), and it has **two projections written by that dispatch**: the pinned `goal.md`, and the **dispatch entry** — the transcript entry the same text landed as when it came through the front door (§2.11: a root's opening user message, a child's dispatch message). Both are the goal, not history, so `mark_for_deletion` **declines** either: the nomination is refused in-band, at the door, and nothing is staged. This is not an exemption for "the first message". It is the one place where the compactor is *shown the same fact twice* — its own boilerplate goal quotes the dispatching branch's `goal.md` verbatim (below), while the dispatch entry sits in the transcript it is told to prune — so the branch's opening prompt is the single entry that reads as **pure duplication** to a model correctly doing its job. Every later user message has no second copy and was never at risk. The decline is at the *nomination* rather than at the landing because the fact is knowable from the path alone; live-branch-wins (§2.6) is resolved at the landing precisely because *its* fact — a race with the live branch — is not knowable when the compactor nominates. Declining in-band also keeps the compactor's own model of the branch true: a summary is never written premised on a deletion that silently did not happen.

**The system slot's files are the second instance** (bl-541b). `goal.md`, `soul.md` and `name` are the structural wire homes of the system slot (§5.2), written at the dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2) and never rewritten on the branch thereafter (§2.3) — the same never-rewritten shape the goal has, so `mark_for_deletion` declines all three, at the same door, through the same predicate and with no second mechanism. What makes them worth naming rather than leaving to the landing is that the consequence is worse than the dispatch entry's. A compactor writes its own three at its **own** dispatch commit, so a nomination after that is a deletion inside the `dispatch..tip` range the landing classifies as the compactor's product (§2.6): it lands as a `git rm` against the *dispatching* branch's tree, and that branch then keeps stepping with no goal, no soul, or no identity line on every later model call — a system slot composed from a tree that no longer holds it. Unlike the dispatch entry this was never observed in the wild; it is closed on the same knowable-from-the-path argument rather than on evidence, which is exactly what makes it cheap to close.

**What the compaction writes is the third instance** (bl-c7bb). The pass's own **summary** is not the branch's history either — it is the thing the history is being turned into — and it is the instance with the largest blast radius, because the landing admits the summary and the deletions and nothing else (§2.6). A landing carrying a `git rm` of its own summary therefore squashes the whole compaction span into a base that holds no record of it: the uncompacted content is gone and there is no summary of it. This was observed live, in one compactor's own transcript: `write_summary` returned `summary/001.md` and `mark_for_deletion` of that same path was accepted seconds later, through the other half of the same tool pair. The damage did not land only because a second compactor's base superseded that one — luck, not a guard.

A blanket `summary/**` refusal would be the wrong shape. Superseding an *earlier* pass's summary is what the compactor is instructed to do (`souls/compactor.md`), and it is the only thing that keeps the summary chain from growing without bound — "a summary the compactor cannot see is a summary it *destroys* when it supersedes it" cuts both ways. The distinction is this run's output versus a prior run's, and it is read at the anchor the landing already classifies the product by: what changed after the compactor's **own dispatch commit** is the pass's, and everything at or before it is the branch it inherited (§2.6, `compactor::land::base`). One definition of "the compaction product", read by the landing that carries it forward and by the door that refuses to shed it. It is read against the index rather than a commit pair, which is exactly the set `git rm` could carry away; an untracked write is not in that set and meets the nonexistent-path decline instead.

Giving the compactor no general filesystem write surface makes "deletion-only" structural rather than disciplinary: the worst case is lost information, never corrupted information. The pair is owned by this procedure, not by the role: it is injected by the step for the compactor role alone, never declared in a `providers.yaml` `tools:` list (§4.3 — the role stays a pure config-selection key; the instrument stays the landing's). Its view of the dispatching branch's work needs no special channel: forked off the compaction point (§2.6), its worktree carries that branch's transcript (§2.3), summaries, and work products, and it is composed from that tree like any other role's — through the `compactor` entry in `manifest.yaml` (§5.1: the tree bounds, the manifest selects). What the shipped entry selects is `summary/**`, alongside the unconditional transcript tail and the system slot (§5.2). Widening the view is a config edit to that entry, not a code change.

**What the compactor is told to read is what it receives** (bl-2c63). The boilerplate goal (`src/prompt/compactor/mod.rs`) names three sources, and the shipped configuration delivers all three:

- **The transcript**, which composes unconditionally (§5.2) and needs no entry.
- **The summary chain**, which composes through `order: [summary/**]` **or nowhere**. This category is not a deployment preference but what "signal-preserving" costs: each summary supersedes its predecessor and nominates it for deletion, and the compaction landing admits only the summary and the deletions (§2.6), so a prior compactor's reasoning never enters the dispatching branch's transcript. A summary the compactor cannot see is a summary it *destroys* when it supersedes it — lost signal dressed as compaction.
- **The dispatching branch's goal**, which the compactor decides relevance against. It reaches the compactor by *quotation*: the compaction dispatch reads that branch's `goal.md` and interpolates it into the compactor's own boilerplate goal, which the system slot then composes (§2.3). No second channel and no new path — the child's own `goal.md` *is* that boilerplate, so the parent's goal has no other route in, and the source file is written once and never rewritten (§2.8), so the quote cannot drift from it.

**And nothing else is named.** Work products are out of both the entry and the prose: they are "everything else" in the tree, with no honest glob, and a view of the branch's *history* is not a restatement of its outputs — the acts that produced them are already transcript entries, and §2.6 keeps the products themselves out of the compaction product entirely. Spent `skills/` bodies stay nominable without being composed: `load_skill` returns the worktree path it wrote (§3.3), so the transcript names every loaded body at a fraction of its bytes. So **the compactor gains no read tool**: with nothing it is told to read left uncomposed, a general filesystem-read surface would buy nothing and would put a grant on the one role whose empty grant is what makes its two-tool ceiling exact (`compactor::builtin_tool_schemas`).

**Declared is not callable.** That inherited view is also inherited *history*: the compactor forks with the dispatching branch's transcript in its tree (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance*), and the checkpoint clock is read closing a tool step (§6), so the history a compactor ships to the provider routinely contains `tool_use` / `tool_result` pairs for tools that are not its two. A provider validates the request as a whole and refuses one whose history names a tool the `tools: [...]` array omits, so the compactor's request **declares** those inherited tools alongside its own pair (§3.3 *Tools-list assembly*, closure). It does not gain them: a compactor reaching for an inherited tool is declined in-band — an `is_error` `tool_result` naming its own two tools, the executor never entered — so "the compactor can only summarize and delete" stays structural. The alternative, rewriting or textualizing the inherited `tool_use` blocks to fit the two-tool array, is rejected outright: transcript entries are immutable (§2.3) and the wire framing is transcript-backed (§3.3), so it would make the model call disagree with the branch's own record.

**Checkpoints.** Compaction runs at checkpoints during a branch's execution: the harness dispatches a compactor off the **compaction point** (§2.6) — the branch's tip, or `HEAD~keep_recent` behind it when the workflow retains a recent tail (§6; a due clock whose whole span sits inside the retained tail dispatches nothing) — with a goal that instructs it to compact. The compactor writes a new numbered summary (`summary/<seq>.md`, on its own branch) and marks superseded files — prior summaries, stale transcript entries, spent skill bodies — for deletion. Its return lands by rebase-forward (§2.6), admitting **those two products alone** — the summary and the deletions — so the compactor's own goal, soul, and transcript stay on its branch; the dispatching agent steps straight through the whole operation, since compaction is surgical and no quiescence is required (§2.6). Checkpoint triggers are declared in `workflow.yaml` (§6): by commit count, by elapsed time, or by an explicit `flush` action the agent may call. A branch with no configured trigger never compacts.

**The compaction-eligible set.** Three invariants bound what a checkpoint trigger may fire on, and all three are properties of the branch, not of the config:

- **A branch's checkpoint clock starts at its own founding commit.** Every branch is forked off its parent's tip and inherits the parent's whole history (§2.3 *Fork and inheritance*), so "commits on this branch" can never mean "commits reachable from HEAD" — a seconds-old child would read its parent's history as its own and be due on arrival. The commit that founds a branch is the only commit naming it: its **dispatch commit**, `dispatch: <role> [<agent-id>]` for a child and `step 001: dispatch [<agent-id>]` for a root. Both end in `[<agent-id>]`, so one pattern founds every branch and the root is the general case rather than an exception. The clock measures from the newer of that commit and the branch's last compaction base (a retired-mechanism `compaction merge [` commit still counts, so pre-replacement histories keep their origins). After a landing, commits-since-checkpoint is exactly the replayed tail — the branch's uncompacted content — which is the honest reading: what the span left out is what the next pass is due against.
- **A compactor is never compaction-eligible.** A compactor *is* the compaction, not a subject of one: compacting it would fork a compactor off a compactor, whose transcript is the very compaction it was dispatched to perform. Its role is derived from the same founding commit (§6 role-aware resolution), so the exclusion needs no new state.
- **A branch with a compaction in flight is not due** (bl-b9f0). The two above bound *which branches* may be compacted; this one bounds *when* a branch that may be is due, and it is about a case neither of them reaches — a branch legitimately eligible, firing the same checkpoint again because the answer to its last firing has not come back. The clock measures from a landing, and a compaction that has been dispatched has landed nothing, so the next boundary computes the same span, sees the same count, and dispatches a second compactor: two were observed eight seconds apart on a twelve-step conversation, each a full model loop at the compactor's model, and nothing bounded it at two. The suppressor is a derivation, not a stored flag, and it is assembled from three facts that already have single homes: a compactor child's branch is `<parent>-<sub-id>` (the hyphenated descent, §2.3), its role is its dispatch commit's subject (§6), and whether it has come back is the **returned mark** `refs/litany/returned/<child>` every result deposit writes (§8). All three are refs or commit subjects readable from the branch's own worktree, so the read costs one `for-each-ref` plus one `show-ref` per child. The window it closes is dispatch-to-return; the residual — return-to-landing — is at most one step wide, because the hop interprets pending child results *before* it steps and runs the checkpoint *after*, and a pass dispatched inside it is one duplicate the landing then refuses (below), not a runaway.

Any of the first two alone is sufficient against the runaway case; all three are stated because they are different facts, and the first also governs every non-compactor child.

**Two passes racing to one summary path: the late lander is refused.** Compactors forked off the same point each number their summary `001` — `write_summary` picks the next seq by scanning a directory the other pass's write is not in — so "what if both land" is a real question and the answer is that they cannot. The first landing rebases the compaction point out of the branch (§2.6 rebase-forward), and the second pass then cannot prove its own point is still reachable from `HEAD`, so it is **superseded**: it lands nothing, is reported to the operator, and is not marked a defect, because a checkpoint that fires afresh afterwards is the correct outcome. The summary on the branch is therefore always some pass's own text, never an overwrite of one pass's by another's and never a merge of the two. The path is not versioned to dodge the collision, and the collision is not resolved last-write: the landing's existing supersede arm *is* the answer, and it needs no new mechanism.

There is no *terminal* compaction stage anymore: a child's result message carries its own terminal response (§2.6), not a compactor product, and with merge-back gone (§2.6) there is no merge payload to slim before returning. A workflow that wants a distilled hand-off before a child returns binds a compactor dispatch to the child's completion event (§6) — policy in config, not a pipeline stage.

Summaries are never parent-visible: `summary/**` is branch-scoped (§2.2) and excluded from the work-product transfer (§2.6), so a child's summaries structurally cannot reach its parent's tree — they exist to help the branch manage its own context window. What a parent receives from a child is the result message and the filtered diff (§2.6); "parents see only returned results, never raw internal state" is enforced by the shape of the channel, not by a merge discipline.

**Compaction is the sole sanctioned reorganization point — and it is the rebase-forward landing.** Between compactions, assembly is append-only (§5.5): the assembled context only grows at the tail, keeping the provider's cached prefix valid. The landing (§2.6) is where the context is rebuilt: the compaction base rewrites the tree at the compaction point — superseded transcript entries deleted, a new summary landed — the live tail replays on top, the manifest's category order is reapplied at the next assembly (§5.2), and the cache prefix is deliberately flushed from the point onward. The base commit *is* the rebuild point (§5.5); no other event reorganizes a running branch's context. Why this one rewrite is legitimate where merge-back was not is the two-channels polarity of §2.6: a compactor's payload is the dispatching branch's own context, rewritten on purpose — its payload, not its contamination.

In every other respect the compactor is an ordinary child: it returns by depositing its result message like any other (§2.5, §2.6), and the dispatching branch does not idle while it runs (§2.6).

Compaction failures (compactor produces garbage, times out, etc.) land no merge: the branch continues uncompacted and retries at its next checkpoint trigger; the failed compactor is an ordinary stopped child (§2.5, §2.9), surfaced for user review like any other child failure.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-9dbd).** The v0.3 terminal-compaction stub is **deleted** and the real compaction mechanism ships, split into three tested procedures under `src/prompt/compactor/`. (1) **No terminal compaction.** The stage the spec deleted is gone from code: `terminal::finish` no longer dispatches a compactor at a final response, `src/prompt/dispatcher.rs` (the `Dispatcher`/`SpawnDispatcher` re-entry that existed only to run the terminal stub) and the `DispatchFailed` error are removed with it, and the `deps.dispatcher` field is dropped from `Deps`. (2) **The compactor is an ordinary child**, dispatched through `child_dispatch` like a worker: `ChildDispatchRequest` gained a `role` field, so `litany dispatch compactor` (`dispatch_cli`) forks a compactor-souled child (`souls/compactor.md`) with a boilerplate goal (`--goal` rejected) that runs its own step loop via `litany advance` — a **real model call via `bz`**, no stub. (3) **The two-tool toolset is built into the primitive** (`src/prompt/tool/builtin/compaction/`, wired at `litany tool write_summary` / `litany tool mark_for_deletion`, `compactor::tools`): `write_summary` writes the next `summary/<NNN>.md`; `mark_for_deletion` is a **deletion-only** `git rm` (it can remove, never write content — structural, not disciplinary), staged so the compactor step's `git add -A` tool commit carries it and the compaction merge lands it. They are available to the compactor role's injected toolset alone, never a `providers.yaml` `tools:` list. (4) **Checkpoint triggers** are read at the step boundary by the executor (`compactor::checkpoint`): `due(compaction_config, state)` is a pure predicate over a `CheckpointState` the executor derives from git (`state()` — commit count and elapsed seconds measured from the last compaction-merge commit, or the branch root when none has landed; both derived, never stored), handling `every_n_commits`, `every_t_seconds`, and the agent-elected `on_flush`; a branch with no configured trigger never compacts, and a malformed threshold fails closed. (5) **The compaction merge** (`compactor::merge`, `MergeOutcome`) lands the compactor branch `--no-ff` into the dispatching branch: `merge --no-ff --no-commit` then `git add -A` (which stages live-branch state) then commit, so the summary and every non-overlapping deletion land while the **one overlap — a work product the live branch rewrote since `C`** — resolves **live-branch-wins** (git leaves the live version in the worktree on a modify/delete, and `add -A` keeps it, dropping the compactor's deletion). The merge commit is the §5.5 cache-rebuild point; an up-to-date ref is a `NoOp`. The disjoint-write-set argument (§2.6) and the dropped-deletion overlap are proven against real git (`compactor/merge/tests.rs` — `overlap_drops_work_product_deletion_live_branch_wins`). Compaction failure (a non-final-response epitaph) lands no merge; the branch continues uncompacted — enforced at the delivered-result interpreter (bl-1c94): `compaction_merge` is epitaph-gated in `child_result.rs`, and a non-`final-response` compactor return is delivered like an ordinary child's result instead, so the parent sees the epitaph and nothing of the compactor's branch crosses. **Coordinated with bl-6a3b (deferred, tracked):** the workflow-binding interpreter declares `worker_flush: dispatch(compactor)` and `compactor_return: compaction_merge` in the shipped template and parses both actions. **These bindings are now live (bl-2d5a):** the delivered-child-result interpreter (`src/prompt/dispatch/child_result.rs`) wires `compactor_return`→`compactor::merge` and the `worker_flush` checkpoint→`dispatch(compactor)` at the `litany advance` step-boundary seam, and role-aware `advance` resolution (`src/prompt/resolve.rs` + `src/prompt/role.rs`) resolves a dispatched compactor's soul + injected toolset from its dispatch commit subject rather than the worker default — see the §6 bl-2d5a shipped-state note.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-a9eb, from yog bl-ebbd).** The three defects a live workspace exhibited — one root conversation minting 226 compactor branches in ~90 seconds under a declared `max_depth: 4`, ending with three unresolved git conflicts committed into its own live `summary/001.md` — are fixed, each by an invariant rather than a per-case guard. (1) **Checkpoint scope** (`compactor::checkpoint`): `state()` now takes the agent id and measures from the branch's own **founding commit** — one `git log -n1 -E --grep '\[<agent-id>\]$' --grep '^compaction merge \['`, which returns the newer of the branch's dispatch commit and its last compaction merge and is uniform over roots and children. Before this the count measured from the branch *root*, so a compactor that inherited a 20-commit parent history was due the instant it existed and dispatched a compactor of its own, generation after generation. (2) **Role exclusion**: `CheckpointState` carries `is_compactor`, derived from that same founding commit through `prompt::role::derive` (the single home for an agent's role), and `due()` returns `false` for it under every trigger — the compaction-eligible set excludes the compaction. (3) **One budget gate at the one fork** (`child_dispatch::run`): the governing config's `workflow.yaml` `budgets:` are evaluated against the child's prospective id *before* `spawn_subagent_branch`, so a `max_depth` breach never creates a ref, a worktree, an inbox or a launch; the refusal is `Error::DispatchRefused`. `child_dispatch::run_procedure` is the harness-initiated wrapper the §6 bindings use (`worker_flush`, the verifier gate and its reject re-dispatch) — identical gate, refusal reported rather than propagated.
