(4) **The compaction merge declines a content conflict** (`compactor::merge::decline`): before `git add -A`, `git ls-files -u` is read for paths carrying both stage 2 and stage 3 — exactly the case where git wrote conflict markers — and any such path aborts the merge, marks `refs/litany/conflicted/<compactor-id>` at the compactor's tip, and lands nothing (`MergeOutcome::Conflicted`); a modify/delete overlap populates one stage only and stays live-branch-wins. The conflicted-ref prefix now has one home, `workspace::CONFLICTED_REF_PREFIX`, shared with the work-product transfer. Pinned by `checkpoint/tests/mod.rs::a_compactor_is_never_compaction_eligible` and `checkpoint/tests/derive.rs::{state_measures_from_the_branchs_own_dispatch_commit_not_inherited_history, state_reads_the_branch_role_from_its_own_dispatch_commit}` (one file until the bl-89f7 split, below), `child_result/tests/gate.rs::run_flush_never_compacts_a_compactor_branch`, `child_dispatch/tests/budget.rs::{max_depth_refuses_the_dispatch_that_would_breach_it, a_workflow_triggered_dispatch_reports_a_refusal_and_does_not_fail_the_branch}`, and `compactor/merge/tests/mod.rs::a_content_conflict_is_refused_and_marked_never_committed`.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-4fdf).** The merge's **filter to the compaction product** (§2.6) is live in `compactor::merge`. The unfiltered merge landed the compactor's whole tree: its dispatch `goal.md`/`soul.md` overwrote the dispatching branch's, and its own `messages/**` entries — including a failed tool entry whose `tool_result` had no `tool_use` on the parent's branch — were imported into the parent's transcript, wedging the parent's next step. `merge` now runs one filtering pass between `git add -A` and the commit: under `--no-commit` the merge's `HEAD` is still the dispatching branch's own tip, so `git diff --cached --no-renames HEAD -- ':(exclude)summary'` names the three classes by themselves — `A` is a compactor transcript entry (removed with `git rm`), `M` is its `goal.md`/`soul.md` or a filename collision git resolved into a conflict (restored with `git checkout HEAD --`), and `D` is the compaction the merge exists to land (left untouched). Live-branch-wins is unaffected: it is a dropped *deletion*, and the filter reads deletions not at all. Pinned end to end — `compactor/merge/tests.rs::the_compactors_own_dialog_never_crosses_the_merge` (the summary and the deletion land, zero compactor entries cross, the dispatching branch keeps its own goal, and the compactor's ref keeps its full transcript) and `prompt/tests/advance_compaction.rs::a_compaction_merge_lands_the_product_and_the_next_step_assembles_clean` (the hop merges, then the parent's next step assembles and calls with no orphaned `tool_result` in its request).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-89f7).** The **checkpoint clock never fired**, and the bl-a9eb note above records why: its founding pattern was `--grep '\[<agent-id>\]$'` — the `[<agent-id>]` tail alone. The executor's own transcript commits carry that tail too (`transcript NNN: <origin> [<agent-id>]`, `dispatch/transcript.rs`; likewise the stray recovery's `transcript: recover delivered stray [<agent-id>]`), and `git log -n 1` stops at the newest match, so `checkpoint::origin` answered with the **newest transcript commit** rather than the branch's dispatch commit. `commits_since_checkpoint` was therefore 0 or 1 forever and `every_n_commits` could not reach any threshold; `every_t_seconds` measured from that same commit's time and was equally dead; and the compaction span's lower bound (`land/span.rs`) was mis-derived wherever no base had landed yet. The suite was green because the checkpoint tests built their branches with synthetic subjects (`"root"`, `"step"`) that carry no tail at all, so the fallback answered and the production subject shape was never put to `origin`. The fix is the **one derivation** bl-22a5 already established for the retarget landing: the two dispatch spellings matched *exactly*, now hoisted to `role::founding_pattern(agent_id)` — `^(dispatch: .+|step 001: dispatch) \[<agent-id>\]$` — which `role::founding_sha` and `checkpoint::origin` both grep, `origin` OR'ing it with its two landing prefixes as before. Pinned by `checkpoint/tests/derive.rs::state_measures_from_the_dispatch_commit_not_a_later_transcript_commit`, whose branch carries real subjects: a `step 001: dispatch [p1]` founding plus two `transcript NNN: <origin> [p1]` commits reads 2, not 0, and a child founded by `dispatch: worker [p1-c1]` is unmoved by a following stray-recovery commit. The operator's ruling is that the first prompt is handled **exactly like every other user prompt**, and the fix is the eligibility rule above, enforced at `compactor::tools::mark_for_deletion` (`Error::DispatchEntryNotEligible`), with the boilerplate goal stating it so the decline is predictable rather than a surprise. The dispatch entry is derived from its name alone — the `NNN-` prefix the transcript counter already owns — so the rule needs no tree, no config and no state.

> **Why the duplication itself was not dissolved instead.** It is a *projection*, not a second fact: one input written at one dispatch into two places, neither ever rewritten (`src/prompt/child_dispatch.rs`; `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` single source of truth is satisfied because nothing can drift). Neither direction of collapse holds. `goal.md` cannot *derive* from the first transcript entry, because the transcript is exactly the region compaction rewrites — a derived goal would silently change identity the moment any early entry was shed, breaking §2.8's "a goal is set at dispatch and is not rewritten during execution", and it would take `goal.md` out of the manifest's `pinned` set, out of the souls that name it, out of the retarget re-pin (§7.2) and out of the compactor's own quote. The entry cannot derive from `goal.md` either: the ruling forbids the exemption, and a root whose inbox deposit never happened has an **empty** message array on its first model call, which no provider accepts. Nor does moving the compaction span's lower bound reach the defect — the base applies the nominated deletions to the tree at the compaction point whatever commit it is parented on (`compactor::land::base`), so the span's bound never governed which *files* the pass removes. A `keep_first` knob was refused outright: policy added to work around a duplication nobody wants.

### 2.8 Goals

Every agent has a **goal**: the stated objective it was dispatched with. The goal is written to `goal.md` at the root of the branch's worktree (alongside `soul.md`) on the dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2) and is pinned at the head of the context assembled for every model call on that branch, regardless of position in the message sequence. A child's goal lives on the child's branch and nowhere else: no merge-back exists and the work-product transfer excludes it (§2.6), so a child's goal structurally cannot touch its parent's.

An agent's **name** (§2.1, §2.3) follows the same law for the same reason — set at the dispatch that creates the agent, written beside `goal.md`, never rewritten — and it reaches the model through the same channel: **the system slot states it, on every model call on the branch.** After the goal block, before the role's soul, the slot carries one derived sentence and no more —

```
Your name is <name>.
```

— because a name is *who the agent is*, not *what it is to do*: no instruction rides it, and nothing about being addressable, replying, or introducing itself is attached (an agent that needs those learns them from the `message` tool's description, §3.3). The line is **derived at composition time from the `name` file** and stored nowhere else (§2.3 — one home; `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` single source of truth), so it is a query on the read-state tree like every other part of the request and replay reproduces it byte-for-byte (§2.10). An **unnamed agent states nothing**: the empty `name` blob composes an empty identity, leaving the slot byte-identical to a pre-name harness's, so absence is the general path with empty inputs rather than a second shape (§2.3 *Every dispatch commit writes the file*) — a state only the pre-mint stock still occupies, since every new dispatch settles a supplied or minted name (§2.3, yog bl-aca4).

The name rides the system slot rather than being prepended to the first user message, which is the failure this replaced: the first user message is the *user's*, and stamping harness prose onto it makes an agent's identity indistinguishable from something its sender said. Identity belongs on the channel that already carries the role's soul.

Because the slot is the name's structural wire home, the `name` file is never *also* composed as body text — a role naming it in `pinned` or `order` adds nothing and must not send it twice (§5.2 *Structural wire homes*).

A goal is set at dispatch and is not rewritten during execution. If an agent determines its goal is wrong, the expected workflow is:

1. Terminate the current branch (stopped or with an explanatory terminal response).
2. Analyze the failure.
3. Dispatch a new agent with a corrected goal and/or a different config (§2.2).

Rewriting a goal in place is not structurally forbidden but is not a core path — the expected unit of iteration is the agent, not the goal. Mid-flight steering does not rewrite the goal either: a message (§2.11) adds context *beside* the pinned goal; the goal stays what the agent was dispatched with.

The pinned goal resolves the recency-decay problem in deep agent trees where sequence-as-authority (last user message = current order) fails.

### 2.9 Stopped branches

Stops are aggressive but agent-scoped. A stop targets **one agent** — the user-driven case is the CLI subcommand `litany stop <workspace> <agent>` per §3.4, idempotent against an already-terminal branch; the timeout/budget case is the same act issued by the harness (§6). It does **not** reach across the agent boundary into that agent's children by default: parent and child are separate agents, not a process hierarchy, so stopping a parent leaves a running child alive to finish and revive the parent on its return deposit (§2.5, §2.11). The agent→agent cascade is opt-in — `litany stop --stop-children` (below) — never a kernel-group side effect. When a stop is issued:

1. SIGTERM is sent to the process group of the **one executor** driving the branch. The kernel cascades through that group — the executor's *own* provider adapter and cooperating tool subprocesses (§3.3, §4.4), which are its limbs and must die with it (in-flight HTTP dropped) — and stops there: a dispatched child harness has taken its own process group (below), so the group signal does not cross into it.
2. In-flight HTTP requests to provider endpoints are dropped as a side effect of the adapter receiving SIGTERM — `bz` installs no signal handlers and dies at once (§4.4 Cancellation); already-flushed event lines stay valid.
3. The adapter (`bz`) is already dead (step 2), so the latest step's `response.json` (§4.4 "On-disk response shape: appended attempt segments") will close without a terminal `end` event — this missing terminal event *is* the on-disk signature of a stopped step. No separate cancel marker is written; the absence of a trailing `end` on a closed file is sufficient (§3.5). The executor itself does **not** die on the spot: SIGTERM is catchable (unlike the uncatchable crashes of §8), and its handler does one async-signal-safe thing — set a flag — which the step loop reads at its next check point and, on its way out, deposits the branch's **result message** with a `stopped` epitaph into its parent's inbox (§2.6, and "deposits a result" below) — a stop is an **obituary**, so it is addressed to the dispatcher whoever last prompted the branch, and a root has no dispatcher — then exits, at which point the kernel closes its fds and the `response.json` receives `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` (§3.5). The deposit lands in the parent's inbox tree; the stop signature lives on the stopped branch's own `response.json` — independent writes, neither disturbing the other, so the missing-`end` signature is untouched by the return. **The check points are not model-call-only.** The flag is read between steps, during the model call, *and* across tool execution (§3.3): a stop landing while the executor is running a tool takes the identical path, because the tool subprocesses are the executor's limbs and die with the group SIGTERM (steps 1-2). A tool cut down that way reports its signal death as a harness-level `KilledBySignal` fault (§2.10), but with the stop flag set the executor reads it as *this* stop, not a fault — so a tool-window stop reaches the one stopped-deposit exit above, never a non-zero propagation. **The model-call window obeys the same rule, and there the flag — not the error's shape — is what classifies.** A kill lands wherever the adapter happened to be, so what the harness is left holding varies: a clean line boundary yields the missing-`end` half-stream, a torn one an unparseable fragment, an already-failed attempt a provider error. Each is a *different* harness fault out of stop context, and none is distinguishable from a genuine one on its own evidence — the flag is the only reliable witness that this was the stop. So with the flag set the executor discards whatever the model call surfaced and takes the stopped-deposit exit; with no flag set the same fault propagates as a fault (a genuinely dying adapter must still surface, §2.10). Since wall time is dominated by the model call, this is the *ordinary* stop rather than an edge — the clean exit is the common observable, not the rare one. The stop flag also bounds the retry loop (§2.10): once it is set the executor stops re-invoking `bz`, so a stop never spends a further model call. There is thus a single terminal sequence for every stop, whichever window it lands in; the model-call window merely also leaves the missing-`end` signature on `response.json`, while a tool window's step already closed its `response.json` with `end` (the stop's evidence there is derived state — no live executor — not a written flag, §2.4/§3.5).
4. The branch is left where it was, **with its tool window settled** (below). Its `stopped` status is derived state, not a written flag — consistent with `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Single source of truth": a branch with no live executor whose latest step's `response.json` is closed without a trailing `end` is `stopped` (§3.5). (Crashes, kills, and explicit user stops are indistinguishable on disk and treated identically.)

**The stopped exit settles its own tool window.** A stop landing in a tool window finds the step's model-output entry already committed (§2.5 — the assistant entry lands before any tool runs) and one or more of its `tool_use` blocks unanswered. Left that way the branch tip is the §6 *one non-replayable state*, which `litany advance` declines loudly — so no deposit could ever revive the branch, and the stop would **retire** the agent rather than end the work it had in flight, contradicting the resume path below ("a stop is not a locked door"). So on its way out, before the deposit, the executor commits **one in-band `is_error` `tool_result` per unanswered `tool_use` id** — the same in-band shape a grant decline and a tool-control refusal already commit (§3.3) — saying the invocation was cut short and produced no result. The tail is then settled, the warrant is *model call due* (§6), an ordinary deposit revives the agent, and the model reads **in band** that it was interrupted, which is both the truthful record and the useful one. Deleting the tail — what the dispatch commit does at a *fork* (§2.3 step 2, §2.5) — is the wrong repair here: that tail is on the agent's **own** branch, where deleting it would discard the assistant's own reasoning and leave the model no evidence it was ever cut off. A stop landing in the **model-call window** needs none of this: the loop breaks before the assistant entry is committed, so the tail is already settled and nothing is unpaired. A **hold** is deliberately not settled (§3.3 *Tool control*): a parked branch's unpaired tail plus its mark *are* its state. A stop that fells a control mid-consult decides nothing, so the mark stands — but the window it settles on the way out makes that mark **stale** by §3.3's own rule (its invocation's result is committed), and the next drive's existing stale sweep clears it. No verb is added for any of this: `litany stop` already means "end the work in flight", and settlement is what makes that true rather than aspirational.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-b98d).** The settlement is `src/prompt/dispatch/tool_step/settle.rs`, reached from the **one** seam every stopped window passes through — `tool_step::run_tool_calls`'s three stop exits (a tool felled by the group SIGTERM, a control felled mid-consult, a multi-tool envelope felled mid-fan-out) — so both drivers get it from one place: `run_exchange`'s loop and every `litany advance` hop, including a held branch's resume. The answered ids are read back from the transcript (`transcript::committed_result_ids`), never a stored cursor, so results that committed before the stop keep the single entry they already have and re-entry is idempotent. Before this, a `litany stop` issued while an agent was inside a tool call wedged that agent permanently: the next `litany advance` returned `Error::UnpairedToolUse` and every subsequent deposit was stranded, recoverable only by fork-from-history. Pinned by `dispatch/tool_step/tests/settle.rs` (the felled block and the never-entered one are each settled once, the already-committed one is untouched, and the resulting tail composes tool-side — i.e. warrants a model call), `prompt/tests/stop_deposit/mod.rs::stop_during_tool_execution_deposits_stopped_and_skips_compaction` (end to end through `run_exchange`), and `prompt/tests/tool_control_resume.rs::a_stop_felling_the_resumes_control_is_the_stopped_terminal` (the stale-mark interaction). Since bl-4187 the same module carries the §6 crash settlement's sentence too — `settle::crashed`, reached from the drive boundary (`dispatch/advance/crash.rs`, pinned by `prompt/tests/advance_crash.rs`) — one settlement mechanism, two wordings.

The user-action stop (`litany stop <workspace> <agent>`) discovers the harness pid by the **executor lock**: it scans `/proc/<pid>/fd/*` for the process holding the agent's inbox-directory fd open — the `flock` home (§2.11), acquired at step-loop start and held for the whole loop — and signals that pid's process group. That fd is the *is-anyone-driving* signal by construction: held across tool execution, inbox drains, and retry backoff alike (§2.11), so discovery lands whenever an executor is alive — not only while a model call is in flight. (The earlier design scanned for the writer holding the latest step's `response.json` open; that fd is open only during a model call, so a stop issued during a long-running tool — exactly when it is most wanted — found no pid. Discovery is now the lock fd; `response.json`'s fd remains the narrower *which-model-call-pid-is-in-flight* signal, §3.5's `in_flight` sub-state.) There is no sidecar pid file — the open lock fd is the same kernel-lifetime observation the §2.11 lock probe already reads. **Every** driver runs in its own process group: `litany prompt` (root) `setpgid(0, 0)` at startup, and a deposit-launched driver — `litany advance`, including every dispatched child's — is detached into its own group at launch (`setsid`, §2.11). A root thereby escapes the invoking shell or UI process group, and a child executor takes its own group like a root does — so `kill(-pgid, SIGTERM)` reaches exactly one executor's provider adapter and tool subprocesses and no further. This retires the earlier rule — that dispatched child harnesses *not* setpgid, inheriting the parent's group so a parent's group signal would fell them — which was wrong for the non-hierarchical agent model: it made the kernel silently cascade a stop across the agent boundary, exactly the reach the agent-scoped default forbids. With children in their own groups, the collateral-kill hazard of a deposit-launched revival driver dissolving alongside its dying parent is gone too.

**Discovery trusts a pgid only from a group leader.** The pid is discovered before its group is known, and the two facts do not settle together: between the fork that creates a driver and the `setpgid`/`setsid` it runs at startup, `/proc/<pid>/stat` reports **the group the driver inherited from whoever spawned it** — an operator's shell job, a parent tool subprocess, a test runner. `kill(-pgid, SIGTERM)` against that reading fells the spawner's whole tree instead of one executor, and it is not a theoretical window: under parallel load the e2e stop tests SIGTERMed their own coverage run through it. The invariant that closes it needs nothing new on disk, because the settled state is already self-describing — **a group leader's pgid is its own pid**, and every driver makes itself one (above). So discovery accepts a pgid only when it equals the holder's pid, re-reads a **bounded number of times** while it does not (a retry *count*, not a wall-clock deadline: this race only appears under load, and a deadline measured under load reports the load), and then refuses outright rather than signalling a reading it cannot vouch for. Refusal is the right terminal: a `litany stop` that signals nothing is idempotent and re-runnable, while one that signals the wrong group is unrecoverable. The cascade then re-checks the same hazard from the other end — it **refuses any pgid the stopping process itself belongs to** (`getpgrp`), which no detached executor can legitimately be in, and refuses the whole sweep rather than filtering the one target, since a resolution that produced an impossible pgid has not established what its other targets are either.

**`litany stop --stop-children` walks the id namespace.** The opt-in agent→agent cascade is a CLI-level walk, not a kernel behavior. Descent is already encoded in the hyphenated agent id (§2.3), so the descendants of `<agent>` are exactly the inbox directories prefixed `<agent>-` — a single source of truth already on disk. The flag enumerates that prefix (one scan reaches every depth, because the flat id namespace *is* the tree — no recursion) and folds each descendant executor's pgid, discovered by the same lock-fd probe, into the one SIGTERM sweep. This walk is the shape the budget derivation reuses over the shared `steps/` tree (§6). A bare `litany stop` omits the walk and touches only the one agent's group.

A stopped agent deposits a result. A stopped child deposits its result message with a `stopped` epitaph (§2.6), performed by its executor on its way out (step 3) — the SIGTERM handler only sets an async-signal-safe flag; the deposit itself runs outside the handler, at the step loop's next check point — the deposit is **executor-side**, never a model tool call (the model has no return verb; termination is the return, §2.3 step 5, "Return is not a verb" in `docs/PRINCIPLES.md`). The parent is thereby revived (§2.11) and offered the manner of ending, not left to infer it from an absent message. A stopped root agent has no dispatcher to report to: its exchange ends without a terminal response (§2.4). An obituary is addressed by descent and not by the conversation (§2.6), so a child an operator had been speaking to still reports its stop to the agent that dispatched it. A child that crashed too hard to run its handler (SIGKILL, OOM, panic) deposits nothing itself; the §8 sweep deposits on its behalf, with a `died` epitaph. The branch remains as a ref for retention (§9.2), and a stop is not a locked door: the paths forward are the ordinary primitives — a message into the stopped agent's inbox starts a driver and resumes the same branch (§2.4, §2.11; for a stop landing in a tool window, the settlement above is what keeps that true — and a *crashed* window is settled the same way by the next drive itself, §6 bl-4187, so the same deposit revives a crashed agent too), or a fork off any of its commits starts a fresh agent (§2.3). There is no distinct "resume" operation — message-and-drive and fork-from-history subsume what resume would have done, and a third name would only obscure that.

Between-step lulls are not stops: the latest step's `response.json` ends with `end`, distinguishing a boundary from a terminated chain. Within one `litany advance` chain there is in fact no process lull at all — the successor is `exec`'d by the same process (§6), so the pid, the process group, and the lock fd persist across the hop and a stop aimed at the chain's group lands whichever hop is current. The residual gap is around *detached launches* (a writer's post-deposit spawn, an exiting executor's self-directed spawn, §2.11): there the deposit-before-probe ordering guarantees the work exists on disk before anyone checks, so during normal operation a chain has either an emitted terminal event or a live process — never neither.

Retention is an operator decision, exercised through §9.2's verbs (branch deletion plus object pruning); the harness runs no timer and ships no default expiry — a branch persists until someone deletes it. (Historical: this line named a 30-day default retention. Nothing ever implemented it, and a default with no mechanism is a claim a reader has to be disappointed by: the deletion is now a verb an operator or a frontend invokes, `litany delete`, §9.2.)

### 2.10 Retries and failures

**Read state per step.** Step 1 commits the dispatch artifacts (`goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name` per §2.3 step 2) on the new branch *before* its model call; that commit is step 1's read state. Step ≥2 takes no separate pre-call commit — the prior step's tip (advanced by its transcript entries, worktree side effects, and any delivery commits, §2.3, §2.11) is its read state. The branch tip at step-start is recorded in `meta.json`'s `commit` field (§2.3) so retry and replay are tractable: a failed model call is reissued by re-running the context assembler (§5) against the recorded sha and re-invoking the adapter, with no drift from the original wire input. The on-disk `request.json` is diagnostic, not authoritative.

- **Retryable provider errors** (transient network failure, 429 rate limit, 5xx) are retried inline with backoff, bounded by the attempt cap in `workflow.yaml` (§6). The backoff is that config schedule floored by the failed attempt's `Retry-After` pacing hint, when it carries one (§4.4). Retryability is classified by `CanonicalError::retryable()` from the linked brazen crate (§4.4) — computed from the in-band `Error` event, never re-derived by the harness. Each retry is a fresh attempt: a new `bz` invocation appending a new segment to `response.json` (§4.4). Retries do not produce additional commits — the commit frames the model call, not the individual attempt. The loop is bounded by the §2.9 stop flag as well as by the attempt cap: a stop pending where the loop would launch the next attempt — raised during the attempt, or during the backoff pause — ends the loop instead. A `bz` spawned *after* the group SIGTERM is outside the cascade's reach, so retrying through a stop would buy nothing and cost a whole further model call before the executor could settle the branch as stopped.
- **Non-retryable errors** (400 validation failure, auth failure, impossible-to-satisfy schema) abort the step. The branch is left in the state it held before the model call and flagged for operator attention. The flag is derived, never stored (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth): the step's `response.json` already ends in the `Error`-terminated segment (§4.4), and that framing tail is the silent-death derivation's evidence (§2.3, §8) — `litany scan` counts and **names** such a branch, root and child alike (a root has no parent inbox for a `died` deposit, so the name in the scan report is its whole surfacing). Messaging the branch stays legal and *is* the recovery path (§2.9's resume shape — deposit, driver, re-run against the unchanged read state — once the cause is fixed: config, credentials, a provider gap); the deposit is never declined and the exit code is untouched, but `litany message` prints a stderr advisory when the recipient is quiescent with a failed latest call, so the sender is told the branch was not merely idle.
- **Unknown or ambiguous failures** trigger a diagnostic dispatch: a child agent is dispatched off the branch's current commit with a goal describing the failure, access to the branch state and the raw error, and instructions to produce a recommended next action (retry, abort, modify config, escalate).

**Resumable pauses.** A `Finish{Pause}` (the linked brazen crate's `FinishReason::Pause`, Anthropic `pause_turn`) is neither a completion nor a failure: the provider stopped mid-work and expects the partial assistant content replayed back to continue (brazen `specs/anthropic-messages.md` §3.5, "resume by re-sending the assistant content as-is"). The harness absorbs it *within the same step*, not as a new step: on the `Pause` segment it re-invokes `bz` with the paused content appended — read from the writer's staging file (§2.3 *The transcript writer*), where a `Pause`-terminated segment's blocks accumulate; the writer reading its own sink, not a diagnostic record — landing a further attempt segment (§2.1) on the same `response.json` — the identical multi-segment shape as retry, differing only in the request delta (a retry re-sends the identical request; a Pause continuation appends the paused content). A *new* step is structurally impossible here: a step's read state is a commit (§2.3), but the paused partial content is never committed worktree data — it lives in the staging file and the diagnostic `response.json`, neither of which is a commit and neither of which context assembly may read (§2.3, §5.1) — so it has no read-state home, and only within-step continuation preserves the replay-from-recorded-commit invariant above. Each continuation is a billed attempt (§6, §8), and the loop is bounded by the step's attempt cap (§6) so a pathological pause loop terminates. brazen emits `Pause` *only* with provider server tools (brazen `specs/anthropic-messages.md` §3.5), which litany's stdio tool contract (§3.3) does not enable — so no v1 model call can pause; the absorption is specified here so classification (§3.5, §4.4) is total, and it activates whenever server tools are wired (a future milestone).

Tool failures follow the same shape at the tool-executor level, surfaced to the emitting agent as the tool's `tool_result` content. The agent decides whether to retry, ignore, or escalate — this is an ordinary agent decision, not a harness one.

### 2.11 Messages

A **message** is content addressed to an *existing* agent. Any sender may deposit one: the user, the agent's parent, one of its children, a sibling — or the agent itself. This generalizes the dispatch symmetry (§2.5): where dispatch makes the parent indistinguishable from a user *at spawn*, messaging makes **every sender indistinguishable from a user for the rest of the agent's life**. One primitive dissolves what would otherwise be five features — the user steering a running exchange, the user reprompting a finished one (§2.4), a parent steering a dispatched child, a child reporting upward to its still-running parent (the reminder/watchdog shape — and, as the result message, every return, §2.6), and the user dropping into any descendant of the tree. There is no "attach" operation to define: watching any branch is already the frontend contract (§3.5), and speaking to it is a message.

An agent is exactly two things — recoverable on-disk state, and at most one in-flight execution. A message is a write to the first that the second picks up at its own pace; and if there is no second, the write conjures one (below).

> **Shipped-state note.** The deposit half of the channel is shipped (bl-cb44): the executor lock (`flock` on the agent's `inbox/<agent-id>/` fd, `src/prompt/inbox/lock.rs`, acquired at the start of the shipped `litany prompt` step loop and released on exit), the create-only deposit (`src/prompt/inbox/deposit.rs`), the `litany message <workspace> <agent> <content>` verb, and the `message` built-in tool (`src/prompt/tool/builtin/message/`, sender from `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH`). The **step-boundary drain is shipped** (bl-1129, `src/prompt/dispatch/drain.rs`): the executor's step loop, before it captures each step's read state, commits any renamed-but-uncommitted stray in `messages/`, then moves each pending `inbox/<agent-id>/<sender>-<NNN>.md` into the worktree as `messages/<counterNNN>-<sender>.md` (a literal `rename(2)`, drawn on the branch's transcript counter inside the executor lock) and commits the move — the delivery commit — in a deterministic `(mtime, filename)` order. The initial user message now rides this same path: the root executor deposits it into the agent's own inbox and the step-1 drain delivers it (the front-door unification the §2.4 reprompt rule implies), so the §2.3 direct-commit stand-in is gone. The **result-message return path** is now shipped too (bl-4ce8, bl-9f53): the result-message deposit (`inbox::deposit_result`; its `deposit_child_result` parent-derivation wrapper is since deleted, bl-a96a §2.6), the epitaph deposits wired at the root's terminal events (final-response, budget-exhausted, and stop — the executor-side SIGTERM handler of §2.9 step 3, bl-9f53), and the delivery-time **work-product transfer** with its declined-transfer mark are all built (§2.6 Shipped-state note) — and, since bl-c33b, a dispatched child's `litany advance` reaches those epitaph deposits for real at its terminal event (a user-prompted root's stays a no-op — its reply addresses no inbox, §2.6); the stop handler's catch-deposit-and-exit-clean behavior around the root no-op is itself live and real-signal-tested (`tests/stop_cli.rs`). The spawn is now real (bl-4684): every launch seam — the verb's post-deposit probe, the exit protocol's self-directed launch and its recipient-side revival launch (below, bl-5846, bl-4a6c), and the `litany scan` flush — calls the one injected launcher, whose production implementation detach-spawns **`litany advance <workspace> <agent>`** (§6): `setsid` in the child (its own session and process group), stdin and stdout bound to null with stderr captured to `steps/<agent-id>/driver.log` (bl-55f9), `LITANY_LOCK_FD` scrubbed from its environment, fire-and-forget. A deposit into a quiescent agent therefore causes execution, not just a queued file. This section is the shipped contract.

**Deposit.** *Addressed to an existing agent* is enforced, not assumed: before writing anything, the verb requires the recipient id to be a single path component (§2.3 — `..`, a separator, or an absolute id is **declined, never sanitized**, because `Path::join` honours all three and would put the file outside the workspace) and requires an `agents/<agent-id>` ref to exist for it (the ref namespace is the registry, §2.3 — existence is a query, never a stored fact). A deposit into a nonexistent agent is refused loudly rather than left in an inbox directory no drain would ever come for: silent message loss is the failure this rule exists to prevent, and the deposit-before-branch case cannot arise, since `litany dispatch` forks the child branch *before* depositing its dispatch message (§2.5, Writer/driver totality below). The id guard is the same rule at every verb that takes an agent id from outside — `message`, `advance`, `stop`, `dispatch`, `bundle`, `delete` — since an id is a branch name, a worktree directory, and two namespace keys (§2.2). (The *existence* guard beside it is not universal: `delete` (§9.2) deliberately admits an absent id, because absence is exactly what it establishes.) `litany message <workspace> <agent> <content>` (§3.4) then writes the message to the recipient's **inbox** at `<workspace>/inbox/<agent-id>/<sender>-<NNN>.md` — at the workspace root, outside every worktree, namespaced by agent id exactly like `steps/` (§2.2). `<sender>` is the depositing agent's id, or `user`; `<NNN>` is the sender's own sequence. A deposit is a **create-only new file**, never an append or edit — an append is two messages — and writes are temp-path + atomic rename. This makes the inbox lock-free by construction: sender-namespacing makes cross-sender collision impossible (two senders never target the same file — the same construction as §2.5's write-path guarantee), a single sender is sequential with itself, and create-only means no reader can observe a half-written mutation. The file carries `from:` and `deposited_at:` frontmatter, and the division of labor is general: **the path carries exactly one fact — framing** (the sender in the deposit name; the wire role from the origin token after delivery, §2.3) — **every other fact a message asserts rides its frontmatter, and the body is the content.** The result message (§2.6) is the worked example: an ordinary deposit whose frontmatter adds `epitaph:` and `terminal_ref:`, whose body is the terminal response verbatim, and whose body is absent exactly when the agent never spoke — one file shape, no sidecar, no variant kinds; code branches on frontmatter values, never on shape. Frontmatter is delivered with the message and is model-visible, which is the point: the epitaph exists for the parent to reason about. Provenance is thus recorded even though the recipient treats every sender uniformly. That uniformity is by design, not an oversight — every sender is influence-equivalent for the recipient's whole life, so a hostile message is a lateral prompt-injection surface the primitive does not itself fence; the single-user assumption (§1.1) covers authorization but not influence, and defense is deferred and layered (§11), not a change to the deposit.

The model-facing side is the `message` built-in tool (`litany tool message`, input `{agent, content}`), which goes through the CLI per §3.4. The sender identity is taken from `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH` (§3.3) — harness-derived, never model-supplied, so an agent cannot forge provenance. Deposit is synchronous and returns `{status: deposited}`: it is not a dispatch, creates no branch, and returns no child address. Addressing needs no registry, because an agent's address *is* its id (§2.3): the children's addresses are the ids their dispatches returned (§2.5), the parent's address is the agent's own id minus its last descent segment, and the user reads addresses off the UI's branch view (§7.1).

**`message` also accepts a name, and refuses ambiguity rather than guessing.** An id is what the harness *prints*; a **name** (§2.1) is what an operator or a peer agent *speaks*, and a recipient addressed by the display name every surface uses must resolve to something rather than fail. So `message` — the verb and the built-in tool alike — resolves its `agent` argument as: an exact id match first; else the unique living agent wearing that name (`git show agents/<id>:name` over the `agents/*` refs, §2.3); else the needle unchanged, so the shared existence guard above speaks the one "no such agent" decline. A name worn by two living agents is a **loud refusal naming the candidate ids** — never a pick — and it is the only ambiguity that can arise, since names and ids are disjoint spaces by construction (§2.3). This still adds no registry: the reading is a query over the same ref namespace the id reading uses. **The other id-taking verbs do not resolve names, deliberately**: `advance` is a launch target on the driver hot path, `delete` (§9.2) admits an id no ref answers to by design, and `stop` / `bundle` / `dispatch`'s parent argument address an id the harness itself produced — none of them is a place a name is spoken. Adopting the reading at one of them later is one call to the same function, not a new mechanism.

**A deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver.** Delivery needs a step boundary and a step boundary needs an executor, so after depositing, `litany message` probes the executor lock (a non-blocking try-acquire; *succeeding* means no one is driving the branch). Finding the agent quiescent, it releases the probe and launches a driver — `litany advance` (§6) — rather than refusing: the driver acquires the lock, rematerializes the worktree if it was torn down (§2.3 step 6), drains the inbox, and steps. This is the reprompt path (§2.4): "messaging a quiescent agent" *is* how a root agent receives its next user message, so it must succeed and must cause execution. (The v0.3 rule was the loud inverse — a deposit with no live executor was refused, and the remedy was a fresh dispatch consuming the old branch "as context." The refusal inverted when reprompt collapsed onto the message primitive: the branch itself continues, so nothing needs to be declined and no new agent needs to exist.) The launch is race-free without coordination: if several deposits each launch a driver, or a driver was already starting, the lock admits one and the rest exit as clean no-ops (Writer/driver totality, below). Launching is not driving — the message verb never holds the lock past the probe; the driver it spawns competes for the acquire like any other.

**A release over unseen mail starts a driver: the release rule, the deposit rule's dual.** The deposit rule above covers mail arriving at a quiescent agent; the **release rule** covers quiescence arriving over pending mail — the same invariant (*mail pending + nobody driving ⇒ launch*) observed from the other end. The two must close jointly, because a writer's probe races the holder's release: a deposit can land *after* the holder's last inbox read yet *before* its release, so the writer reads Busy — "the holder drains at its next step boundary" — and defers to a holder that has no boundary left. Without the dual, nothing ever drives that deposit: undelivered mail, no lock holder, no driver, until a hand-run `litany scan` (the bl-9c8f lost wakeup — a proven production strand, not a hypothetical). So: **an executor that voluntarily releases its lease re-reads the inbox after releasing, and if any deposit is pending that its own last read under the lease never saw, it completes that deposit's launch — probe-and-launch at its own agent, the unmodified writer seam.** *Completes*, exactly: the rule replays the racing writer's own launch decision, no more — a plain message's writer (`litany message`, `litany dispatch`, the scan flush) always launches, and a result message launches by pin 2's one epitaph decision (below): `final-response` and `died` wake the recipient, `stopped` and `budget-exhausted` deliberately park it — so a deposit's fate never turns on which side of the release it landed. The two rules partition the timeline airtight: a writer whose probe succeeded launches (nobody held); a writer whose probe failed deposited before probing, and probed while the holder still held — so the holder's release, and therefore its post-release re-read, come strictly later and necessarily see the deposit. Three qualifiers carry the invariants. *After releasing*: the re-read runs with no lease held, so if a rival took the freed lock first, the releaser's probe reads Busy and defers exactly like any writer's — no double-drive, and the rival's own release re-reads when it comes. *Its own last read never saw*: the seen-set holds the (name, mtime) file identities of what the read deliberately left pending, never bare names — delivery and interpretation free a `<sender>-<NNN>` name for reuse (*Deposit* above derives `NNN` from the current listing), so a reused name is a new deposit and fires — while a gate-held result (§6) is untouched from enumeration to release, matches its identity, and fires nothing: a hold cannot relaunch-loop, every fired launch is funded by a strictly newer deposit, and delivering consumes it, so launching terminates. *Voluntarily*: an executor that errors out or dies releases by crash and re-reads nothing — the accepted crash class below, healed by the next touch.

**Delivery.** At each step boundary — after the prior step resolves, before the next model call's context is assembled — the executor drains the inbox: each pending message file moves into the branch's worktree as a transcript entry at `messages/<NNN>-<sender>.md` — `NNN` drawn from the branch's single transcript counter (§2.3), the same sequence the executor's own assistant output and tool results commit into — and the move is committed (the **delivery commit**). The move is a literal `rename(2)` — the message file has exactly one home at every instant, so delivery can neither duplicate nor lose it — and the crash window between rename and commit is closed at the other end: a drain *begins* by committing any renamed-but-uncommitted stray a prior death left in `messages/` (the file left the inbox, so it must land — the inverse of §2.3's "an entry that never committed never happened"). A result message (§2.6) additionally applies its work-product transfer as a commit immediately before its own delivery commit. Delivery is where ordering becomes authoritative: arrival order across senders in the inbox is advisory (mtimes), and the committed sequence is the record — the order question has one home, and it is the commit. From there, no new mechanism exists or is needed:

- **Context inclusion needs no manifest entry.** Delivered messages are transcript entries, and the transcript tail is the one part of assembly the manifest does not gate (§5.1, §5.2), so a delivered message composes into every subsequent model call at its sequence position (§2.3, §5.5). The path is the hint (§5.3): `messages/007-user.md` reads as provenance at a glance.
- **Replay is the read-state rule.** A step's read state is a commit (§2.3, §2.10). Because delivery is a commit landing ahead of the model call, replay from `meta.json`'s `commit` reproduces exactly what the step saw — a message cannot appear in a prompt without a commit that carries it.
- **Curation is deletion.** The agent may `rm` a stale message (§5.4); the compactor may `mark_for_deletion` delivered messages like any other worktree file — except what the dispatch wrote and never rewrote, which is not compaction-eligible (§2.7): the **dispatch entry**, which is the goal in transcript form, and the system slot's `goal.md` / `soul.md` / `name`.
- **Nothing crosses to the parent.** A child's delivered messages are transcript entries on the child's branch, and they stay there: no merge-back exists, and the work-product transfer excludes `messages/**` (§2.6).

Step-boundary delivery is not a compromise; it is the only possible seam. Mid-step injection is structurally impossible twice over: the provider wire requires every `tool_use` matched by a `tool_result` in the immediately following wire message (§2.5), and a mid-step message would have no read-state home (§2.3). A deposit therefore never interrupts in-flight work — interruption remains `litany stop` (§2.9), a different verb because it is a different act.

A parent that has dispatched a child and gone quiescent (§2.4) is this same rule at its limit: with no step in flight, the child's deposit — addressed here, since the dispatch message is the child's last prompt (§2.6) — *revives* it — the deposit starts a driver, the delivery commit lands at the fresh step's boundary, and the parent reacts ("A deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver," above). The parent never blocks on a child, so there is no distant-boundary case to reconcile: it is either stepping — draining at the next boundary — or quiescent — revived by the deposit. Waiting on a child is the same act as waiting on the user (§2.5); parking is parking.

**The executor lock: at most one step loop per branch.** Messaging makes concurrent invocations against one branch an ordinary event — a user and a child acting on the same agent at once — so the exclusivity that was previously implicit is a stated invariant: **at most one executor per branch**. The mechanism is `flock(2)` on the agent's inbox directory fd, acquired non-blocking when an executor starts and held for the whole step loop. (The inbox directory lives at the workspace root and persists across worktree teardown, §2.3 step 6, so the lock's home outlives the substrate's materialization.) The lock is kernel state bound to process lifetime: released by the kernel on any death, observable but never written — the same family as the fd-open `in_flight` signal (§2.9, §3.5, §4.4). Liveness is observed, not stored, and there is no stale-lock cleanup because there is nothing on disk to go stale (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Single source of truth"). **A voluntary release is an explicit `flock(fd, LOCK_UN)`, never a bare close.** The lock rides the open file description, so closing one fd naming it releases the lease only once every fd naming it is gone — and every subprocess spawn transiently makes more of them, since `fork`/`clone` copies the whole fd table and close-on-exec fires at `execve`, not at the fork. A lease released by close while any spawn anywhere in the process sits in that window stays kernel-held until that unrelated child execs, and the next probe reads *another executor drives this branch* — the one lie this signal must never tell, since it turns a driver into a silent no-op (Writer/driver totality, below) and a silently-dead agent into a live one (§8). `LOCK_UN` clears the lock from the description itself, so every copy of it loses the lease at once; release is therefore exact rather than "when the last duplicate happens to go away". Death is unaffected: the kernel drops the description with its last fd. The lock fd is deliberately inherited across the §6 exec baton (`litany advance` exec'ing the next `litany advance`), so the lease spans the chain exactly as long as the chain lives — inheritance that must be *worked for*, since fds are opened close-on-exec by default: the hop clears the flag on the lock fd immediately before exec and publishes the number as `LITANY_LOCK_FD`, and the successor adopts and fstat-validates it instead of reacquiring (mechanics pinned in §6, "The exec baton carries the lease"). Together with git's one-branch-one-worktree rule, the lock makes driver exclusivity total (§2.3 "Executor exclusivity is two existing rules").

The lock and the fd-open model-call signal (§2.9, §4.4) are **two observations of two different facts, deliberately not collapsed**: the lock answers *is anyone driving this branch* — held across tool execution, inbox drains, and retry backoff sleeps alike, and so it is the fd `litany stop` scans to find the pid to signal (§2.9) — while the open `response.json` answers *is a model call in flight right now* (§3.5's `in_flight` sub-state, and the narrower which-model-call-pid refinement). Widening either to serve the other would misclassify a tool-executing driver as absent or a between-calls driver as in a model call — the very reason stop discovers by the lock fd, not the model-call fd: a `response.json`-fd scan finds no driver mid-tool, exactly when a stop is wanted. What they share is the discipline, stated once here: kernel state bound to process lifetime, observed, never stored, nothing on disk to go stale.

**Writer/driver totality.** Every invocation against a branch is exactly one of two things, never both. A **writer** (`litany message`, and `litany dispatch` — which forks the child then deposits its dispatch message, §2.5) deposits and exits; its lock try-acquire is the liveness probe above — released immediately, never held to drive — and a writer never contends for execution at all: when the probe finds no driver, the writer *launches* one and exits, it does not become one. (`litany dispatch` probes and launches the *child's* branch, never its own; the fork it does first is a write to a fresh branch with no executor to contend with.) A **driver** (`litany prompt`, `litany advance`) acquires-or-exits: winning the acquire, it *is* the executor and runs the ordinary synchronous loop; losing it, it has nothing to deposit and exits as a clean no-op — not an error, because the branch being already driven is the condition it exists to establish. No abandonment, no takeover, no watcher left behind: the loser terminates, and any process that wants to *observe* the branch does so through the read-only frontend contract (§3.5), which is not a role the lock assigns. Because no verb combines the arms, the losing path is the same code as the uncontended one — a deposit is a deposit, a no-op is an exit — and there is no second-class branch of a combined flow to drift out of step. This closes a gap that predates messaging: §6's idempotence rule covers *sequential* replay of `litany advance`, and the executor lock is its concurrency half — two simultaneous drivers against one branch resolve to one executor and one no-op, never two step loops.

**Undelivered is derived.** A deposit can land in the gap between an executor's last inbox read and its lock release; every voluntary release closes that gap — each runs the release rule's re-read (above): the no-op driver's release via pin 1 below, and the terminal releases whatever the epitaph (the terminal-release paragraph below); a final-response exit *additionally* launches unconditionally, and that driver's under-lock drain is the authoritative re-read — and the writer's post-deposit probe covers the case where the executor is already gone. Both are actions against observed state, so the classification stays on disk rather than in anyone's memory: a message file still under `inbox/<agent-id>/` whose agent has no live executor is *undelivered* — a derived classification (inbox listing + the lock probe), never a flag. Under normal operation it is transient — the next driver, launched by any deposit or started by hand, delivers it. The sender observes its outcome the way anything is observed here: its file left the inbox in a delivery commit, or it didn't. Undelivered count is a §8 health metric alongside silent deaths.

**The exit protocol: the self-directed launch.** The channel above carries all of normal operation by itself: `litany message` deposits, probes, and launches; the executor drains at every step boundary; and deposit-before-probe ordering means a queued item exists before anyone checks — the healthy path needs zero scanning and zero inbox-watching. What it leaves open is the graceful-exit crack: a deposit landing *after* the executor's final drain but *before* its lock release meets a lock-held writer probe (nothing launched) **and** an executor that will never look again. The close is unconditional, not merely the conditional post-release re-read every voluntary release runs (the release rule above runs at this release too; the unconditional launch is the stronger close, since its driver's under-lock drain is the authoritative re-read): **one terminal sequence, no agent kinds** — reach a terminal event → deposit the result message at the address its epitaph names (§2.3 step 5, §2.6 *A reply answers the last prompter*; a structural no-op when that address is nobody — an operator-prompted reply, a parentless agent's obituary) → release own lock → spawn a driver at own agent, fire-and-forget → **probe-and-launch at the recipient the deposit just landed in** → exit. The only conditional in the sequence is "is there a recipient," and it is one derivation (§2.6) — the same answer governs the deposit and the recipient-side launch, because they are one act. Addressing them separately is exactly the bug that would strand a reply in an inbox nothing was launched at.

That last step is not a second protocol: it is the *deposit's* own launch, the plain "a deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver" rule (above) applied to the one deposit an executor makes on its way out — the exiting executor is a writer at its recipient, so it probes and launches there exactly as `litany message` does (Writer/driver totality: probe, release, launch, never drive). It is the mechanism behind revival-on-deposit (§2.5, and the quiescent-parent limit case above); without it a child's return would sit in a torn-down recipient's inbox until something else touched the workspace, which is the scanning the healthy path is supposed not to need. It is placed *after* the release rather than at the deposit for the same no-authority reason the self-directed launch is: from the release onward the exiting process only spawns and exits, so a revived recipient that immediately messages or stops this agent meets no lingering lease. **Pin 2 below governs both launches**: the epitaph decides once, at own agent and at the recipient alike.

The governing symmetry: **no launcher ever decides whether launching is warranted; warrant is decided by the launched driver under the lock.** Writers already work this way (Writer/driver totality, above); the exit launch is the same act with the sender's own agent as target — same act, same name, no new noun. Invariant: **after release, the exiting process has no authority** — spawn and exit are its only remaining acts. This dissolves the dual-processing reverse race: from the acquire onward, the new lock-holder is the sole writer, and the exiting process can no longer touch what it handed off. Two pins hold the sequence:

1. **The no-op driver path.** A driver that acquires and finds nothing to deliver on a terminal branch exits silently — no step, no epitaph — and its own lease release honours the release rule (above): the exit launches nothing *only* because the post-release re-read confirms no deposit raced its last look; unseen mail found there fires the racing deposit's own launch (per its warrant — the replay above), and the launched driver's drain consumes it. This still structurally terminates the recursion: found-mail → step to a new terminal → exit-launch again (the normal lifecycle); found-nothing-and-nothing-raced → nothing relaunches — every launch is funded by a strictly newer deposit, never free-running. (Before the release rule this pin was the proven strand path, bl-9c8f: the no-op driver's last read → a writer's deposit → the writer's Busy probe → the no-op release, and the deposit waited for a hand-run `litany scan`.) The same path is what makes racy launches free (needed anyway): N deposits may each launch a driver, one wins the acquire, the rest exit as clean no-ops.
2. **Exit-launch by epitaph value** (§2.6: code branches on the epitaph's value, never on message shape) — the epitaph-*funded* launches, the ones termination alone would pay for; a launch funded by a racing deposit is the release rule's, decided separately (the terminal-release paragraph below): *final response* → launch; *stopped* → never — a relaunch funded by nothing new would resurrect the branch the operator just killed; mail the final drain saw and left waits for the next explicit touch, a deposit that raced the release is completed per its own warrant by the release rule (below), and future deposits still revive the branch per §2.9; *budget-exhausted* → never — drain → exhaust → deposit is an epitaph-spam cycle against a hard ceiling. **One decision, both targets.** The epitaph governs the recipient-side launch on the same terms, and for the same reasons read from the recipient's side: a *final response* is an answer to whoever asked, and the asker is exactly who should wake for it; a *stopped* child would hand its dispatcher a kill to react to — plausibly by re-dispatching — undoing the operator's stop one level up; and a *budget-exhausted* child's ceiling is the whole tree's (§6), so the revived dispatcher would exhaust on its own next check and deposit again — the same spam cycle, climbing. In every "never" case the deposit still lands and stays undelivered (derived, above): the next explicit touch delivers it.

**Terminal releases run the same rule, whatever the releaser's epitaph — pin 2 governs a different launch.** A terminal event releases a lease too, and a deposit can race that release exactly as it races the no-op driver's, so the terminal tail funnels through the same release-then-re-read helper: one release path, never an epitaph-conditioned copy. The interaction with pin 2 is decided, not implied: **the release-side re-read completes a racing deposit's launch regardless of how the releaser ended — the launch is decided by the racing deposit's own warrant (the replay above), never by the releaser's epitaph.** The two pins never conflict, because they fund different launches: pin 2 forbids relaunching with *nothing new* — its "never" arms deny the epitaph-funded launches, the self-directed spawn and the parent revival that termination alone would pay for — while the release rule's launch is funded by a strictly newer deposit, which is new work by definition. Per the releaser's epitaph, argued: a *final response* launches unconditionally anyway, and that driver's under-lock drain is a strictly stronger re-read than any lockless listing, so the re-read there costs at most one extra spawn the lock arbitrates away (racy launches are free, above). For a *stopped* releaser, a racing steering message launches — deliberately: §2.9 rules that a stop is not a locked door and a message into the stopped agent's inbox starts a driver and resumes the same branch, §2.10 makes messaging a failed branch *the* recovery path, and a deposit that raced the final step would have launched its own driver had it landed a millisecond later, when its writer's probe found the lease free. Making the deposit's fate turn on that millisecond is a race its sender can neither observe nor influence, and it protects nothing: the very next deposit revives the branch anyway — a stop was never a promise against future mail, only against relaunch with nothing new. Mail the stopped executor's last drain *saw* and deliberately left still waits (the seen-set, above); only a racing deposit fires. For a *budget-exhausted* releaser, the same rule, and termination still holds: the launched driver delivers the racing mail and the branch runs back into the same ceiling — at worst one further exhausted deposit into the parent, which pin 2 still refuses to wake for — so nothing cycles: every release-rule launch is funded by a strictly newer deposit into the releaser's *own* inbox, delivery consumes it, and the exhausted result lands in the *parent's*. The epitaph-spam cycle pin 2 kills was funded by the ending itself; the release rule funds nothing from an ending. The warrant replay closes the other direction with the same symmetry: a racing *result* deposit is completed exactly as its own writer would have — a `stopped` child's report racing its parent's release parks in that inbox on either side of the release (the pin-2 parked state, "stays undelivered ... the next explicit touch"), because the child's exit protocol would not have launched for it either; launching there would erase the parked state pin 2 specifies and hand a just-stopped tree a wake it refused.

The cost is one no-op spawn per normal termination — termination frequency, not step frequency — accepted at current scale and optimized only if measurement shows it. **The spawn is detached, and detachment is the pinned contract, not an implementation nicety**: `setsid` in the child (its own session and its own process group), stdin and stdout bound to null (a driver reads nothing, and by the §3.4 one-product convention it says nothing on stdout) with **stderr captured** to `steps/<agent-id>/driver.log` (*A detached driver's stderr is captured, not discarded*, below), `LITANY_LOCK_FD` scrubbed from its environment (a launched driver acquires; only an exec'd successor adopts, §6), and fire-and-forget (the launcher never waits on the child — an unreaped driver reparents to init when its short-lived launcher exits). **The driver target is injected at the binding, not resolved by name** (§3.4): the launcher spawns an executable handed to it at construction — the `litany` binary injects its own image path, a linked host injects its own re-exec target (it carries the driver loop) or a PATH-resolved `litany` — so a linked litany never hard-depends on a `litany` binary being on PATH mid-loop. Detachment is what makes launching scriptable rather than flaky-under-scripting: a driver spawned from inside another agent's tool subprocess, a user's shell script, or a dying executor outlives its launcher, and a §2.9 stop cascade against the launching process's group never reaches the driver it launched. A spawn failure falls into the accepted crash class below. inotify is a correctness mechanism nowhere in this: holding an inbox fd confers no notifications and inotify queues overflow; it may serve as a mid-loop latency optimization only (the same advisory status as §3.1's frontend watching). The authoritative read is the drain's readdir under the lock.

**A detached driver's stderr is captured, not discarded (bl-55f9).** The three stdio fds are not one policy. A driver reads nothing, and by the §3.4 one-product convention it writes no product to stdout, so both are null. **Stderr is different in kind**: it is where every driver states what it *declined* — a compaction landing declined or superseded (§2.6), a launch that failed into the accepted crash class (above), a §6 budget stop — and those lines are addressed to an operator. A `setsid` child has no terminal to inherit and its launcher is short-lived by design (a tool subprocess, a dying executor, a shell one-liner), so binding stderr to null did not "keep the driver quiet": it made the harness announce a refusal into nothing, which is the silent degradation `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` (*Decline illegal operations*) forbids. Capture is therefore the contract, at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/driver.log`, opened append-create by the launcher and inherited by the child — and by every §6 exec successor after it, since the fd rides the baton like the pid does. Three things make this not a config knob: the path is **derived** from the workspace and agent id every launch already carries (no setting, no flag, nothing to configure — `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth); the destination is the **existing** diagnostic tree the same driver's step records land in (§2.3), so the driver's diagnostics have one home rather than two; and it is the same *capture, don't discard* rule §4.4 already applies to the adapter subprocess's stderr (`steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/stderr.log`), read one level up because a driver spans the steps it takes and a launch may take none. It stays **diagnostic-only** under the §2.3 contract — appended, never read back, no runtime behaviour turns on it, and a `driver.log` sitting beside the numbered step directories is skipped by the step derivations that parse those names as integers. A sink that cannot be opened **declines the launch** rather than falling back to null: a workspace whose `steps/` tree is unwritable is one the driver could not have recorded a step in either, the caller already treats a failed launch as the accepted crash class, and the refusal surfaces on the launcher's own stderr instead of vanishing the way the lines it was protecting did. (This supersedes the earlier claim that "a log location would be config the null default doesn't need" — the null default needed one; the location is derivation, not config.)

**Every operator notice on that stream carries the prefix `litany: notice: ` (bl-9495).** Capture put the driver's declines somewhere; the prefix makes them readable by the *program* that captured them. Two populations share the stream and nothing else separates them: the Ok-path lines above — a compaction landing declined or superseded (§2.6), a budget stop (§6), a launch that fell into the accepted crash class (§2.11), a retarget decline (§2.2), a crashed tool window settled on adoption (§6), a procedure dispatch the budget refused (§2.7), a `setpgid` that did not take (§2.9) — and whatever a dying process writes on its way out. A consumer reading `driver.log` could only tell them apart by matching the prose, which is brittle across releases in exactly the way this document's wording is meant to be free. **The prefix is the whole contract, and it is deliberately narrow: a line beginning `litany: notice: ` states something the harness declined, or accepted and stepped past; the process continues and the exit code is untouched.** The sentence after it stays free prose and promises nothing, which is what keeps the contract cheap to honor. A decline that *is* the outcome — a refused verb, a fatal error — exits non-zero and is not a notice; that is the population the prefix exists to be distinguished from. Neither is a verb's own confirmation to the operator who just typed it (`litany retarget`'s mark, `litany prime`'s founding report above, `litany message`'s failed-branch advisory, §2.11): it is spoken to somebody present, it never reaches a captured driver sink, and marking it *not a failure* would answer a question its reader never asked. The prefix has one home in the code (`src/prompt/notice.rs`) and one emitter (the `notice!` macro it exports), so a site cannot misspell it or forget it.

**Crashes are a failure class, not a scan trigger.** Uncatchable death (SIGKILL, OOM, panic, reboot) strands what it strands: a dead child's undeposited result parks its parent until the next touch, and messages deposited while a doomed executor lived wait as undelivered. Everything is on disk, so nothing is lost, only late — and the next touch heals it. That touch is a user reprompt anywhere (the deposit starts a driver, above), or the operator verb **`litany scan`**: one workspace-wide pass with two derived actions. First, the **silent-death sweep** (§8): for each hard-crashed child in the candidate set — no live executor, died mid-work or never deposited — it deposits the `died`-epitaph result message on the child's behalf (§2.6, §2.9). Second, the **inbox flush**: it lists `inbox/*/*`, and every agent with pending files and a free lock gets a driver launched for it — *launched, never drained*: the scanner moves no files and commits nothing; only an agent's own lock-holding executor delivers (Writer/driver totality, above), and an agent whose lock is held is left alone — its executor drains at its next boundary. The listing is **intersected with the `agents/*` refs** (the enumeration seam again): an inbox directory whose name carries no ref names no agent, so there is nothing to drive — a driver launched for it is refused by the §2.3 existence guard (`litany advance: no agent "…"`, exit 1) on this pass and on every pass after, writing nothing. The pass counts it as debris an operator may delete and otherwise leaves it in place; the scanner deletes nothing, exactly as it moves nothing. The sweep's own deposits are picked up by the flush that follows in the same pass. `litany scan` is an **operator tool** — run by hand, or by cron if an operator wants a heartbeat (config, not schema) — and is never wired into any hot path or default schedule: the events it compensates for happen at crash rate, not step rate, and wiring it into driver startup would both pay O(branches) per spawn and hand the worst failure modes an accelerant — an automatic sweep relaunching the runaway agent an operator is trying to kill. A fully idle workspace therefore stays unswept until its next touch, and that is accepted rather than patched: with nothing running, nobody is blocked on the outcome. The stalled-parent story needs no new verb: reprompting a parked parent starts a driver whose drain delivers whatever is pending, and a hand-run `litany scan` deposits a dead child's epitaph and launches the drivers to deliver it.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-d148, bl-5846, bl-4a6c).** The scan is shipped (`src/prompt/inbox/scan/`) behind the operator verb **`litany scan <workspace>`** — and *only* there: no driver startup runs it (`litany prompt` drives only its own branch; `litany dispatch` touches only the one child it forks). The sweep's candidate derivation is built in full: the executor-lock probe, the died-mid-work classification (the framing-tail `response.json` read via the §4.4 `segment::classify`, framing-only), the sender-namespaced never-deposited derivation (parent inbox *and* transcript), and the `died`-epitaph deposit-as-scribe; idempotence holds because the never-deposited test sees the sweep's own prior deposit. The **exit protocol is shipped** at both drivers' terminal seams (`dispatch::run_exchange` and the `litany advance` hop, through `dispatch::terminal`): deposit (§2.6 note) → explicit lock release → epitaph-valued launch decision (final response launches; stopped and budget-exhausted never; an executor that errors out deposits nothing and launches nothing — the accepted crash class) → the same decision's **recipient-side launch**, `terminal::revive_recipient` (bl-4a6c; renamed and re-pointed by bl-a96a, §2.6): the address the deposit itself derived (`dispatch::result_deposit::recipient`), handed to the *unmodified* writer seam `inbox::probe_and_launch` — one probe implementation for `litany message`, `litany dispatch`, and the terminal return alike, so a recipient whose lease is held is left to its own next drain and a quiescent one is woken by a real detached `litany advance`. Before it, a child's return sat in the parent's inbox until a hand-run `litany scan`, which the zero-scanning claim above forbids. The launched driver's own-branch entry — acquire-or-exit, deliver pending mail, or exit silently on an empty inbox (pin 1) — is `dispatch::driver::drive`, the seam `litany advance` (§6) runs on arrival. The **release rule is shipped** at every voluntary release (bl-9c8f, `dispatch::driver::release_then_reprobe`): the drain returns the (name, mtime) identities of what it deliberately left pending — the executor's last accounted inbox read under the lease — and every release funnels through the one release-then-re-read helper: both `NothingToDo` releases in `dispatch::advance::run` directly, and both terminal tails through the shared `dispatch::terminal::conclude` (`run_exchange`'s tail and the hop's Terminal arm — one sequence, whatever the epitaph); the helper's launch rides the unmodified writer seam `inbox::probe_and_launch`, gated by the racing deposit's own warrant (`driver::deposit_warrants_launch` — plain mail always, a result by its epitaph value). Every launch seam — the writer's post-deposit probe, the flush, and the exit launch — calls the one injected launcher, whose production implementation now really detach-spawns `litany advance <workspace> <agent>` per the contract above (bl-4684): the flush and the probe cause execution, not just queued files. One note honoring shipped reality: children now run (bl-c33b, §2.5), so a real died-child *can* arise from a run — but reproducing a hard crash (SIGKILL/OOM/panic mid-run) deterministically is impractical, so the sweep's `died` derivation is still exercised against constructed on-disk states, which is also its honest unit. (The former ref-namespace divergence is gone: the workspace substrate landed `agents/*` refs, and the sweep enumerates that prefix exactly as §8 writes it.)

**Self-messages are legal and unremarkable.** An agent may deposit into its own inbox — a note its own next step boundary delivers. No special case arises; the sender happens to equal the recipient. (Distinct from the executor's own step output, which is not a message — not addressed to anyone — and takes no inbox hop: it delivery-commits directly into the same sequence, §2.3 "Self-output commits directly.")

---

## 3. Component Architecture

### 3.1 Disk as Bus

All components communicate through the filesystem. No shared memory, no direct function calls between components. This applies to:

- Harness → provider adapter (request mirrored to disk, adapter reads from stdin; response events mirrored back to disk from adapter stdout — see §4.4).
- Harness → tool execution (tool call record written to disk, executor reads, output streamed to disk).
- Harness → UI: the filesystem is the event stream. The UI watches paths in the workspace (git refs — config and agent branches alike; worktree contents `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, `summary/`, `messages/`, `descriptions/`, `skills/`; the workspace-root step records under `steps/<agent-id>/NNN/` per §2.2; the workspace-root inboxes under `inbox/<agent-id>/` per §2.11) and re-renders on change. Notification is inotify where available, polling otherwise.
- UI → Harness: user actions are issued as `litany <subcommand>` invocations per §3.4. There is no input directory.

**Processes, not threads.** There is no single resident harness process, and "worker" and "executor" are not threads sharing its memory. Execution is carried by **transient drivers** (§2.11): a driver — `litany prompt` or `litany advance` — is a short-lived process that acquires a branch's executor lock, drives its step loop, and exits, at most one per branch. (`litany dispatch` is a *writer*, not a driver — it forks a child and deposits, §2.5.) Concurrency across branches is concurrency across *separate processes*, and the §6 exec baton hands the workflow chain from one process to the next rather than parking it in a resident interpreter. The disk contract is therefore a genuine inter-process bus: the per-branch drivers, the tool subprocesses they invoke, and the provider-adapter subprocesses (§4.4) share nothing but the filesystem. (A single driver may still use OS threads internally — to supervise concurrent tool subprocesses within one step, say — but that is an implementation detail below the disk contract, not how roles or branches relate.) Routing every inter-role edge through disk — never shared memory, never a direct call — is load-bearing rather than ceremonial: it is what buys inspectability, the audit trail, and the single-author-per-file discipline (§2.5) that keeps concurrent drivers from corrupting each other's state. No process is resident and none is load-bearing; liveness rides on causation — a deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver (§2.11), and an exiting executor launches one at itself when work remains — and disk is the only thing that persists across a driver's exit. That the exec baton rebuilds the process image at every hop rather than looping in place is what makes this statelessness **structural rather than disciplinary**: memory cannot smuggle state across a step boundary because the address space is destroyed and remade, so a fresh image and a continuing one are the same code reading the same disk — the argument, and its cost accounting, live in §6 ("The image reload is the point, not the price"; `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Structure over discipline").

Notification mechanism: inotify where available, with a polling fallback. Every event write updates a `last_event_ts` sentinel file as a sanity check for consumers to detect missed events.

Consequences:

- Every component is independently restartable (drivers and tool/adapter subprocesses alike are relaunched from disk state).
- Replay from any point is `git checkout <ref>` + re-tail the event log.
- Latency is higher than in-memory IPC. Accepted and compensated by streaming-first UI design.

### 3.2 Components

- **Harness.** The `litany` code that drives execution — forks branches, runs model calls via the provider adapter layer (§4.4), invokes the tool executor, triggers compactions and lands their merges (§2.6), updates state. It runs as **transient drivers**, never a resident program: a driver (`litany prompt`, `litany advance`; §2.11, §3.1) acquires a branch's executor lock, drives its step loop, and exits — it does not watch for events and does not outlive its work.
