(`litany dispatch` is writer-shaped — fork plus deposit — not a driver, §2.5.) There is **no daemon and no resident watcher**: liveness rides on causation (a deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver, §2.11; an exiting executor launches one at itself; the §6 exec baton carries the workflow chain forward), so **"daemon" is a rejected framing, not a synonym** for this role. While a driver runs it owns all external↔filesystem interaction on the repo — provider endpoints (via adapter subprocesses), tool subprocesses, git operations — and it holds no state across its exit: every driver resumes from disk. Any place this document says "the harness does X", it is this role.
- **Tool executor.** Runs tool subprocesses on behalf of the harness; contract in §3.3. Per tool call: assembles stdin from the `tool_use.input` the model emitted; invokes the tool binary (`litany tool <name>` in-process or `litany-tool-<name>` external); captures stdout and stderr atomically (temp path + rename) into the diagnostic `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/tools/<tool-id>/output.json` (out of every worktree, §2.2, §2.3); derives the canonical `tool_result` block (the §3.3 *result envelope* as content — exit code stated, stdout, then stderr under its marker whenever the child wrote any, each stream first bounded per the §3.3 *bounded transcript projection* — and `is_error` from the exit code) and commits it as a transcript entry `messages/NNN-tool.json` on the emitting branch (§2.3, §3.3), from which the next step's request payload composes. Cascades SIGTERM on cancel (§2.9) with a 5s deadline before SIGKILL (§3.3). Termination by a signal other than the harness's own SIGTERM (SIGSEGV, SIGABRT, etc.) is a harness-level fault per §2.10. Does not auto-dispatch on oversized tool output in v0.3 (§11).
- **Provider adapter.** External binary — brazen's `bz`, one binary for every provider — that owns HTTP, wire dialects, and auth. Invoked per attempt over stdio; non-resident (process per attempt, no long-lived state). Transient-error retry belongs to the harness (§2.10), not the adapter. Contract in §4.4.
- **UI** (permitted synonym: **frontend**). A stateless renderer over the workspace. Reads and watches filesystem paths in the repo; issues user actions exclusively as `litany <subcommand>` invocations per §3.4. Holds no persistent state — every render is a pure function of filesystem state at the current git ref. The UI is pluggable: multiple frontends (desktop GUI, webclient, TUI) may run concurrently against one repo without coordination, because they share nothing but the filesystem and the CLI. Contract in §3.5. "Frontend" is allowed as a shorthand; both refer to the same role.

The harness, the tool executor, and provider adapters share the same disk contract. None share memory. The UI participates in the same disk contract as a read-only consumer.

### 3.3 Tools and skills

Tools and skills are separate primitives. Every tool has a skill; skills can exist without tools.

**Skill.** A directory containing a `SKILL.md` with YAML frontmatter (`name`, `description`) plus markdown instructions, optionally with bundled scripts and reference files. Skill source directories live globally at `<data-root>/skills/<name>/` and are referenced from the workspace by two mechanisms:

- **Description-always.** Every available skill's `SKILL.md` frontmatter (name + description) is committed in the config under `descriptions/skills/` (§2.2), where it is inherited by every agent branch via git and composed into the context of every role whose manifest entry selects it, on every model call (§5.1 — the shipped `worker` pins `descriptions/**`; the `compactor` pins neither, §5.2). This is the Anthropic progressive-disclosure convention (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §4). **Two wire homes, one per kind of skill, split by whether a tool claims the entry** (a `descriptions/tools/<name>.json` committed beside it): a **tool's** skill composes as the `description` field of that tool's `tools:` entry (below, point 3), selected by the role's `tools:` list; a **standalone** skill's — one no tool claims — has no such home and composes as a path-framed head text block (§5.2, §5.3), so the agent can see it and elect `load_skill`. The split is a query on the branch's own tree, so assembly stays a pure function of it (§5.1), and each entry composes at most once — never both as a tool description and as text.
- **Body-on-demand.** When an agent elects to use a skill, the harness copies the skill directory into the current branch's worktree at `skills/<name>/`. From that point on, the skill body is part of the agent's context — for a role whose `order` names `skills/**`, which the shipped `worker` does (§5.1, §5.2). The compactor may `mark_for_deletion` a skill directory when it is no longer needed — next context assembly sees the branch without it.

Copying (not symlinking) is deliberate. It is the same portability discipline as the rest of the repo (§2.2): a skill that lives in the worktree is self-contained and survives the global skill directory changing or disappearing. Disk cost is trivial.

**Election is a tool call: the `load_skill` built-in.** The Body-on-demand trigger is the in-process built-in `litany tool load_skill`, input `{name}`. The election being a tool call is what makes it structural rather than conventional: the copy lands as that tool call's worktree side effect under the ordinary commit-per-side-effect discipline (below), so the load is a transcript entry plus a commit — auditable, replayable from the read-state commit like any other context change, and requiring no new channel. The tool copies `<data-root>/skills/<name>/` into the branch worktree at `skills/<name>/` and returns a `{status, path}` JSON object as its `tool_result` content — `status: loaded` on a fresh copy, `status: already_loaded` on an idempotent no-op, `path` the worktree-relative `skills/<name>`. A `{name}` with no directory in the data-root pool is declined — `is_error` with the available pool named — never fuzzy-matched; a `name` that is not a single path component (a slash, a `..`) is likewise declined, never sanitized. A skill already present in the worktree is reported already-loaded and the worktree copy is left untouched, even if the data-root pool has changed since: the loaded copy is the snapshot the branch is pinned to (the same snapshot-not-mirror discipline as `descriptions/**`); picking up a newer pool version is `rm` then reload, an ordinary curation act. The load inserts into the assembled body (§5.2 `skills/**`) and pays the cache flush from its position (§5.5) — the tool's own `SKILL.md` says to load early. An agent whose role omits `load_skill` from its `tools:` list (§3.3 Tools-list assembly) simply cannot elect skills; description-always composition is unaffected.

> **Shipped-state note.** Body-on-demand is shipped (bl-4af9). The built-in is `src/prompt/tool/builtin/load_skill/`, routed by the `builtin::run` dispatcher and wired at `litany tool load_skill`; its input schema (`schemas/tools/load_skill.json`) and `SKILL.md` (`skills/load_skill/`) install into the data-root pools like every other tool triple (§3.3 *Tool*), so a config authored after install advertises `load_skill` in `descriptions/**` and any role listing it in `tools:` can elect. The copy is committed by the ordinary tool-result commit: **a tool commit now stages the whole worktree** (`git add -A`, `src/prompt/dispatch/transcript.rs::commit_tool`), realizing §2.3's "each tool call … commits its result — and any worktree side effect — as it lands" for *every* tool, not skills alone — `steps/` and `inbox/` sit outside every worktree (§2.2), so `-A` captures exactly the tool's worktree footprint. The data root resolves from the same `LITANY_HOME` / XDG env the harness reads (`src/harness_root.rs`); `LITANY_CONV_REPO` / `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH` (§3.3) give the target worktree. The compactor's `mark_for_deletion` over a loaded `skills/<name>/` (above) is the existing compactor toolset (§2.7), unchanged by this work.

A standalone skill (no associated tool) exists to give an agent capability via prompt — recipes, conventions, workflows — without a callable binary. *Shipped-state note (bl-ae66):* the head-block half of Description-always is `src/prompt/dispatch/assembler/body.rs` — the §5.2 walk skips `descriptions/tools/**` and every tool-claimed skill description (`tool_backed`), leaving the standalone remainder to compose through the role's ordinary `pinned` patterns. The tools-array half is unchanged (`src/prompt/dispatch/tools.rs`).

**Tool.** Composed of three required artifacts:

1. **Binary.** An executable invoked by the harness. **In-process** tools are subcommands of the `litany` binary, addressed as `litany tool <name>` — the default for tools shipped with the harness (v0.3 ships `bash` and `read_file` here). **External** tools are standalone binaries named `litany-tool-<name>` — the externalization pattern that lets contributors ship tools without patching the core (the provider layer used the same per-name pattern until v0.6 retired it for brazen, §4.4). The harness looks up `litany-tool-<name>` at `<data-root>/tools/` (installed by `make install`) before falling back to `PATH`. The choice of flavor is per-tool; the stdio contract below is identical.
2. **JSON schema.** Declares the tool's `input` parameters at `<data-root>/tools/<name>.json`. Required by provider APIs. Either generated from the binary's metadata or hand-authored. Sent verbatim as the schema of the tool's entry in the canonical request's `tools: [...]` array (a brazen `Tool::Custom` — each protocol projects it into its own spelling, §4.4). A copy is committed under `descriptions/tools/` in the config (§2.2), inherited by every agent branch via git, and composed into the context.
3. **Skill.** A `SKILL.md` describing when and how the tool should be used. Required for every tool; follows the skill lifecycle above. The frontmatter `description` becomes the `description` field of the tool's entry in the `tools: [...]` array.

> **A tool definition is the only frame litany ships (bl-298c).** Points 2 and 3 are the *whole* of what a model is told about a tool: litany injects no environment preamble, because §5.1 admits no second input to assembly — everything on the wire is selected from the branch's own worktree, and goal, soul and transcript are all operator- or agent-authored. Codex, the harness gpt-5.x models are tuned against, can afford a bare description ("Runs a shell command and returns its output.") precisely because it injects the frame separately, as an `<environment_context>` block (cwd, shell, date, timezone) plus a sandbox-mode developer message. litany has no such channel, so anything a model must know about a tool's *environment* belongs in the definition or nowhere. Found live: a gpt-5.x agent read `bash`'s older description — "Run a shell command via `sh -c` and return its stdout" — as a **remote interactive** shell, told the operator that "the shell available to me would only show the server's IP, not yours," and after running the command anyway still hedged that the result might "belong to the tool's execution environment." Every hedge named a fact the definition had left open. The shipped `bash` definition now asserts four: **local** (same machine as the harness process; no server, container, or sandbox in between), **non-interactive** (one `sh -c` per tool call, stdin `/dev/null`, no TTY, no prompt to answer), **rooted in the agent's current working directory** (*Working directory*, below — its worktree until the agent moves with `cd`, and only worktree writes are committed), and **stateless between tool calls** (shell state dies with the process; worktree files persist and are committed). The four are pinned by `src/install/tests/toolspec.rs`. The *schema* is deliberately unchanged: codex's current `shell_command` also takes `command` as a single string — the argv-array form belonged to its retired `shell` tool — and its `workdir` / `timeout_ms` have no litany meaning, since the cwd is one mutable fact about the agent rather than a per-tool-call parameter (*The working directory is one mutable per-agent fact*, below) and the executor imposes no wall-clock limit (only the §2.9 cancel cascade). Matching a tuned shape is worth doing where the shape differs; here it did not.

**Tools-list assembly.** A role's enabled tools are declared in the config's `providers.yaml` `roles:` section under a `tools: [...]` field (§2.2, §4.3). Any on-disk triple (binary + schema + skill) is generally discoverable; role configs select from that pool. At request-assembly time the harness composes the declared names against the schemas committed in the branch's read-state tree (§2.10): each declared tool whose `descriptions/tools/<name>.json` schema is present becomes one entry in the canonical request's `tools: [...]` array, with that file sent verbatim as `input_schema`; a declared name with no committed schema is dropped (the intersection of declaration and availability), and a present-but-malformed schema is declined rather than dropped. **Shipped-state note:** both halves are wired — the consumer (this assembly) shipped with bl-9e96 and the descriptions-always producer with bl-3092; the producer now lives where §2.2 homes it, as a step of first config-commit authoring (`litany new`, `src/template`), snapshotting the data-root pools into the config commit's `descriptions/**`. A role declaring an available tool therefore composes a populated `tools: [...]` entry; an emptied data-root pool still yields an empty `descriptions/**` and an empty array, unchanged — the composer's behavior, not a reachable state of a fresh install: `litany new` founds the pools before it snapshots them (§2.2 *Founding is a step of `litany new`*), so an unseeded root cannot silently author a toolless config. An entry's `input_schema` (from `descriptions/tools/`) and its `description` (point 3, the tool's own `SKILL.md` frontmatter via `descriptions/skills/`) compose in one pass; a tool whose schema is present but whose skill frontmatter is absent composes with no top-level `description` rather than being dropped.

**The array is closed over the history it ships.** Election answers "what may this role offer?"; a provider asks a different question, because it validates the request as a whole: a `tool_use` / `tool_result` pair naming a tool the `tools: [...]` array omits is refused outright, whatever the role may offer. So the array additionally carries an entry for every tool the assembled history already names, and this second rule reads the opposite way from election — a name the history references is never dropped, because the exchange has already happened. Where the tool's committed schema is present it is sent verbatim, exactly as for an elected tool; where it is not (a name the model invented, whose exchange nonetheless landed in the transcript), a bare `{"type": "object"}` stands in — the entry exists to make the history legible, not to offer the tool. The standing case is the compactor (§2.7), whose inherited transcript names the dispatching branch's tools; the general case is any branch whose history outlives a change to its role's `tools:` list. Declaring is not permitting: what a role may *call* is decided when the tool window runs (§2.5), and nothing this rule appends widens it.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-f021).** Both rules live in one composer, `src/prompt/dispatch/tools.rs::compose(worktree, declared, history, injected)`, which both step drivers call (`dispatch::run_exchange` and the `advance` hop): injection, then election, then the closure over the history. (Injection was the second of the three and took the role as a parameter until bl-9001 gave it a second source and its own precedence — *Host-injected tools* above.) What a compactor may *call* is `compactor::refusal(role, tool)`, consulted by `dispatch/tool_step.rs` before the executor. Before this, a compactor's request declared only its own two tools while shipping the inherited `tool_use` pair, and the provider refused its very first model call (`codex`/`openai_responses`: `parse_input`, "tool accepts only text content") — so compaction never completed on any branch that had used a tool, which the checkpoint clock makes the common case, not an edge case. Proven end to end over real `bz` against a recording provider row (`src/e2e/compaction_wire.rs`), and at the hop (`src/prompt/tests/compaction_toolset.rs`).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-5a1f).** *Declaring is not permitting* now holds for **every** role, not the compactor alone. The execution gate is `src/prompt/dispatch/tool_step/permit.rs::refusal(role, grant, injected, tool)`, consulted by both step drivers before the executor is entered: a `tool_use` naming a tool outside the calling role's **effective toolset** — its `providers.yaml` `tools:` grant plus whatever is injected into the request (`dispatch::tools::injected`: the compactor's pair for that role, plus any host injection, *Host-injected tools* above) — is declined in-band as an `is_error` `tool_result` naming that toolset, committed like any other transcript entry so the model reads the decline and steps on. It replaces `compactor::refusal(role, tool)` (bl-f021 above), which answered `None` for every role but the compactor: any other role could *run* whatever the closure rule had declared on its behalf, and the closure declares whatever the inherited transcript names. Found in the fleet demo — a `sensor` granted `[slack_read, message]` carried `bash` in its request because its dispatcher had used `bash`, so §4.3's grant boundary (a read-only observer on an outward surface) held only until a dispatcher used the guarded tool. The grant travels with the role out of the one resolution that reads both (`src/prompt/resolve.rs` → `dispatch::Resolved.tools`), so the gate opens no second role-resolution path. The compactor's confinement is unchanged in substance — its shipped row grants no tools (`src/install/tests.rs`), so its effective toolset is exactly the injected pair — but it is now the general rule applied to a grantless role rather than a role-shaped branch in the executor (§2.7, §4.3 *The compactor's paired toolset*). Covered by `src/prompt/dispatch/tool_step/tests/permit.rs` and, at the hop, the unchanged `src/prompt/tests/compaction_toolset.rs`.

**Host-injected tools (bl-9001).** A **linked** binding (§3.4) may hand the harness tool definitions of its own plus a **router** that answers them, through one optional object on the binding seam — `cmd::Fx::tool_injection`, injected exactly as `driver_target` and `adapter_target` are, and `None` for the exec binding. The object carries **both halves at once**, because either half alone is a defect: `tools()` — asked per assembly, for the agent the request is assembled for (bl-ddaa: the same `workspace`/`agent` discriminants a routed call carries, so a host whose declared set is per-agent state never has to guess the agent from its own argv) — is spliced into the request's `tools: [...]` array *and* unioned into the grant gate's effective toolset (so a host tool is never declared-then-refused), and `route()` **is the executor while the object is installed** — it answers every invocation the agent makes, and the three-hop resolution above stands behind it for no name at all. A **routed** invocation is indistinguishable downstream from a spawned one: the router answers in this contract's own vocabulary — stdout, stderr, exit code — so the result envelope, the `is_error` mapping, the bounded projection and the `input.json` / `output.json` record are the same code on the same paths, and the record is landed by the executor rather than by the host. What the router owes, and cannot be made to owe by construction (it runs on the executor's thread, where no SIGTERM cascade reaches it): its own deadline, a vanished endpoint rendered as a non-zero result rather than a hang, and an eye on the cancel flag it is handed. **Injection is the same mechanism the compactor's pair (§2.7) rides**, with two sources — the calling role's procedure and the binding — meeting in one list; where a host name collides with an elected one the injected definition wins in both halves, so the schema the model reads is the schema of what will run. **Nothing else moves.** The tools are individually named — never a multiplexer, the `docs/DESIGN_MCP_BRIDGE.md` §6 ruling, now binding on the host too — so every downstream boundary keeps seeing one name per capability. The set changes only when the embedder changes it (nothing here polls or discovers), so §5.5's append-only prompt-cache discipline holds and a host that does change it pays the prefix rebuild knowingly. And the tool control below adjudicates every invocation, routed or local, *before* anything is routed; it is a predicate and this seam does not touch it. The design record, its terms and its refusals are `docs/DESIGN_TOOL_INJECTION.md`.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-9001).** The seam is `src/prompt/tool/inject.rs` (`ToolInjection`, `InjectedTool`, `RoutedCall`, `RoutedCapture`), re-exported from `src/cmd/mod.rs` — the halves that consume it are below the surface and may not name `crate::cmd`. The router is reached in `prompt::tool::spawn` (`execute` and `execute_all`, in place of `resolve` — see bl-a00a below); the two production wirings are `cmd::prompt` and `dispatch::advance::cli`, both `SpawnTool::new(…).with_injection(…)`. The declaration and permission halves read one list, `dispatch::tools::injected(role, executor)` — off the **executor**, not `Deps`, because the thing that will answer a call is the honest source of what may be called (PRINCIPLES, single source of truth). The compactor's own injection was generalized into that list in the same change: `compactor::builtin_tool_schemas(role)` returns `Vec<InjectedTool>` and the separate names half (`compactor::injected`) is deleted, one list read two ways.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-a00a): the router's scope is total, and only its scope changed.** `route()` could once decline a name, which fell through to the three hops; it no longer can, and its return type says so (`RoutedCapture`, not `Option<RoutedCapture>`). The binding therefore chooses **one** execution pipeline for the whole process — install an injection and every tool is routed, install none and every tool is spawned — rather than a name-by-name mix in which which pipeline an operator hit depended on which names a host happened to own, each with its own adjudication story and its own containment claim. A name a host does not own is a refusal the host renders in this contract's own vocabulary (non-zero, reason on `stderr`), indistinguishable from the "no such tool" an absent binary produced; a host with nothing to route to therefore refuses every invocation in band. Executed against yog's `docs/REMOTE.md` §5 (*"the engine's driver keeps no local executor"*) under its §12 front-door invariant. **The exec binding keeps its spawn**, deliberately and not as a fallback: it installs no injection at all, so nothing is reachable through it that a host could also have answered. That decision is priced in `docs/DESIGN_TOOL_INJECTION.md` §3.4, along with the one consequence left open for the composing host — that the compactor's procedure pair (§2.7) is routed like every other name.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-4cbb): the built-in set is readable on the surface.** Because the router's scope is total, a host that installs an injection is the only thing standing between the agent and every name it calls — and *which* names this engine can answer behind its own front door (the third resolver hop, `<driver_target> tool <name>`) is a fact only this crate holds. It is now on the command surface: `cmd::BUILTIN_TOOLS`, the same `prompt::tool::builtin::NAMES` const the unknown-tool decline and `litany tool --help` render, re-exported for the reason the injection seam itself is (the halves that consume it are below the surface and may not name `crate::cmd`). Nothing behaves differently; what changes is that a host re-entering the front door for the engine's own tools can *ask* rather than restate — a restatement no gate on either side of the crate boundary can catch going stale, since an eighth built-in simply refuses on a host that never heard of it and the failure reads as the host's.

**Descriptions-always population.** The `descriptions/**` tree the assembly above intersects against is written by a single step of config-commit authoring (§2.2): the harness copies, from the data-root pools, every available tool's JSON schema (`<data-root>/tools/<name>.json` → `descriptions/tools/<name>.json`, point 2) and every available skill's `SKILL.md` frontmatter (`<data-root>/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` → `descriptions/skills/<name>.md`, the Description-always mechanism above). It is **one mechanism over two artifact kinds, not two producers.** Committing it in the config is what lets every agent branch inherit the descriptors via git (§2.2, §2.3) and makes context assembly intersect against the branch's own read-state tree (§2.10, §5.1) instead of re-reading mutable data-root state — the same snapshot-not-symlink discipline as skill bodies above. (Under follow-the-tip, §2.2 bl-403b, a lineage advance that changes a grant leaves this dispatch-time cut behind; the refresh is filed, bl-37cd.) The data-root pools are the single source of truth for *what this install provides*; the committed `descriptions/**` snapshot is the single source of truth for *what agents forked from this config are pinned to see* — distinct facts that must not drift together, so the copy is a snapshot, not a mirror (PRINCIPLES, single source of truth).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-e3f5).** The snapshot pass parses what it copies, with the same parsers the composer above uses at prompt time: each `SKILL.md` frontmatter body through `skill::parse` (the same function `read_description`, §3.3 point 3, applies to the committed form), each tool schema through `serde_json::from_slice` (the same function `read_schema` applies). Before this, the pass only checked a skill's frontmatter *fence* — present and closed — never the YAML inside it, and never touched a tool schema's bytes at all beyond a verbatim `fs::copy`; a well-fenced but malformed body (an unquoted `description: posts to slack: general`, the plain-scalar trap — a bare `: ` mid-scalar reads as a second mapping key) or a malformed schema passed `litany new` / `litany config` untouched and surfaced only at the forked agent's first prompt step, deep inside this section's composer, after the workspace, the config commit, and (for `new`) the agent branch already existed. `src/template/descriptions.rs::snapshot` now declines — naming the offending pool file and the parse error — before any commit lands; the transient-checkout teardown discipline above (*Teardown is structural*) tears down on this decline exactly as on every other. `litany prime`'s own shipped pools (`schemas/tools/**`, `skills/**`) are covered by a same-parser test (`src/install/tests.rs`) rather than a runtime check, since a shipped asset that failed to parse would refuse every fresh install's first `litany new`.

**The fork derives the branch's descriptors from the config commit, filtered to the role's grant.** One config commit serves every role, so the snapshot it carries is necessarily the whole pool — and the fork is where that stops being true of a *branch*. The dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2), already trimming the config's control files out of the agent's tree, additionally makes `descriptions/tools/` exactly the forking role's `tools:` grant: every granted tool's `descriptions/tools/<name>.json` and its tool-claimed `descriptions/skills/<name>.md` are **checked out of the governing config commit** (§2.2), and every descriptor the forked-in tree carries that the grant does not name is removed. A standalone skill — one no tool claims — is never removed: it has a wire home of its own (the path-framed head block above) and is what `load_skill` elects from.

**The governing config commit is the descriptor set's one home; a branch's tree is a derived view of it.** The derivation reads that commit and never the tree it forked off, which is what keeps a child's toolset a function of the child's *own* grant. Pruning the fork point's tree instead capped every grant at its dispatcher's — the parent's tree was itself already cut to the parent's grant, so a chain of dispatches intersected grant after grant, and a role's `tools:` could only ever narrow along the chain. A granted tool that survived nowhere in the chain then vanished from the request with no diagnostic, because tools-list assembly (above) intersects declaration against the tree.

**A grant the config commit does not describe is declined at the fork.** A role granting a tool with no `descriptions/tools/<name>.json` anywhere in its governing config commit is a config fault — `providers.yaml` and `descriptions/**` disagree inside one commit — so the dispatch is refused, naming the tool and the described pool, before a branch, worktree or inbox exists (the validity-before-fork discipline of role validation, §4.3, and the §6 budget gate). Composing the smaller toolset instead is the one outcome that is invisible in both places a reader would look: the wire array and the agent's own tree. A tool whose *schema* is committed but whose skill frontmatter is not stays sanctioned — the entry composes with no top-level `description` (above), which is a shape, not a disagreement.

This makes the tree honest rather than adding a policy. A non-granted tool's descriptors compose **nowhere**: the body walk skips `descriptions/tools/**` and every tool-claimed skill description (*two wire homes*, above), and the tools array carries only what the role declared plus what the history names. An uncomposed worktree file is ordinary — the manifest is the inclusion list (§5.1) — but these particular bytes *purported to describe the callable set*, and they were reachable by `bash`, which is how they were found: asked why it could not message a sibling, an agent read `descriptions/tools/message.json` and `descriptions/skills/message.md`, concluded the environment supported messaging, and spent many steps discovering that its wire array said otherwise (twice reproduced; the grant gap behind it is §4.3 *Toolset*). `providers.yaml` is removed from the tree by the same commit, so nothing on the branch could have told it otherwise. After the cut, `descriptions/tools/` **is** the callable set and the question is answered from the agent's own branch in one listing.

No resolved-grant file is written: a second on-branch representation of the grant is a second thing to drift (PRINCIPLES, single source of truth). The removal half issues no git command when the forked-in tree strands nothing — the shipped default's case (§4.3), and equally a fork off a parent tip cut to the same grant. Its one consequence is on the compactor, whose `tools:` is empty by design: the tools its inherited transcript names lose their verbatim schemas, so *the array is closed over the history it ships* (above) stands in a bare `{"type": "object"}` — the entry exists to make the history legible, not to offer the tool, which is exactly the case that rule was written for.

> **Shipped-state note.** The derivation is `src/prompt/dispatch/step_commit/descriptors.rs`, run by the dispatch commit's `trim_to_context` for both fork paths — the root's step-1 commit and `subagent::spawn_subagent_branch`'s child dispatch. It carries the role, its grant and the governing config commit together (`Grant`), because the cut means nothing without all three. The decline is `Error::GrantUndescribed`, pre-flighted in `child_dispatch::run` — in the *parent's* worktree, before the fork — so a refused dispatch leaves no branch debris. Shipped with [bl-a900], which replaced the parent-tree prune of [bl-18a9] while keeping its property: a role still carries no descriptor for a tool it cannot compose.

**Stdio contract.** Identical for in-process and external tools:

- **Stdin.** The `tool_use.input` JSON object the model emitted, passed verbatim. The tool owns its own input-schema validation; the harness does not interpret the payload beyond extracting it from the `tool_use` block.
- **Stdout.** Raw bytes. The harness carries them verbatim inside the tool call's **result envelope** (below), which becomes the `content` of the canonical `tool_result` block, committed as a transcript entry (§2.3) and composed into the next step's request from there. Tools stay simple: a tool prints, and the framing is the harness's concern.
- **Stderr.** Raw bytes. Captured to the on-disk record regardless of exit status, and carried into `tool_result.content` under the envelope's marker whenever the tool wrote any — on a zero exit as well as a non-zero one.
- **Exit code.** Stated on the envelope's first line, *and* mapped to the flag: 0 → `tool_result.is_error = false`; non-zero → `tool_result.is_error = true`. Termination by a signal other than the harness's own SIGTERM (SIGSEGV, SIGABRT, etc.) is a harness-level fault per §2.10 — the step aborts and the branch is flagged, not delivered to the model as a semantic error.
- **Environment.** The harness sets two env vars on every tool subprocess so tools that depend on the calling agent's identity can read it without the model having to thread context through the input schema: `LITANY_CONV_REPO` (absolute path to the workspace root, §2.2) and `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH` (the calling agent's id == its full hyphenated descent, §2.3). The names predate the ladder and are kept — they are code, not prose. Both are derived from the executor's `step_dir` so they are guaranteed-correct for the tool call. Tools that do not need them ignore them; the v0.4 `dispatch` and `message` built-ins are the canonical readers. The direction and discipline are fixed: harness → subprocess, and the harness owns the names.
- **Working directory.** Every tool subprocess runs with its working directory set to the **calling agent's current working directory** — its worktree (`<workspace>/agents/<agent-id>/`, §2.2) by default, and thereafter wherever the agent's own `cd` call moved it (*The working directory is one mutable per-agent fact*, below) — never the directory the harness process itself happens to be sitting in. The default is what makes a tool's side effects *the agent's work product*: `bash` writing `out.txt`, a relative path handed to `read_file`, and a `git` invocation with no `-C` all act on the agent's branch, so the tool commit's `git add -A` captures exactly them (Commit-per-side-effect, below). The worktree is derived from the executor's `step_dir` — the same single derivation as the env vars above, so the directory a tool runs in and the worktree its `LITANY_CONV_*` vars name cannot disagree. A `step_dir` that names no materialized worktree is declined (`ExecError::NoWorktree`), never run in an inherited directory: a detached driver started from an arbitrary cwd would otherwise act on the operator's own files. In-process tools inherit this cwd through the `litany tool <name>` re-entry, so the built-ins need no cwd handling of their own — the shell `bash` forks lands there by inheritance, and a `cd` *inside* that shell dies with it, which is why moving is a tool call and not a command.
- **SIGTERM and deadline.** The harness sends SIGTERM on cancel (§2.9); the tool has 5 seconds to flush and exit cleanly, after which SIGKILL follows. The flush deadline is tools-only: the provider adapter needs none — `bz` dies on SIGTERM at once, and the missing trailing `end` is the signature (§4.4 Cancellation).

**Result envelope (bl-ffc5).** The **result envelope** is the model-facing rendering of one finished tool call — the bytes that become `tool_result.content`. It is plain text, not a JSON wrapper (a tool's stdout is often itself JSON, and double-encoding it would make the model parse twice), and it has exactly three parts in a fixed order: the line `Exit code: <N>`; the child's stdout verbatim; and, whenever the child wrote any, its stderr after a `--- stderr ---` marker line. An empty stream contributes nothing and its marker is not emitted — the marker announces bytes, and there are none to announce. The exit-code line is unconditional, which additionally means no tool call can produce empty content, a block some provider protocols refuse outright.

*Why the code is stated and not merely flagged.* `is_error` is one bit, so a model reading it alone cannot distinguish exit 1 (ran, failed) from 127 (no such command) from 143 (the §2.9 cancel cascade) — three different next moves. It is also the least reliable field on the wire: each protocol spells `is_error` differently (§4.4), and brazen's round-trip of it degrades across providers, while `content` is carried verbatim by all of them. Codex, the harness gpt-5.x models are tuned against, states the code in the content for exactly this reason and those models are trained to read it there — the same "match a tuned shape where the shape differs" test that left `bash`'s *schema* alone (*A tool definition is the only frame litany ships*, above) here says to change it.

*Why stderr is surfaced on success.* A command that exits 0 while writing to stderr is the ordinary case — compilers, test runners, anything that logs progress. Dropping those bytes hid every warning and deprecation from the agent and made `2>&1` the only way to see them, which the agent had to think to do before it knew there was anything to see.

*Why the streams are labelled rather than merged.* Codex merges them, but it captures them merged; litany reads two pipes (`prompt::tool::subprocess`), so merging would discard a distinction the capture already holds. It would also splice diagnostics into products: `load_skill` returns `{status, path}` on stdout and `cd` returns `{cwd}`, and a warning landing mid-object corrupts what the model is meant to read. The cost of keeping them apart is that interleaving order is lost — which separate capture had already lost — and `2>&1` remains available to any `bash` command that wants it.

*What the envelope deliberately omits.* Wall time, which codex also reports. It is recorded per tool call in the diagnostic `output.json` (`started_at` / `ended_at`, below) and can be surfaced when a decision hangs on it; until one does, it is a per-tool-call token cost for a fact no model behaviour turns on, and the filed defect was the exit code and stderr.

*One derivation, one place.* The envelope is rendered by `prompt::tool::envelope::render`, called only by the executor, which is where all three facts live. A tool call **declined before the executor was entered** — the grant gate of *declaring is not permitting* (above) — carries the harness's own decline text with no envelope: no child ran, so there is no exit code, and synthesizing one would make the transcript assert a process that never existed. Every result that *did* run a child is enveloped, uniformly, `bash` and built-ins alike.

**The working directory is one mutable per-agent fact: the `cd` built-in.** An agent changes where it works by calling `litany tool cd`, input `{path}`; the harness reads the result at every subsequent tool spawn. This is a *fact about the agent*, not a per-tool-call parameter and not shell state: a `cd` inside a `bash` command moves only that one `sh -c` process, and a `workdir`-style tool input would be a second home for the same fact (which is why `bash`'s schema has none, *A tool definition is the only frame litany ships*, above). The semantics are pinned: a relative `path` resolves against the current working directory — the tool process *runs* in it, so resolution is the kernel's answer rather than a re-derivation of one — `..` and symlinks resolve, and the result is the absolute directory (`{cwd}`). A `path` that names nothing, or names a non-directory, is **declined** as an ordinary `is_error` `tool_result` and the agent does not move (PRINCIPLES, decline illegal operations).

**The fact's one home is the agent's own mark, `refs/litany/cwd/<agent-id>`.** It joins the per-agent mark namespace (§2.2 `workspace::MARK_REF_ROOT`) beside `conflicted` (§2.6), `budget-exhausted` (§6), `returned` (§8), `abandoned` and `notify` — no second mechanism, and three properties fall out of the choice rather than being arranged: it is reaped with the agent by `litany delete`, which enumerates the mark *root* and not a list of kinds (§9.2); it crosses no fork, no transfer and no merge, because a mark is keyed by agent id and nothing merges marks — so a child starts in *its* worktree, never its parent's cwd, unless its own dispatch named a directory (*The mark's second writer*, below); and it is not context (§5.1), because it lives outside every worktree — the agent learns where it is from the tool result, which is a committed transcript entry like any other. This mark differs from its siblings in one way: **it carries a value.** The ref names a blob whose bytes are the absolute path, where the other kinds are bare assertions whose existence is the whole fact. A ref may name any object, so the extra fact needs no extra home, and `git gc` keeps the blob for exactly as long as the mark.

**Resolution at spawn: the mark if it names a live directory, else the worktree.** An unset mark is the state of every agent that never called `cd`, so the default is the general path with the fact absent — not a bootstrap case, and behaviour is unchanged for an agent that never moves. A mark whose directory has since disappeared resolves to the worktree **rather than declining the call**: `cd` is itself a tool call, so a hard decline would strand the agent somewhere it could never leave. `ExecError::NoWorktree` is unchanged and still governs the worktree itself, which remains the anchor every agent has.

**The mark's second writer: `--cwd` at creation.** `cd` moves an agent that is already running; a caller that wants an agent to work in a directory it names has nothing to write. So `litany prompt` and `litany dispatch` each take an optional `--cwd <path>` that **seeds** the mark — writes it once, at creation, after the agent's id has settled and before the first step. It is a second writer for the one fact, not a second fact and not a second channel: the same ref, read by the same resolution above, with omission being today's behaviour (the agent's worktree) — the general path with the fact absent, not a bootstrap case.

*Why a parameter, and not a caller-side ref write.* The mark's format is public, so a caller could write the ref itself with two git commands. It cannot win the race: a dispatch lands the fork, deposits the goal and launches the driver in one move, so the caller learns the agent's id only after step 1 may already be underway, and there is no lock to take. **Creation is the only moment that precedes the first step.** The parameter therefore buys *ordering*, not capability — and keeps consumers out of litany's ref namespace.

*Validated before the fork, like a pin (§2.5).* The directory runs the same validation `cd` runs — it must exist, it must be a directory, and it must survive the mark's trimmed-UTF-8 round trip — at the binding, before anything of the agent exists, so a refusal leaves no branch, ref, worktree or inbox behind. One voice with `cd`, not a second set of rules. Past that point the only failure left is git's, and it fails the creation: no agent starts in a directory its caller did not ask for.

*Nothing is inherited.* Marks are keyed by agent id and nothing forks, merges or transfers them, so a child's mark is unset unless its own dispatch named one — including a child of an agent that was itself seeded. Absence is the default, deliberately: a consumer whose isolation rule is "no two write-capable agents share a mutable checkout" depends on a directory reaching exactly the agent its caller named.

*Policy-blind, and CLI-only.* The parameter is a directory and nothing more — litany learns nothing of what the caller is doing with it. The model-facing `dispatch` tool input (§2.5) gets **no** such parameter: it would cost tokens on every request and hand a running agent the power to place its children anywhere on the machine. A caller that fires through the CLI already has the flag.

**No containment, deliberately — but the work-product boundary is real.** The target may be any directory on the machine. v1.0 bounds a tool's *authority* nowhere: §3.6 defers that to the v1.1 sandbox and places it on the artifact, uniformly, so a `cd`-shaped exception would be policy invented in the one place it does not belong (PRINCIPLES, severability), and `bash` could already reach outside the worktree with an absolute path — `cd` moves where relative paths land, not what a tool may touch. What *does* change is what becomes a work product: commit-per-side-effect stages the worktree (below), so edits an agent makes elsewhere are real but uncommitted — off its branch, invisible to a parent (§2.6), absent from replay (§9.2). An agent working outside its worktree is working off the record, and the `cd` tool definition is where it is told so (the same "the definition or nowhere" rule as `bash`, above).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-a501).** The built-in is `src/prompt/tool/builtin/cd/`, routed by the `builtin::run` dispatcher and wired at `litany tool cd`; its schema (`schemas/tools/cd.json`) and `SKILL.md` (`skills/cd/`) install into the data-root pools like every other tool triple, and the shipped `worker` grant carries it (`template/providers.yaml`). The mark is `src/workspace/cwd.rs` — `read` / `write` over `refs/litany/cwd/<agent-id>`, the value staged and hashed with `git hash-object` because the value round-trips as trimmed UTF-8, which is also why a path that trimming would change is declined at write. The spawn-side resolution is `prompt::tool::spawn::Caller::resolve`, which gained the mark read beside the existing worktree derivation, so the cwd and the `LITANY_CONV_*` vars still come from one place.
>
> **`--cwd` (bl-d0b4).** The flag is on `litany prompt` and `litany dispatch` (`src/cmd/prompt.rs`, `src/cmd/dispatch.rs`), which resolve it through `workspace::cwd::resolve` — the shared validation the `cd` built-in now also calls, so the three declines (names nothing / not a directory / would not round-trip) have one home and one wording. The write is `prompt::seed_cwd`, called by both creation paths at the same point: `dispatch::run_exchange` after the conv-id settles and before `spawn_branch`, `child_dispatch::run` after the child's id settles and before `spawn_subagent_branch`. Non-inheritance is pinned by `child_dispatch::tests::cwd::a_dispatchers_own_working_directory_is_never_inherited`; the ordering (seed precedes the fork) by `prompt::tests::cwd_seed`; the CLI parity of the refusal by `cmd::tests::cwd`.

**The patch tool: `apply_patch`, the structured edit path (bl-ae6b).** Before it, every file edit rode `bash` — sed, heredocs, `python -c` — so edit failures were shell-quoting accidents rather than typed declines. `apply_patch` takes one **patch envelope** — a single string opening `*** Begin Patch` and closing `*** End Patch` — carrying add (`*** Add File:`), delete (`*** Delete File:`), update (`*** Update File:`, hunks of ` `/`-`/`+` lines) and rename (`*** Move to:`) for any number of files, in **one tool invocation, applied all-or-nothing**: every operation is validated and every post-state computed in memory before any write lands, so a patch that cannot apply in full applies not at all. The grammar is codex's `apply_patch` and the input schema is one string field named `input`, both verbatim — the criterion is what models are trained on, not what is elegant to parse (the same "match a tuned shape where the shape differs" test as `bash`'s schema, above).

An update hunk locates its context by descending the **matching ladder** — exact match; ignore trailing whitespace; ignore edge whitespace; Unicode-normalized (smart quotes, NBSP-class spaces, typographic dashes folded to ASCII) — mirroring `git apply`'s fuzz as codex's `seek_sequence` implements it. A rung is consulted only when every rung above found *nothing*, and the winning rung's match must be **unique** from the previous hunk's landing point onward: more than one match is a loud typed decline naming the count and the rung — never a guessed edit — and the repair recipe is bounded, an `@@ <enclosing symbol>` anchor line (sought first, cursor moving past it) or more context, not "paste the file". The stale-state guard (bl-e249) is structural rather than a version number: an add declines when the path exists, an update/delete when it does not, and drifted content stops matching the authored context, so the patch refuses rather than overwriting unseen changes — re-read and re-author. Success prints a JSON report (per file: op; per hunk: winning rung, landing line, and — when a fuzzy rung won — the lines actually replaced), so the executor's ordinary capture preserves the true pre/post delta in the diagnostic `output.json` beside the authored patch in `input.json`, and every decline reason reaches the model through the ordinary `is_error` `tool_result`. Commit-per-side-effect (below) is untouched: the application is the side effect, and the per-invocation `git add -A` commit rides exactly as for `bash`.

*Why in-process, not a `litany-tool-*` binary.* Point 1 above already answers it: in-process is "the default for tools shipped with the harness", and the external slot is the *contributor* channel — shipping a first-party tool through it would open a second distribution path for core code (PRINCIPLES, one way through). §3.6 frames the real stake — shipping in-process places the tool in the trusted computing base — and `apply_patch`'s authority (write files where the invocation's cwd points) is strictly a subset of what the in-process `bash` already wields, so externalizing it would bound nothing while `bash` sits beside it. The extraction discipline the external flavor argues for is real, but it lives at the module boundary — the grammar, ladder and applier are pure functions with no harness imports (`src/prompt/tool/builtin/apply_patch/{parse,seek,apply}.rs`) — not at a process boundary; the provider layer's per-name binaries were retired on the same reasoning once brazen gave the layer a real interface (§4.4). Whether the model-familiar grammar moves solve rate is an agent-eval hypothesis (§9.3), deliberately not an acceptance premise here.

**The multi-tool: one round trip fanned into N tool invocations (bl-8ee7).** The built-in `multi_tool` takes a list of **inner invocations** — the same `{name, input}` shapes the individual tools declare — plus execution metadata, and runs them in one tool window: one model round trip, N tool executions (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §4 *Multi-tool envelope*). The metadata is one field, `on_failure`: `abort` (the default — a failed or declined entry ends the envelope, every later entry reported `skipped` without running) or `run_all` (every entry runs regardless). The aggregate result attributes each entry in order — `[k/N] <name>: ok|failed|declined|skipped` with that invocation's own result envelope (or decline / skip reason) as the section body — and is `is_error` when any entry failed or was declined.

**The multi-tool's binary is the step loop.** It is the one tool the tool window answers itself (`src/prompt/dispatch/tool_step/multi.rs`), intercepted by name before the executor — because everything the envelope means lives on the harness side of the subprocess boundary, and a subprocess implementation would have to bypass all of it. Instead, **no inner invocation bypasses anything a top-level one passes through**: `multi_tool` itself must be in the role's grant (the ordinary *declaring is not permitting* gate runs first) and is adjudicated by the configured tool control like any tool (*Tool control*, below — the control sees the whole envelope as the invocation's input), and each inner invocation then re-enters that same refusal gate *and* the same control, then the same executor with the same stop flag and the same `tool_output:` bounded projection. One degradation is deliberate: an inner **hold cannot park mid-envelope** — entries before it have already executed, and a park would make their uncommitted side effects re-run on resume, exactly what the hold mark exists to rule out — so it becomes an in-band decline that keeps the hold's one guarantee (the invocation does not run unreviewed) and tells the model to re-issue it as a top-level `tool_use`, where a hold parks properly. Its diagnostic record lands under `steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/tools/<outer-id>-<k>/` like any other, the id derived from the envelope's wire id plus the 1-based position, since the model minted no id for it. Consequently `multi_tool` is not in the `litany tool <name>` pool — there is no subprocess form to address — while its schema/skill pair (`schemas/tools/multi_tool.json`, `skills/multi_tool/`) installs, snapshots, and grants like any other tool's.

**Serial by default; the envelope may assert otherwise.** Inner invocations run strictly in list order, one at a time, unless the envelope's `execution` says `parallel`. Serial is the default because order is load-bearing by construction: every side effect shares one worktree, and the working directory is one mutable per-agent fact (*above*) that an inner `cd` moves for the invocations after it. The round-trip saving — the model states N invocations for one completion's price — is the point of the envelope; overlap is the extra.

**The assertion is the agent's, and the harness classifies nothing.** A per-tool read/write table (Codex's split: read-safe tools share the window, mutating tools take exclusive slots) was considered and rejected. It approximates a per-tool-call fact with a per-tool one — it must assume `bash` collides with everything, because some `bash` does — and it needs a new fact with no authoritative home (the input schema describes shape, not effects; the grant is per-role and would let one tool carry two classifications). Only the caller knows whether *these* invocations touch the same paths, so the caller states it and the harness takes it at face value. Nothing verifies the claim, and two hazards are deliberately unpoliced: concurrent writers to one path are last-write-wins, and an inner `cd` races every sibling's cwd resolution. Both are legal — unwise, not forbidden — and guard rails wait until a real workload goes over the edge rather than being designed against in advance.

**Concurrency lives in the executor, not the step loop.** `parallel` gates every entry first, on the loop's own thread — the depth check, the grant, the tool control — and hands only the survivors to `ToolExecutor::execute_all` together. Gating before starting anything also means a refusal cannot land after a sibling it was meant to precede. The executor overlaps the part worth overlapping (N blocking subprocess waits) and nothing else: its clock, git runner and PATH lookup never cross a thread boundary, which is what keeps `Sync` off three traits that have nothing to do with threading (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`, severability). `execute_all`'s default implementation runs the calls serially, so an executor that declines to overlap is still correct — concurrency is an optimization, not a semantic.

**What `parallel` does not change.** Results render in list order, never completion order, so the aggregate is deterministic whatever the scheduler did. The envelope still commits **once** — it never committed per-invocation, so all N writes ride the same `git add -A` sweep and no attribution is lost. `on_failure` is not consulted: it is a sequencing policy, and once every entry has started there is nothing left to skip.

**Block-on-all, structurally.** All inner results return together as the envelope's single `tool_result`. "Return answers as they come" would mean several result entries for one `tool_use` id, and that has no home at either layer: the transcript commits exactly one entry per tool invocation (§2.3), and every protocol brazen encodes carries exactly one result per id — Anthropic pairs one `tool_result` per `tool_use`, OpenAI one `role: "tool"` message per `tool_call_id`, google one `function_response` (§4.4). Incremental delivery is therefore out of scope as a wire fact, not a deferral; the tool's own description says so, and an agent that needs a result before choosing its next invocation issues top-level invocations instead. The envelope's worktree side effects ride its one tool commit together — commit-per-side-effect (below) counts the envelope as one tool invocation, which it is: one `tool_use` block, one committed result.

**Depth 1.** An inner invocation naming `multi_tool` is declined in place: nesting adds no expressive power to a flat list — the entries would run in the same serial order — and would compound id derivation and attribution for nothing. A malformed envelope is declined whole, in-band, restating the expected shape; an empty `invocations` list is the general path with empty inputs — zero entries run, the tally says so, `is_error` false.

**Disk record.** The tool executor (§3.2) lands two files per tool call under `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/tools/<tool-id>/` — at the workspace root, outside every worktree (§2.2, §2.3):

- `input.json` — the `tool_use` block from the model verbatim (`id`, `name`, `input`).
- `output.json` — `{stdout, stderr, exit_code, started_at, ended_at}`.

`<tool-id>` is the `tool_use.id` from the wire (e.g. `toolu_01abc…`). Writes use temp-path + atomic rename. These records are not git-tracked (the location is outside every worktree).

**Commit-per-side-effect, serialized.** A tool call's *diagnostic* record (above) is not a commit — it is an out-of-worktree plain file. Every tool call commits at least its `tool_result` transcript entry on the emitting branch (§2.3); a tool call's *worktree side effects* (e.g. `bash` editing files in the branch's worktree) ride the same commits, landed before the next tool runs. Sibling tool calls running in parallel serialize their worktree commits, since a dirty worktree between siblings would violate "Single author per file" (§2.5, PRINCIPLES).

**Wire `tool_result` framing is transcript-backed.** The wire-level `tool_result` blocks the agent reads on its next step compose from the committed transcript entries (`messages/NNN-tool.json`, §2.3), never from `steps/`: the tool executor derives the canonical block — the *result envelope* as `content` (above), `is_error` from the exit code, the pairing `tool_use_id` — once, at commit time. The per-tool-call `output.json` remains the raw capture (full stdout/stderr/exit code with timestamps) for audit; the transcript entry is what entered context. These are two facts, not two copies — *what the subprocess emitted* versus *what the model saw* — the same snapshot discipline as `descriptions/**` (above). Tool outputs are nondeterministic and cannot be reconstructed by re-running, which is exactly why their context home must be a committed file (§2.3): replay from the read-state commit re-reads the same entry bit-for-bit. "Tool in progress" is derived state too — the emitting step's model-output transcript entry carries a `tool_use` block with no matching committed `tool_result` entry yet — not a separate file.

**Bounded transcript projection (bl-d5fa).** The transcript entry is a **bounded projection** of the capture: before the result envelope is rendered, each stream — stdout and stderr **independently** — is bounded to its first `head_bytes` and last `tail_bytes` per the governing `workflow.yaml`'s `tool_output:` block (§6), the omitted middle replaced by a one-line marker: `[... <stream> truncated: <B> bytes / <L> lines total; showing the first <H> and last <T> bytes; full record: steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/tools/<tool-id>/output.json ...]`. Nothing is lost — the full bytes already have a home in the diagnostic `output.json` (Disk record, below), and the marker says so in workspace-relative terms (no host paths in a committed entry), so the model knows exactly what it lost and can re-run with a filter instead of guessing. Without a bound, one `cat big.log` commits megabytes into the transcript, and assembly being append-only (§5.1) makes the damage permanent until compaction. The head keeps the command banner, the tail keeps the failure end — the two parts a model acts on (codex's HeadTailBuffer shape, with byte counts where codex states token counts: litany has no tokenizer, and a fabricated token count would be a lie in the transcript). The streams are bounded and the envelope is rendered *around* them — the `Exit code:` header and the `--- stderr ---` marker are structure, never cappable content — and a stream within its allowance rides untouched, marker-free: the marker announces cut bytes, and there are none. The policy is severable (§6): omitting `tool_output:` projects streams unbounded — the general path with the policy absent — and the shipped default (16 KiB head + 16 KiB tail per stream) lives in `template/workflow.yaml`, not in code. Mechanism is `prompt::tool::bound::apply`, applied only where the envelope is rendered (the executor, above).

**Tool control (bl-de6d).** A **tool control** is an operator-configured adjudicator standing in front of tool execution: when the governing `workflow.yaml` carries a `tool_control:` block (§6), every invocation the role's grant permits is consulted on **before the executor is entered**, and the control's **verdict** — `pass`, `refuse`, or `hold` — decides what happens. This is a distinct class of connection point — a declared seam where operator-supplied behavior attaches to the harness — from the §6 actions: an action is an *effect* run at a lifecycle or per-step event, while a control is a *predicate* inside the tool window, and none of the closed action set can sit in the path of one `tool_use` block. The seam is the whole of what ships — **no control ships out of the box** (codex's guardian — an LLM adjudicator over a compact transcript, failing closed — maps onto litany as a verifier-role dispatch a control makes; building one is its own severable config). The ordering is fixed: the grant gate (*declaring is not permitting*, above) runs first, so a control adjudicates only what the role may call at all — grants are structure, controls are policy.

- **The control is a binary the config names** (`tool_control: {command: …}` — the §4.2 `adapter:` idiom: config names an executable, the OS resolves it), invoked once per invocation with no arguments. Stdin carries one JSON object — the `tool_use` block verbatim (`id`, `name`, `input`) plus `role` and `agent_id`; stdout answers one JSON verdict (`{"verdict":"pass"}`, or `refuse`/`hold` with a required `reason`); exit 0 is the contract. The `LITANY_CONV_REPO` / `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH` env pair travels exactly as for tools, and everything else a control wants — the transcript, an approval file, a verifier's verdict — it reads from disk. The control runs in the **workspace root**, never the agent's cwd (it acts for the operator; the agent's own `cd` must not move it), and under the ordinary §2.9 stop cascade. All scoping is the control's business: the harness consults it for every permitted invocation and carries no per-tool or per-role filter — policy lives in the control, not the core (PRINCIPLES, severability). Because a held invocation is re-adjudicated (below), a control must be side-effect-free per consult: it is an adjudicator, not an actor.
- **Pass** enters the executor unchanged. **Refuse** never enters it: the control's reason is committed as an in-band `is_error` `tool_result` — the same idiom as the grant decline, and like it enveloped-less (no child ran, so no exit code is invented) — so the model reads why and steps on.
- **Hold parks the invocation before execution.** The seam writes the **hold mark** — `refs/litany/held/<agent-id>`, a value-carrying mark in the per-agent namespace (the `cwd` shape, above) whose blob is one line of JSON: the held `tool_use` id, the tool, and the control's reason — and the window ceases with **nothing executed or committed at or past the held block**. The driver exits without a terminal: no deposit, no epitaph — the branch is *parked*, its whole state disk-derived (the mark plus the unpaired tail), and the model sees nothing (a hold is an operator-side pause, not a transcript event). A parked branch **queues its mail**: delivery onto the unpaired tail would wedge a user entry between a `tool_use` and its `tool_result` (§2.3 pairing), so a drive of a parked branch delivers nothing and the queued mail lands at the resumed chain's next ordinary boundary.
- **Release is re-adjudication, not a verb.** The next drive of the agent (`litany advance`, hand-run or launched) finds the mark, skips the blocks whose results are already committed (derived from the transcript, never a stored cursor), and consults the control **freshly** about the held invocation — pass runs it, refuse declines it in-band, hold parks again. Who releases therefore lives entirely in the control: whatever out-of-band fact changes its answer (an operator's approval file, a verifier child's verdict) is its contract, and the harness guarantees exactly one thing — re-adjudication on the next drive. The mark, not the config, asserts the park: a resume whose workflow no longer names a control still skips the committed blocks and re-adjudicates under the seam's general path — an absent control passes every invocation — so removing the control releases the branch without ever re-running what committed. The mark lifts at the moment its invocation re-adjudicates to any verdict but hold — *before* it can execute — which keeps the mark's assertion exact: **held before execution; nothing at or past it ran**. That assertion is what distinguishes a parked branch from a mid-tools crash (where a tool may have run without committing its result): under the mark, re-entering the window is replay-safe; without it, the next drive settles the window as a crash — an in-band died `tool_result` per unanswered id, committed before any mail delivers (§6, bl-4187) — and only the buried pre-settlement form stays the §6 loud decline. A **stop** landing mid-consult decides nothing, so the mark stands — but the stopped exit settles the window on its way out (§2.9), which makes the mark stale by this same rule and lets the ordinary stale sweep clear it on the next drive. The park is dissolved by the machinery already here, not by a release verb.
- **A control that cannot answer fails closed, loudly.** A control that will not spawn, dies, exits non-zero, or emits anything but one well-formed verdict refuses the invocation *and aborts the step* as a harness-level fault (`Error::ToolControl`). Closed, because a control exists to keep an invocation from running unreviewed — failing open would run exactly what the operator asked to gate (the codex guardian's choice, for the same reason). Loud rather than in-band, because a broken control is operator infrastructure the model can neither see nor fix, and an in-band decline would invite blind retries against it. The one exception is the harness's own stop SIGTERM felling the control mid-consult: with the stop flag set that is the stop (§2.9 step 3), not a fault — and a control fault during a *resume* leaves the mark standing, so the branch stays parked rather than stranded. Severability holds throughout: no `tool_control:` block means no control is consulted, no subprocess, no mark read — the general path with the policy absent — and removing a control deletes config, never code.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-de6d).** The seam is `src/prompt/dispatch/tool_step.rs` (the window's `ToolWindow::{Completed, Stopped, Held}` outcomes) with `tool_step/seam.rs` mapping verdicts; the wire client is `src/prompt/tool/control.rs`; the mark is `src/workspace/hold.rs`; the parked-branch entry — pre-delivery mark check, resume, stale-mark sweep — is `src/prompt/dispatch/advance/held.rs`, and `run_exchange` parks through the same window outcome. The config home is `config/tool_control.rs` (§6). Proven end-to-end by test-fixture controls only (`src/prompt/tests/tool_control.rs`, `dispatch/tool_step/tests/seam.rs`): pass, refuse, and the full park → queue mail → out-of-band approval → resume → hand-off cycle.

**Deferred to v0.4+ (see §11).** Oversized-output auto-dispatch — raw output handed to a parsing subagent, only the compacted result reaching the parent step — is not in v0.3. The bounded projection above is the floor under that shim, not a substitute for it: the projection tells the model what it lost; the shim would hand the loss to a parser.

**Sandbox (v1.1, §3.6).** The v1.1 milestone wraps this contract in a capability sandbox derived from the artifact kind — a `wasm32-wasip2` component runs WASI-clamped, a native binary runs only under an `exec` grant. The stdin/stdout/exit protocol above is unchanged; the sandbox bounds *authority*, not *interface*. See §3.6.

### 3.4 CLI as control plane

`litany` is a single binary with subcommands. A **verb** is one such subcommand — one named operation with its argument set and its semantics (`new`, `config`, `prompt`, `dispatch`, `retarget`, `workflow`, `stop`, `message`, `scan`, `bundle`, `delete`, `replay`, `advance`, `tool`, `prime`). Every procedure the harness can start — child dispatch (§2.5), compaction (§2.7), verification, and any other workflow-invoked procedure (§6) — is a verb. The command surface — the set of verbs, their arguments, and their semantics — is the sole control plane: a procedure invoking another procedure does so by invoking a verb, never through a private function call reaching around it, shared memory, or ad-hoc sockets. Child dispatch, the canonical case, is `litany dispatch …`; messaging an existing agent is `litany message …` (§2.11), the same front door whether the invoker is the user, a frontend, or another agent's `message` tool. The surface is also where the agent **name** enters and is read: `litany prompt` and `litany dispatch` take an optional `--name`, and `litany message` resolves its recipient as an id or a unique name (§2.3, §2.11) — a frontend that shows names therefore reads the same committed fact the harness does, and never keeps a naming table of its own. The same two verbs take an optional `--cwd <path>`, the working directory the new agent starts in (§3.3) — the one channel by which a caller can place an agent outside its worktree, since the fact's other writer, the `cd` tool, only exists once the agent is already running. `litany retarget <workspace> <agent> [--config <name>]` is the surface of §2.2's change of lineage: it writes a ref mark and nothing else, so like every other user act it moves no branch — the agent's own executor lands the re-fork at its next step (§2.2, §2.3). `litany workflow <workspace> <agent> [--config <name> | --clear]` is the surface of §6's workflow mark — the same shape scoped to one fact: it writes (or deletes) the standing mark and nothing else, and resolution reads it at the agent's next step boundary (§6 *The workflow mark*).

**One command surface, two bindings.** The command surface is defined exactly once, in the library crate: the argument parser (the per-verb argument structs and the subcommand enum) and one **entry** function per verb that consumes the struct that verb parses into. A **binding** is how that surface is attached to a process, and there are exactly two. The **exec binding** is the `litany` binary — a thin argv shim: parse argv → perform the process-global preludes → invoke the library entry → perform the outcome it returns. The **linked binding** is a Cargo dependency (the motivating consumer is a linked frontend, §3.5) that constructs the same argument structs and invokes the same entry functions in-process. Parity of verbs and of parameters is therefore **by construction** — same types, same functions — not a convention two code paths must both honor. (This "binding" — a linkage of the command surface to a process — is distinct from a workflow event→action *binding*, §6; the collision is nominal only.)

**The library surface is exactly the command surface plus two enumerated seams — CI-enforced, both directions.** The crate exposes nothing public that is not a verb's entry, its arguments, its products, the **binding preludes** — the process-group-leadership and stop-flag mechanisms (§2.9) that either binding must perform identically before invoking a driver verb's entry — or the **mint seam** (`litany::mint`, since yog bl-aca4: the agent-name mint `mint`, its injected `Rng` trait, the `SplitMix64` production generator and `MintError`, re-exported so the linked consumer previews the very name a fire would mint through the one function, §2.3; the wordlist stays behind the function) — or the **host tool-injection seam** (`ToolInjection` and the three plain types its two methods speak in, the type of `Fx::tool_injection`, §3.3 *Host-injected tools*) — and no verb lacks its entry. This is the amended form of the "no third channel" invariant: the ban is not on *a* library API but on a *divergent* one. A library port that is definitionally the same surface is not a third channel — it is the same control plane, linked instead of exec'd. (§3.4 already sanctions in-process re-entry into the same argument parser as an implementation detail, below; the library port is that re-entry made public.) Parity is enforced mechanically under `make check` — hence in the pre-commit hook and in GitHub Actions alike, since both run it: verb↔entry and parameter identity hold by construction (shared types), and a parity check walks the crate's actual public surface and asserts a bijection with the CLI's introspected verb set. The spec fixes the invariant; the checker's implementation is not spec.

**Process effects stay at the binding.** The library performs no process-global or terminal effect: process-group leadership and signal-handler installation (§2.9), stdin/stdout binding (`tool`), the `$EDITOR` spawn (`config`), and `execve` of a successor (`advance`, §6) are the caller's side of the seam. An entry *returns* the effect to perform — the successor command to exec, the line to print, the outcome to enact — rather than performing it; the binding performs it (the shipped `advance` already returns an `Exec`/`Done` handoff its shim carries out, §6). A linked host that drives driver verbs in-process thereby assumes executor duties (§2.9 stop-cascade: `stop` SIGTERMs the executor's process group; §2.11's executor lock); a host that does not want those duties execs the binary binding instead.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-231c, bl-eb98, bl-4762).** The library port is built (`src/lib.rs` exposes exactly `pub mod cmd`; `src/bin/litany` is the exec binding) and the parity checker is `tests/command_surface_parity/`, which enforces the bijection at three depths: it pairs each `Command` variant with its verb module's entry *as function values*, so the compiler proves argument-type and product-type identity instead of a test asserting it (the "by construction" above, made a proof); it walks the crate's module graph and asserts that every externally reachable declaration — item, field, enum variant, method, derive, trait implementation — is a verb's entry, its arguments, its products or a binding prelude, with every `src/**/*.rs` proven reachable, `cmd` proven to hold no private module, and nothing below the surface allowed to name it; and it asserts per verb that the clap-introspected argument set is exactly that verb's public `Args` fields, by name, by arity and by named-vs-positional form. **`Fx::driver_target` is now the *one* re-entry path in the crate, and the library resolves no `current_exe` at all** (bl-eb98). Every seam that goes back through the front door reads it: the detached `litany advance` launch and the §6 successor `execve` (§2.11), the §3.3 tool resolver's third hop (`<driver_target> tool <name>`, `prompt::tool::spawn::SpawnTool`), and the `dispatch` / `message` built-ins' own re-entry (`SubprocessSpawner`/`SubprocessSender::with_exe`). This is §2.11's "the driver target is injected at the binding, not resolved by name" made total: under a linked host `current_exe` names the *host* binary, which carries no `tool` / `dispatch` / `message` verb, so any surviving `current_exe` would have been a linked-host correctness bug rather than a stylistic one. A consequence: §3.3 resolution is **total** — the third hop always yields a target, so there is no unresolvable-tool error; a name no binary answers to is declined behind the front door as an ordinary non-zero tool exit (`builtin::Error::Unknown` → `is_error` `tool_result`), which is what "Everyone uses the front door" implies. The **prelude-per-verb map is a query on the surface** (`cmd::Command::preludes() -> &'static [fn()]`), not a match in a binding: the library *names* the §2.9 mechanisms a verb needs and the binding performs them, so both bindings read one tested fact instead of each keeping a copy in step with it.

This is the invocation counterpart to §3.1. **Disk-as-bus carries state; command-surface-as-control-plane carries commands.** Between any two procedures, state flows through the filesystem (§3.1) and invocations flow through the command surface. There is no third channel — no *divergent* library API, no sidechannel. Whether a given invocation is dispatched as a subprocess `exec` or as an in-process re-entry into the same argument parser is an implementation detail chosen per-procedure (isolation needs, latency, resource cost); what is invariant is that the *interface* is the command surface — the same arguments, the same on-disk effects, whichever binding runs it. Internal procedures may not shortcut past it via a private function call.

Three consequences fall out:

- **Integration testability.** Every inter-procedure edge is an observable verb invocation. A test captures the arguments and on-disk effects, asserts on their shape, and replays outputs from fixtures without needing to mock in-process interfaces.
- **Embeddability.** Embedding litany in another tool is one of exactly two things: `exec("litany", args)` with env-var auth (the exec binding), or the exact-pinned linked binding — a Cargo dependency on the crate, constructing the argument structs and calling the entries. Both are the same surface; neither is a shared runtime, a plugin loader, or a separate API. The linked binding promises **pin-exact consumption only** — 0.x, no semver stability, the same posture brazen takes toward litany (§4.4).
- **No back-channels.** With disk for state and the command surface for commands — and nothing else — operational atomicity is structural: a procedure has no surface with which to back-channel into another. The single-author-per-file discipline (§2.6) and the per-procedure commit boundaries that make replay work are consequences of this, not separate protections layered on top.

"Procedure" here is the term from §6 (subordinate routines invoked by workflow), extended to cover the dispatch and compactor invocations already described in §2.5 and §2.7 — every named operation the harness can start.

### 3.5 UI contract

A **UI** (or **frontend** — same role, §3.2) is any program that presents the workspace to a user. The architecture admits multiple frontends concurrently against one workspace — a desktop GUI and a webclient rendering the same agent simultaneously is the default case, not a special one.
