The frontend surface is exactly two things, and nothing else:

1. **Filesystem reads.** The frontend reads and watches paths under the workspace. The load-bearing paths follow the layout (§2.2): the git tree itself (refs, commits, objects — branch state is read from `refs/heads/` per §2.3, config commits' control files among them); the workspace-root step records under `steps/<agent-id>/NNN/` (§2.3); the workspace-root inboxes under `inbox/<agent-id>/` (§2.11); and each agent's worktree contents (`goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, `summary/`, `messages/`, `descriptions/`, `skills/`). Notification is inotify where available, polling otherwise (§3.1).

   **Streaming text watch path.** Live model-call output is tailed at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/<NNN>/response.json` — the JSONL stream of canonical events (§4.4) is appended event-by-event as the adapter writes it. **Completion signal: writer closes the fd; inotify `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` marks the response complete.** The frontend tails the file (offset-tracking line read), and on `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` flips the response from "in flight" to "done" — no separate sentinel file, no out-of-band marker. This is the same fd-close convention used elsewhere in the architecture (e.g. atomic-rename writes, where `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` on the temp path precedes the rename); here the streaming append *is* the writer, so close-of-fd directly signals end-of-stream.

   **Agent-state classification.** The agent states the live view renders (§7.1) are derived from the executor lock, refs, and the JSONL terminal event — never a sidecar marker. `live`: the agent's executor holds its lock (§2.11). `in_flight` (a sub-state of live): the latest step's `response.json` is still open (no `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` yet). The harness holds this fd open across *all* of a model call's attempts and the backoff sleeps between them (§4.4 "Fd held open for the whole model call"), so `in_flight` covers the entire retry loop — an intermediate failed attempt's trailing `end`, or a mid-retry `Error` segment, is still `in_flight`, never `stopped`, because the file is still open. The quiescent classifications are evaluated only with no lock held and the fd closed. `quiescent`: the latest step's `response.json` ends with `end` — a finished-for-now agent awaiting a message (§2.4); for a child, its return is its result deposit, visible in the parent's inbox or transcript (§2.6). `stopped`: no lock, closed, and the last JSONL line is not `end` (§2.9 — kill, crash, and explicit stop are indistinguishable on disk; a closed file whose last segment carries an `Error` event is a *failed* step per §2.10, rendered as stopped with the error surfaced). `declined-transfer` (`refs/litany/conflicted/<agent-id>`, §2.6) and `budget-exhausted` (`refs/litany/budget-exhausted/<branch>`, §6) are orthogonal ref-derived marks, rendered alongside. A frontend reads the mark kinds it renders **by name**, never the whole `refs/litany/` root: that root also carries marks which are not states — `cwd` (§3.3) is the agent's working directory and `retarget` (§2.2) is a pending re-fork target, values rather than assertions — so enumerating it generically would render facts as conditions.
2. **Command-surface invocations.** The frontend issues user actions by invoking the command surface (§3.4) — either binding: `exec`'ing `litany <subcommand>`, or, when linked as a Cargo dependency, calling the same verb's library entry in-process. New prompt, message, stop, fork-from-history — all are ordinary verbs per §3.4. There is no *separate* API surface (the linked binding is not separate — it is the same command surface, held to it by CI-enforced parity, §3.4), no socket, no shared input directory. There is no user-facing "resume": continuing a stopped or quiescent agent is `litany message` (the deposit starts a driver, §2.11), and exploring an alternative is fork-from-history (new agent off any commit), per §2.3, §2.9.

Frontends hold no persistent state. Everything a frontend renders is derived from the filesystem at the current git ref; ephemeral UI state (cursor position, scroll offset, selection) lives in memory only and is discarded on exit. Restart is equivalent to re-reading the repo.

This discipline is what makes pluggability structural rather than aspirational. Two frontends running against one repo cannot corrupt each other because neither writes repo state; both observe the same on-disk ground truth, and both issue commands through the same CLI surface the harness itself uses. Swapping a frontend out is unplugging one reader; adding a second is adding another reader.

### 3.6 Sandboxed tools (v1.1)

Forward spec for the v1.1 milestone (§12). The sandbox is a **hosting wrapper** around the §3.3 tool contract, not a change to it: an external tool artifact runs inside a bounded authority envelope while still speaking the identical stdin/stdout/exit protocol. Everything §3.3 says about how a tool is invoked and how its output is framed holds verbatim; §3.6 adds only *with what authority* the artifact runs. In-process built-ins (`litany tool <name>`, §3.3) are subcommands of the trusted `litany` binary — they *are* the harness, not guests, and run with its authority; the sandbox governs only **external** artifacts (the `litany-tool-<name>` slot at `<data-root>/tools/`). Shipping a tool in-process is the decision to place it in the trusted computing base.

**Flavor is derived from the artifact, never configured.** The host reads the artifact's bytes and dispatches on format — there is no `sandbox:` field, no `native:` flag, nothing in `providers.yaml` selecting *how* a tool runs:

- A **`wasm32-wasip2` component** (WebAssembly component-model preamble) runs under a wasmtime host with WASI (WebAssembly System Interface) clamped to the effective authority below.
- A **native executable** (ELF or any host binary) is not a WASI guest and cannot be clamped; the only thing between it and the host is the `exec` capability. It runs *iff* the effective grant includes `exec` (below), with full host-process authority.

The flavor being a property of the artifact — not of config — is the severability line (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`): deleting the sandbox deletes no config field, because the choice was never a field. Removing a default deletes config, not code.

**Manifest, grant, effective authority.** Two inputs meet on five axes — **fs** (filesystem scopes), **net** (egress hosts), **exec** (spawn a host process), **clock** (read wall-clock time), **env** (host environment variables):

- The **capability manifest** is what a tool *declares it needs*. For a WASM component this is not a sidecar file — it is the component's own **WASI imports**, read from the artifact (a component that never imports `wasi:sockets` cannot open a socket; the manifest cannot be under-declared, because a non-imported interface has no host function to reach). Deriving the manifest from the imports is the single-source rule (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Single source of truth"): a sidecar manifest could drift from the actual imports; the imports cannot drift from themselves.
- The **capability grant** is what the **role** *permits* — a `grant:` block in the role's `providers.yaml` entry (§4.3), the ceiling for every tool the role may invoke. **Default: empty.** A role with no `grant:` runs tools with no authority at all, so only a pure-compute tool (empty manifest) loads.
- The **effective authority** a tool runs with is the intersection **manifest ∩ grant** — never a third stored field, always computed at load. Because the load gate below guarantees manifest ⊆ grant, the intersection equals the manifest: a tool gets exactly what it imports, and the grant is the ceiling that must contain it, never a floor that dilates it.

**Native is the `exec` grant.** A native artifact carries no WASI imports to clamp, so running it *is* spawning a host process with host authority — exactly the `exec` capability. A native tool therefore loads only under a role granting `exec`, and then runs unclamped. `exec` is the same axis a WASM component needs to spawn a subprocess of its own: one axis, one meaning — "may hold host-process authority." With the empty default, no native tool runs until a role explicitly grants `exec`; the wide-authority escape hatch is one legible, opt-in word.

**A tool asking beyond its grant fails at load, loudly.** The load gate is **manifest ⊆ grant**, checked when the harness resolves a role's toolset — before any model call. A tool whose manifest names an axis, host, scope, or mode the grant does not cover is rejected with an error naming the tool and the offending axis; it is never silently clamped to a crippled subset that would fail mysteriously at runtime (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Decline illegal operations": silent degradation is never preferable to a loud refusal). The check is total and static: a role's grant either contains a tool's needs, or the repo does not load.

**The grant grammar.** The grant attaches to the §4.3 role block as one `grant:` map — five keys, no nesting beyond a flat list per axis, no per-tool escape hatch, no freeform field to validate:

```yaml
roles:
  worker:
    provider: anthropic
    model: claude-sonnet-5
    tools: [bash, read_file]
    grant:                        # v1.1 — omitted means empty (no authority)
      fs:  [rw:.]                 # <mode>:<subtree>, mode in {r, rw}, worktree-relative
      net: [api.anthropic.com]    # exact egress hosts; no wildcards, no ports
      exec: true                  # may spawn / be a host process
      clock: true                 # may read wall-clock time
      env:  [PATH, HOME]          # host env-var passthrough allowlist
```

Each axis is exactly as wide as its audit unit and no wider: **fs** scopes are `<mode>:<path>` with mode `r` or `rw` over disjoint worktree-relative subtrees (§5.1), so `rw:.` reads at a glance as "read-write the worktree, nothing above it" (granting a parent subsumes its children — do not list both); **net** is a list of exact hostnames — wildcards and port syntax are rejected, because a wildcard host defeats audit-at-a-glance; **exec** and **clock** are booleans, since a host process and a clock are each all-or-nothing; **env** is an allowlist of variable names (the harness-owned `LITANY_CONV_REPO` / `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH`, §3.3, are the contract and always present — never part of the grant). The booleans-and-lists shape is not imposed; it is the natural granularity of each axis. If a grant needs a comment to be understood, the grammar has failed — the inline comments above annotate the *grammar* for this spec, not any real grant. The `grant:` block is the one new config knob the sandbox adds; it is the severability home for capability policy, and everything else the sandbox needs — the flavor, the manifest — is derived from the artifact.

**Boundaries — what the clamp does not solve.** The clamp bounds a tool's *authority*, not the trustworthiness of its code:

- **Supply-chain trust.** A malicious component granted `net: [evil.example]` will exfiltrate to `evil.example` — the host permits exactly the grant. The clamp is an authority bound, not a provenance check. The mitigation is the empty default plus the legible grant: authoring a grant is the operator vouching for the artifact, a decision made once and auditable at a glance. Artifact signing/provenance is out of scope (deferred, §11).
- **Escalation via chained tools.** A tool with `exec` already holds host-process authority; anything it spawns is that grant working as designed, not an escalation — `exec` *is* the ceiling, which is why it is one explicit opt-in axis defaulting off. A tool without `exec` can spawn nothing. Cross-tool data-passing through the worktree is bounded by fs scopes and by single-author-per-file (§2.6): sibling tools cannot target the same file.
- **Clock and env covert channels.** A granted `clock` permits timing side channels and a granted `env` var can carry data; both default off/empty, so the channel is closed unless a role opens it deliberately. The harness holds no provider credentials to leak (brazen owns them, §4.4), so an empty `env` grant exposes nothing sensitive by construction. The residual — that granting an axis grants its misuse — is the accepted cost of the grant, kept visible by the grammar.
- **Resource exhaustion.** The grant bounds *where* a tool acts, not how much it spends. Wall-time is bounded by the SIGTERM deadline (§3.3) and by budgets (§6, `max_wall_seconds`); a disk/CPU quota beyond that is deferred (§11).

---

## 4. Providers, Auth, and Models

### 4.1 Provider abstraction

The taxonomy (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §2) flags "provider" as one of the field's most overloaded terms, naming three distinct roles: **model creator** (trains the weights), **inference provider** (serves the weights over an API), and **gateway** (unifies multiple inference providers behind one surface). This document uses **provider** in the *inference provider* sense throughout: an (endpoint, auth) pair.

The harness does not speak provider wire protocols directly. Every model call goes through the **provider adapter** (§4.4) — brazen's `bz`, one external binary invoked as a subprocess per attempt. One binary serves every provider: which providers exist, their endpoints, their auth, and their wire dialects are **brazen's** facts, declared in brazen's own config (`~/.config/brazen/config.toml`, overridable via `--config`/`BRAZEN_CONFIG`; brazen's `config.md` is authoritative) as named provider rows. litany references a provider row by name and never reads endpoint or credential material — the row name is the entire provider surface litany sees. In the vocabulary of this spec, "provider" names the `(endpoint, auth)` pair (a brazen row); "provider adapter" names the binary. They are distinct terms of art and not interchangeable.

> **Historical.** v0.1–v0.5 shipped a bespoke per-provider adapter contract: one `litany-provider-<name>` binary per provider, `describe`/`complete` subcommands, an Anthropic-Messages-shaped canonical request, a home-grown event vocabulary, and env-var forwarding for auth and endpoints (`auth_env`/`endpoint_env`). v0.6 retires all of it in favor of brazen (§4.4). The externalization *principle* survives — the provider integration is still a separate binary behind a narrow stdio contract — but the contract is now brazen's canonical protocol, and there is one adapter binary rather than one per provider.

**Config split by lifetime and owner:**

- **brazen's config** (`config.toml`; brazen's tooling — `bz --dump-config`, `bz --login` — edits and inspects it). Provider rows: protocol, endpoint, auth mode, model aliases, per-row body defaults. Rotates with key rollover and infrastructure changes.
- **Global litany** (`<config-root>/models.yaml`, replacing the retired global `providers.yaml`; the config root is `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/litany`, §2.2). The optional `adapter:` binary override (§4.4 Extensibility) — and nothing else (bl-35e2: the per-model capabilities/context-window table is retired; no model policy ships or lives here). This sits under the config root, not the data root, precisely because it is hand-edited and must survive a reinstall untouched — the same lifetime as brazen's own `config.toml`.
- **Per-config** (`providers.yaml` in the config commit, §2.2). Role → (provider row, model id) mapping and role toolsets only. Resolved from the followed config commit (§2.2, bl-403b): a lineage advance reaches every agent on it at its next step boundary.

The frozen-bootstrap property is preserved: a config pins *which row and model* its roles use; endpoint and auth resolve at API-call time inside brazen, so rotating a key or endpoint immediately affects in-flight agents (correctly), and the config file never carries machine-local or secret material. Retry policy is not provider config at all — the attempt cap and backoff are workflow policy and live in `workflow.yaml` (§6).

**Row names are the portability contract.** Because a config pins provider *row names* — not endpoints or credentials — replaying or moving a workspace (or an archived bundle, §9.2) to another machine requires that machine's brazen config to define rows of the *same names*. This is the same expectation v0.3's per-provider adapter binary names carried (a repo naming provider `anthropic` needed a `litany-provider-anthropic` there, a row named `anthropic` here), now stated rather than left implicit. A row absent on the target machine is a load-time failure — brazen cannot resolve it and litany declines the model call (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Decline illegal operations") — never a silent fallback to a different provider. The row *name* travels with the workspace; its endpoint, auth, and model aliases stay machine-local by design (they rotate; the config split above).

### 4.2 Model abstraction

A **model**, to the harness, is a (provider row, model id) pointer — nothing more. Both halves live in one home: a role's assignment in the config commit's `providers.yaml` (§4.3). The row name selects `bz --provider <row>`; the model id rides the canonical request verbatim. The global `<config-root>/models.yaml` (the config root is `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/litany`, §2.2) carries only the optional adapter override — mechanism, never model policy:

```yaml
adapter: /usr/local/bin/bz        # optional override; default is `bz` on PATH (§4.4)
```

> **Historical (bl-35e2).** Through v0.6 `models.yaml` also carried a `models:` table — per-model `capabilities` and `context_window`, cross-validated against every role's `model:` at load. Nothing in the harness ever acted on either field, and the table shipped model policy in git: a hand-typed id validated only against another hand-typed line, which is exactly how the fake worker default `claude-sonnet-4-7` shipped unnoticed (bl-3157). The table is retired; a leftover `models:` block in an operator's file parses as inert. The harness stores **no per-model facts at all** — no capability registry, no context windows, no provider tokenizer; token budgets are workflow policy (§5, §6; the ~4 bytes/token estimate in `docs/TAXONOMY.md` "Token and tokenizer").

Model-id validity is brazen's concern, not litany's: `bz` resolves the id against its per-provider model cache (refreshed by `bz --list-models`) and otherwise attempts it verbatim on the wire. litany performs no model-list calls of its own and keeps no mirror of what the provider serves.

**No id reconciliation — the settled stance, and its smoke-test consequence.** A concrete id is hand-authored in one home — a role's `model:` in a config's `providers.yaml` (§4.3). litany reconciles it against nothing: there is no `bz --list-models` check at author or load time, and no capability-profile indirection that resolves the newest matching id from the provider's catalog. Both are severable enhancements deliberately not built (deferred, §11); id validity is brazen's fact. The consequence is deliberate: a wrong id — a typo, or a model that was never real or has been retired — is caught only at the **first live model call**, where brazen returns a not-found error and the harness declines loudly (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Decline illegal operations") rather than degrading silently. A live `litany prompt` is therefore the **required smoke test** after `litany new`: it is the first — and, by this stance, only — check that the authored ids actually resolve on the wire. (Every "shipped" claim that rode wire-mocked tests validated the harness, never an id; this is why the smoke test is a workflow requirement, not an optional nicety.)

### 4.3 Roles

A **role** is a pure **config-selection key**: the name under which a config commit bundles what a dispatch needs. The authoritative definition — including what a role does *not* solve — is `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §1 (*Role*); this section is its mechanism. The role name is the join key across the followed config commit's files (§2.2, bl-403b): `providers.yaml` `roles:` binds the model, toolset, and (v1.1) grant; `souls/<name>.md` binds the soul; `manifest.yaml` `roles:` binds the context-assembly policy (§5.2). Five bindings, one name:

```yaml
roles:
  worker:
    provider: anthropic
    model: claude-sonnet-5
    tools: [bash, read_file]
  compactor:
    provider: anthropic
    model: claude-haiku-4-5
```

- **Model.** `roles.<name>.{provider, model}`: `provider:` names a brazen row; endpoint and auth resolve at API-call time inside brazen (§4.1) — the config carries the (row-name, model-id) pointer, and that pointer is the whole binding (bl-35e2: no global table mediates or cross-validates it). Id validity is brazen's fact, caught at the first live model call (§4.2). This binding is what lets a cheap model serve compaction and an expensive one the worker.
- **Toolset.** The optional `tools:` list selects which tools the role's agent can call, from the on-disk tool pool (§3.3 Tools-list assembly). Omitted or empty means none. **The shipped `worker` row grants the whole pool** (bl-38c2): `worker` is the role every root resolves (*An agent's role has one runtime home*, below), so its grant is what an interactive conversation can call out of the box, and the data-root pool is the install's own declaration of what it provides. Granting a subset there is drift rather than minimalism — the shipped row read `[bash, read_file, load_skill]` while the pool also shipped `message` and `dispatch`, so no root in any workspace this harness created could message a sibling or dispatch a child, and the resulting refusals were twice diagnosed live as a model fault before the config gap was found. The correspondence is held by a test (`src/install/tests.rs::the_shipped_worker_grant_is_the_whole_tool_pool`), not by a runtime derivation: the list stays authored in `template/providers.yaml`, so narrowing a deployment edits config and deletes no code (the severability line, above). The compactor row grants none and is the deliberate exception — its pair is the procedure's, injected, never declarable (*The compactor's paired toolset*, below).
- **Soul.** Read from the governing config commit's `souls/<name>.md` by convention (§2.2) — the name *is* the path; there is no per-role path override and no freeform path field to validate. At dispatch time the harness copies it to the new branch's `soul.md` (§2.3 step 2).
- **Context-assembly policy.** The role's entry in the config's `manifest.yaml` `roles:` map — pinning, order, budget, overflow (§5.2).
- **Capability grant (v1.1).** A `grant:` block may sit beside `tools:`, declaring the capability ceiling — fs/net/exec/clock/env — for every tool the role invokes. Omitted means empty (no authority). The grammar and its manifest-intersect-grant semantics are §3.6; the grant is the one new config knob the sandbox adds, and it is the severability home for capability policy (removing it deletes config, not code).

**The role set is open.** A role is valid iff the governing config commit lists `roles.<name>` *and* carries `souls/<name>.md` — nothing else mints one, and the harness never enumerates role names. Any valid role is dispatchable by any dispatcher — the user, a workflow binding (`dispatch(<role>)` actions are cross-validated against the `roles:` map at config load), or the `dispatch` tool (which checks exactly this entry-plus-soul pair before spawning, §2.5) — and a child's role is unconstrained by its parent's: any role may dispatch any role, bounded only by its own toolset (dispatching requires the `dispatch` tool) and the tree's budgets (§6). Adding a verifier — the v0.7 success criterion — is a config commit (a `roles.verifier` entry plus `souls/verifier.md`), zero code.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-9e2d).** "`dispatch(<role>)` actions are cross-validated against the `roles:` map at config load" above is now **true of the code**, not just the spec. `config::cross::check_workflow_against_roles` was written and tested but reachable from no verb; it now runs inside `resolve_worker` (`src/prompt/resolve.rs`) immediately after `Workflow::parse`, against the `roles:` map read from the **same** config commit — so a workflow binding naming an undeclared role is declined at the load, before the first model call, rather than at whichever hop finally reaches that binding. The same pass subtracted the redundant file-loader variants (`ModelsConfig::load`, `Workflow::load`, `PerRepoProviders::load`) that duplicated the §2.2 content-in-hand `parse` seams: control is read from the config commit's tree, so a path-taking loader had no legitimate caller.

**An agent's role has one runtime home.** A dispatched agent's role is recorded in its dispatch commit subject (`dispatch: <role> [<id>]`, §2.5) and every reader derives it from there (§6 role-aware resolution; no sidecar role table — `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth). A root records no role and resolves the `worker` default — roots are workers — so a config that starts root agents must define `worker`.

**A role carries no behavior; the closed vocabulary is the one bounded exception.** Execution semantics never branch on a role name, with one exception that is the §6 severability line restated: the workflow interpreter's closed event/action vocabulary is code, and that vocabulary references three role names — `worker` (the root default, and the baseline `worker_return` event every role without a vocabulary entry resolves its return to), `compactor` (`compactor_return` → the compaction landing), and `verifier` (the verdict derivation, `verifier_approve` / `verifier_reject`). These are **not a second kind of role** — "primitive role vs config role" is dissolved, not adopted. Each of the three is an ordinary `roles:` entry whose model, soul, toolset, and grant are config like any other's; what is code is the interpreter vocabulary that *names* them, exactly as an action is code while a binding is config (§6). A role name the vocabulary does not mention is fully functional: dispatchable, resolvable, its return delivered through the `worker_return` baseline.

**The compactor's paired toolset is the procedure's, not the role's.** `write_summary` / `mark_for_deletion` are never declared in a `tools:` list and never ride `descriptions/**`: the step injects them for the compactor role alone (§2.7). They belong to the **compaction landing** (§2.6) — its deletion-only instrument — and the deletion-only guarantee ("worst case is lost, never corrupted, information") is structural precisely because the pair is not config: a `tools:` home would hand the procedure's safety property to the config author. The compactor role's *config* toolset (`tools:`, empty in the shipped template) remains ordinary config, exactly like its model and soul. What the compactor's request *declares* is wider than the pair — the inherited transcript's tools ride the array so the wire holds (§2.7, §3.3 closure) — and what it may *call* is exactly the pair; the safety property is the second fact, not the first.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-f72b).** The front door accepts any config-defined role. `litany dispatch <role>` (`src/prompt/dispatch_cli.rs`) enumerates no role names: it **pre-flights the open-set validity check** — role listed in the calling branch's governing config commit's `providers.yaml` `roles:` *and* carrying `souls/<role>.md` in the same tree — **before the fork**, so a rejected role leaves no branch debris, and refuses an undefined role with a config-validation error naming the config commit consulted (never a hard-coded "unknown role"). The check has **one home**, `src/prompt/role/validate.rs` (`role::validate::validate`), which both the model-facing `dispatch` built-in — projecting its `Invalid` verdict onto the tool's typed error (`src/prompt/tool/builtin/dispatch/validate.rs`) — and the CLI consult: the two paths now give one answer, so a role the tool validates no longer fails at the CLI it re-enters. `--goal` is required for every role except the compactor, whose goal is the §2.7 procedure-generated boilerplate — the one role special-cased, and only for the goal rule; the §6 workflow interpreter's closed `worker`/`compactor`/`verifier` vocabulary is unrelated to dispatch validity (the severability line above). A verifier the config adds (a `roles.verifier` entry plus `souls/verifier.md`) is thus dispatchable through the CLI with **zero code** — the v0.7 criterion realized on the front door, proven end-to-end (`src/prompt/dispatch_cli.rs` unit tests, `tests/dispatch_worker_e2e.rs`). The workflow interpreter still dispatches its verifier via the `child_dispatch` primitive directly (§6 gate), a per-procedure exec-vs-in-process choice §3.4 permits; the CLI now agrees with that primitive's open validity rather than contradicting it.

### 4.4 The provider adapter: brazen

The **provider adapter** is [brazen](https://github.com/mudbungie/brazen) — one small, stateless binary (`bz`) that adapts every provider and wire protocol behind a single pipe contract:

```
stdin (canonical request, JSON) → bz → stdout (canonical event stream, NDJSON, one terminal `end`)
```

brazen's own specs are the authoritative contract. They live in the brazen repo (linked above) under `specs/` — `specs/architecture.md`, `specs/config.md`, `specs/providers.md`, and the per-protocol documents beside them (`specs/anthropic-messages.md` and siblings). **Every citation of them is written *brazen `<file>` §N*, never a bare section number** — a bare `§N` anywhere in this document is always a section of *this* document (§2.1). Four sections of brazen `specs/architecture.md` carry the contract this section binds to, quoted here so a litany reader need not fetch them:

- **brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3.1 *The canonical Request*** — the typed request struct litany composes and writes to `bz`'s stdin: `model` ("canonical alias; the Provider row resolves it (computed, not a 2nd table)"), `system` ("`None` = no system; `Some(vec![])` is the same path, not special"), `messages`, and `tools` ("empty = no tools; never `Option`").
- **brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3.2–3.4** — the `v=1` canonical event vocabulary (§3.2 there, *The canonical streaming Response (the Event taxonomy)*), the error event (§3.3 there, *Error — its own event, `retryable` computed*), and §3.4 there, *The one terminator*: "`Event::End` is the single source of truth for 'stream over' across **every mode except genuine `--raw` passthrough**. Every native terminator normalizes to exactly one `End`." That normalization is why `response.json`'s terminal `end` is protocol-independent (§2.3, §2.9).
- **brazen `specs/architecture.md` §8 *Error Model*** — "Every failure → `Event::Error(CanonicalError{ kind, message, provider_detail })` AND a POSIX exit code. Errors travel **in-band through the same Sink**, then exit is set — one path, no special 'error mode.' `retryable` and the exit code are **computed from `kind`**, never stored." This is what §2.10 and §3.5 rest on: litany classifies from the in-band `Error` event, never from the exit code, so brazen's sysexits table (0/64/66/69/70/77/78 plus 130/141/143) is **deliberately not mirrored here** — one home for that fact, and it is brazen's.
- **brazen `specs/architecture.md` §5.10 *The committed CLI surface (frozen at 0.1.0)*** — the argv/stdin/exit surface litany's invocation (below) pins itself to, frozen: "The leading positional argument — the first argv element not starting with `-`, or **any** argument after a `--` opts-terminator — is **always** the prompt. It is never a verb and never a mode selector. Control operations are **always** flags."

This section binds litany to that contract and records the division of labor; it deliberately re-specifies nothing brazen already owns. brazen is our own project: pieces the harness needs that brazen lacks are built in brazen, not worked around here.

**Invocation.** One `bz` process per **attempt** (§2.1): `bz --json --provider <row>`, canonical request on stdin. The model id rides the request; config resolution is brazen's (`--config` / `BRAZEN_CONFIG` / XDG — the harness sets `BRAZEN_CONFIG` only under test isolation). Streaming is brazen's default and litany never overrides it; a provider whose wire response is non-streaming is normalized by brazen into the same event stream. The v0.3 "synthesize JSONL from a non-streaming response" path is deleted — every attempt's stdout is already the on-disk shape.

**The vocabulary is linked; the data plane is exec'd.** litany links the `brazen` crate at an exact pinned version for the canonical *types* — `CanonicalRequest` and its content vocabulary, `Event`, `CanonicalError` — and builds requests as typed structs, which makes brazen's fail-open `extra` map unreachable (the typo hazard of hand-built JSON does not exist). This is a shared vocabulary, not a channel: no litany code calls brazen's `generate()`; every model call crosses the subprocess boundary, preserving §3.4's discipline. Two consequences:

- **`retryable` has one home.** Retry classification is `CanonicalError::retryable()` from the linked crate — a computed query litany never re-implements (§2.10).
- **Version skew is guarded.** At load, `bz --version` must equal the linked crate version; a mismatch is rejected — no silent downgrade (PRINCIPLES "Decline illegal operations"). `make install` installs the pinned binary (`cargo install brazen --version =<pin>`). **An adapter that is not there at all is the same refusal one step earlier**, and carries the same three things — the binary, the section, the literal install command at the linked pin — with the spawn errno as trailing detail; the classification is one function over the spawn `io::Error` (`ErrorKind::NotFound` and everything else), shared by the `--version` probe and every model call, so neither seam can drift into a bare errno. Under a **named adapter target** the version guard is skipped and the in-band event-schema handshake (`MessageStart.v = 1`) governs instead. There are two, one trust class — both are identity the caller vouches for: the `adapter:` override (§4.2), and a **binding-injected adapter target** (`cmd::Fx::adapter_target`, §3.4) — an embedding host naming *itself* (or another binary) as the adapter, the same injection philosophy as the driver target (§2.11: "injected at the binding, not resolved by name"). Adapter resolution is therefore one order, most-specific first: the `models.yaml` `adapter:` override, else the binding-injected target, else `bz` on `PATH`; the guard runs only for that default `PATH` case, when neither named target is present. The load-time guard is reached only from the mutating `prompt` path, so `litany --version` also prints the linked pin (`litany 0.0.1 (brazen 0.0.5)`) — a read-only probe (an embedding host's own capability gate, for one) can compare it against a live `bz --version` without spawning a conversation. Both readings derive from the one `brazen = "=<pin>"` line in `Cargo.toml` (`crate::prompt::brazen_pin`, above) — never a second hard-coded string.

**Attempts and retry — the harness's job.** brazen never retries (a stated brazen non-goal); each `bz` process performs exactly one HTTP round-trip. The harness owns the retry loop (§2.10): on an in-band `Error` event whose kind is retryable, it re-invokes `bz` with the identical request — the assembler is deterministic from the step's recorded `commit`, so no request drift is possible — up to the attempt cap and backoff declared in `workflow.yaml` (§6). One attempt ≡ one API call *by construction*, so "API calls per model call" (§8) is derived, never stored: it is the number of attempt segments in `response.json`.

**Backoff: the config schedule, floored by the provider's pacing hint.** The **pacing hint** is `CanonicalError::retry_after_seconds` — the provider's `Retry-After` response header in whole seconds (RFC 7231 §7.1.3), parsed by brazen from the non-2xx headers and carried in-band on the `Error` event, `None` where the header is absent or unparseable (brazen never fabricates one). The schedule in `workflow.yaml` (§6) — exponential, keyed off the attempt number — governs; the hint is a **floor on it, never a shrink**. So the effective delay before attempt *n+1* is `max(schedule(n), hint)`: absent hint, the config schedule alone, exactly as if no hint existed; a hint under the schedule changes nothing; a hint over it wins, because a provider that says "wait 30s" knows something the schedule does not and retrying sooner buys a second 429. This keeps one owner for retry *policy* — the workflow config, never provider config (§4.2) — while honoring the one transport fact the provider is authoritative about. It is a pure function of the failed attempt's own error, so nothing is carried across attempts and no state is stored — `Backoff::delay(attempt, retry_after_seconds)` in `src/config/workflow.rs` is the single home of the effective delay, and the retry loop (`src/prompt/dispatch/model_call.rs`) only sleeps what it returns.

**On-disk response shape: appended attempt segments.** The harness appends each attempt's stdout verbatim to the step's `response.json`. brazen guarantees every stream — success, refusal, or failure — ends with exactly one `{"type":"end"}` line, so the file is a sequence of self-delimiting **segments**, one per attempt. **Segment authority:** an `Error`-terminated segment is audit-only — the record of a failed attempt, contributing nothing; a `Pause`-terminated segment *contributes* its content, which the continuation resumes rather than replaces (§2.10 Resumable pauses — the provider's own contract: paused content is re-sent and continued); the authoritative content of the model call is the accumulation across contributing segments, sealed at the final `Finish`. With no server tools wired (§3.3) no v1 segment can pause, so the rule degenerates to last-segment-authoritative — but it is stated in the general form because the transcript writer (§2.3) acts on it mechanically. Reading rules (shared by §3.5 classification, metering (§6, §8), and replay tooling):

- *in flight* — the file is open (no `IN_CLOSE_WRITE` yet).
- *complete* — closed, last line `end`, last segment carries a `Finish` (any reason, including refusal) and no `Error`.
- *failed* — closed, last line `end`, last segment carries an `Error` event (retry budget exhausted or non-retryable); the branch is flagged per §2.10.
- *stopped/killed* — closed **without** a trailing `end` line: the writer died mid-stream (§2.9). Kill, crash, and explicit stop remain indistinguishable on disk and are treated identically.

**Fd held open for the whole model call.** The harness opens `response.json` once, at the model call's first attempt, and holds the fd open across *every* attempt and *every* backoff sleep between them (§2.10) — closing it only at step resolution, when the loop settles into `complete`, `failed`, or `stopped`. This makes fd-open the single `in_flight` signal and the four reading rules above **terminal-only**: they are evaluated once the fd is closed. While it is open the step is `in_flight` no matter how many `end`-terminated segments it already carries, so an intermediate failed attempt's trailing `end` never reads as `complete`, and a mid-retry `Error` segment never reads as `failed` — the retry is still pending and the file is still open. This is the same fd-open observation §3.5 classifies `in_flight` on and metering derives from (§6, §8). `litany stop`'s pid discovery no longer rides this fd — it scans the executor-lock fd instead (§2.9, §2.11), the whole-loop liveness signal, so a stop lands during tool execution when no `response.json` is open; `response.json`'s fd stays the narrower which-model-call-pid signal.

**Refusal is a completed model call, not a failure.** brazen surfaces a provider refusal as `Finish{Refusal}` on HTTP 200, exit 0 (brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3.2) — its own truth, never an `Error` — so a refusal segment classifies *complete* (above), with no retry and no operator flag. A root agent surfaces the refusal as its terminal response, exactly like any other terminal `Finish`. A child's refusal reaches its parent through the ordinary path: it is the terminal response its result message carries (§2.6) — no distinct status, because a refusal is a normal completion the parent agent reasons about, not a harness fault. The workflow layer needs no refusal-specific event; the ordinary completion bindings (`worker_return` etc., §6) fire, and any refusal policy is a binding on that existing signal, not a new one.

**A `Finish{Pause}` segment is never terminal.** A paused model call (brazen `FinishReason::Pause`, Anthropic `pause_turn`) is continued *within the same step* (§2.10 Resumable pauses), so the fd stays open across the continuation and the segment always reads *in flight* by the rule above — the classifier never sees `Pause` as a settled state. Pause arises only with provider server tools, which litany's stdio tool contract (§3.3) does not wire, so it cannot occur in v1; the rule is stated so the classification is total.

**Errors.** brazen surfaces every failure in-band as an `Error` event on stdout and also sets a sysexits exit code computed from the same fact. The event is authoritative: the harness classifies from the parsed `CanonicalError` and treats the exit code as diagnostic. A `bz` process that dies without emitting a trailing `end` is the kill signature above — handled by §2.9/§2.10, never delivered to the model.

**Stderr is captured, not discarded.** The in-band contract covers only failures `bz` lives long enough to report. A `bz` that dies *before* it — a malformed brazen config is the canonical case — writes nothing to stdout and everything to stderr, which on disk is the kill signature above with an empty `response.json`. So the harness captures the adapter's stderr on every attempt (concurrently with stdout, so a chatty adapter cannot deadlock against the harness tailing the stream) and appends it to the step's `stderr.log` (§2.3). When a segment ends with no terminal `end`, the surfaced error quotes that attempt's stderr tail and names the artifact; an empty capture is itself the answer — a genuine mid-stream kill. This composes with §2.9 rather than competing with it: a stop-pending half-stream is the *expected* stop signature, and the executor's §2.9 check point discards the outcome without rendering it, so the stderr quote is only ever surfaced with **no stop pending**. The flag classifies; the capture explains what the flag says nothing about.

**Cancellation.** brazen installs no signal handlers: SIGTERM kills `bz` at once (default disposition, exit 143), dropping the in-flight HTTP request; already-flushed NDJSON lines stay valid. The missing trailing `end` on the closed fd *is* the stop signature — no flush deadline, no cancel marker, determinism via absence of mechanism. (The 5-second SIGTERM→SIGKILL deadline remains the contract for *tools*, §3.3, which may need to flush real work.)

**Auth and endpoints.** Entirely brazen's: provider rows carry auth mode and endpoint; credentials live in brazen's 0600 credstore; interactive flows are `bz --login`, operator-run, never harness-run — the harness never prompts and never sees credential material. The v0.3 `auth_env`/`endpoint_env` forwarding machinery is retired with the rest of the bespoke contract.

**Every in-band failure names the provider row, and a credential failure names the fix (bl-7e9e).** Owning auth is not the same as owning the *decline*. brazen reports a failure it can describe wire-accurately but can only address as `<id>` — which name litany routed this model call under is litany's fact, a role's `provider:` in the governing config commit's `providers.yaml` (§4.3), invisible on brazen's side of the pipe. So the harness's rendering of a settled `Error` event states the row: `provider error (<kind>) on provider row "<row>": <message>`. A workspace binds several rows across its roles, and a decline naming none of them tells an operator nothing about where to look. The **`auth` kind** — what brazen normalizes a 401/403 into — is split out and carries a remedy rather than a classification: the row named inside `bz --login --provider <row>`, the alternative of that row's API-key env var, `bz --list-providers` for every row's auth mode and credential state, and where the binding is declared. It is split for the same reason the missing-adapter error is split from a bare spawn errno: it is the one provider failure a first-run install is certain to hit and can fix unaided, and the harness stays inside its own knowledge doing it — it names a row and a command, never credential material. Where the line lands follows §2.11: an operator-run `litany prompt` puts it on the terminal, a detached driver into `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/driver.log`.

**Fit with disk-as-bus (§3.1).** Unchanged in shape: the adapter's stdin and stdout are pipes; the harness mirrors the assembled canonical request to the step's diagnostic `request.json` (one per step — attempts share the same assembled request) and appends events to `response.json`. Both remain outside every worktree and outside context assembly (§2.3); the terminal-line classification above reads only event framing from `response.json`'s tail — the same observation the frontend makes (§3.5). Because the pinned `CanonicalRequest` sets no `skip_serializing_if`, unset options serialize as explicit `null`, so the mirrored `request.json` shows `"stream":null`, `"temperature":null`, and the like — this is consistent with "litany never overrides `stream`" (Invocation): `null` *is* the non-override, and brazen's `fill_absent` resolves it to the configured default. The pipes are the wire; the disk is the record.

**Extensibility.** A new provider on a supported protocol is a brazen config row — no code anywhere. A new wire protocol or auth mode is a contribution to brazen. The escape hatch for a deployment that cannot ship through brazen is the `adapter:` override in `models.yaml` (§4.2): any binary honoring the same pipe contract — canonical request in, `v=1` events out, one `end` — slots in verbatim, with the in-band `MessageStart.v` handshake as its compatibility gate.

---

## 5. Context Assembly

### 5.1 The worktree invariant

**Everything composed into an agent's prompt comes from that branch's worktree — and the role's manifest entry picks which of it does.** Two statements, and only the first is an invariant: assembly has one input, the read-state commit's tree, and there is no second — no out-of-band preamble, no environment block, no path outside the worktree root, and no fact from anywhere but the tree. (Framing is the harness's: a `<file path="…">` wrapper, the `<goal>` block, the identity sentence the `name` derives, §2.8. Every one of them is a rendering of tree bytes, which is why replay reproduces the request exactly.) What actually composes is exactly what the role's manifest entry names — its `pinned` paths and its `order` categories (§5.2) — plus the transcript tail, which is unconditional. **The manifest is the inclusion list.** A worktree file neither list matches composes into nothing, and a role absent from the manifest's `roles:` map assembles transcript-only (§5.2).

The division is clean: **the worktree bounds what may compose; the manifest decides what does.** The bound is structural — control lives in the governing config commit, whose harness-facing files the dispatch commit removes from the agent's tree (§2.2, §2.3 step 2), and `steps/` / `inbox/` live at the workspace root, so no worktree-relative pattern can even name them. The selection is policy, and it lives in config where policy belongs (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Testability via configuration): what a role pays tokens for is an operator's edit to `manifest.yaml`, not a rule compiled into the assembler.

> **Correction (bl-b415).** Earlier revisions of this section asserted the stronger form — *everything* in a worktree composes, so the manifest "sequences and budgets" but never includes. The shipped assembler has never done that, and the stronger form is not wanted: it bills every role for every artifact its agent writes, and would immediately need the exclusion list it was written to abolish — the filter rebuilt inside the core, where the manifest already puts it in severable config. Sections that leaned on the stronger form (§2.2's control-files-leave argument, §2.7's compactor view, §3.3's descriptor cut, `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Context has one home") are restated against the honest one.

Consequences:

- A file reaches the model only if it is **both** in the worktree and named by the role's manifest entry. Neither half alone suffices, so a new context-bearing path is not delivered by writing it into the tree: the roles that must see it have to pin or order it. An implementer reasoning about what a fork sees checks both.
- Work products are ordinarily *not* in context. Under the shipped `worker` entry (pinned `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `descriptions/**`; order `summary/**`, `skills/**`, §5.2) a `feature.txt` the agent writes is in the tree and out of the prompt. (`name` is the exception that proves the split: it composes into every model call, but through the system slot's structural home — §2.8 — not because any list names it.) The cost is small because an agent reaches its own files with `read_file` / `bash` at the moment it wants them; the cost is not zero for a role whose grant has no read tool, which is why such a role's entry must name everything its goal tells it to read (the compactor, §2.7).
- Agents curate their own context by `rm` (§5.4). Deletion is the direction that always works: a file removed from the tree drops out of every selection, whatever the manifest says. The primitive they need already exists in the filesystem.
- The compactor's `mark_for_deletion` operates on worktree paths and takes effect on the next assembly.
- Control files (`manifest.yaml`, `workflow.yaml`, `providers.yaml`, `souls/`, `version`) are read from the immutable config commit (§2.2) and are absent from every agent worktree — never composed. This is structural, not disciplinary: the path is not under the worktree root, so no pattern can reach it. (`steps/` and `inbox/` are outside the same way, by living at the workspace root, §2.2.)

The one-input half is total now that the transcript lives in the worktree (§2.3): the agent's own model-facing history — assistant output, tool results, delivered messages — is worktree data like everything else. Nothing context-bearing lives anywhere but the branch's tree, so assembly is a pure function of the read-state commit (§2.10) with no second input — the manifest itself resolves from that commit's derived config ancestor (§2.2), so even the selection needs no separate input. Assembling a running step and replaying an old one are the same operation. `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Disk-first stops having an exception to explain.

### 5.2 Assembly rules

> **Shipped-state note (bl-e0cb) — this section is implemented.** `src/prompt/dispatch/assembler.rs` (with its `body` submodule) composes the §5.5 parts in order — the manifest head/body, then the transcript tail — and both step drivers pass the role's rules, read from the governing config commit by `src/prompt/resolve.rs` through the content-in-hand `Manifest::parse` seam (§2.2 — the path-taking `Manifest::load` is subtracted). The claims that leaned on manifest-driven assembly (the §5.5 rebuild points, §2.7's "category order reapplied at the next assembly") now describe running code. Four readings this section under-determined, pinned by the implementation:
>
> - **Structural wire homes.** A path whose wire home already exists composes through that home, never as body text, and composes there whether or not a role's lists name it: `goal.md`, `name` and `soul.md` ride the system slot (§2.3 *Goal and soul are pinned files*; the `name` as the derived identity line, §2.8), `descriptions/tools/**` — and every `descriptions/skills/<name>.md` a committed `descriptions/tools/<name>.json` claims — rides the typed request's `tools:` array (§3.3 tools-list assembly), and `messages/**` is the transcript tail, never an `order` category. Every *other* pinned path composes as a path-framed head text block (§5.3: `<file path="…">…</file>`), ahead of the body, always included. That remainder is exactly Description-always' standalone skills (§3.3, bl-ae66): a `descriptions/skills/` entry no tool claims has no structural home, so it composes as an ordinary head block under the role's `pinned` — no rule of its own, and no new manifest vocabulary. It follows that a role reaches standalone skills only if its `pinned` selects them (the shipped `worker` pins `descriptions/**`; the `compactor`, whose toolset is built-in and whose context is the dispatching branch's work, does not) — composition is manifest-governed like all other head material, which is where assembly policy belongs.
> - **Budget scope.** `budget_tokens` covers pinned + body only. Pinned counts toward the budget but is never shed ("regardless of budget"); the transcript is outside the budget entirely, so the body is a pure function of inputs that change only at rebuild points (§5.5 body stability) and a growing transcript never evicts a summary mid-branch. The transcript's pressure valve is compaction (§2.7, §6), not assembly. Tokens are estimated at ~4 bytes/token (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` "Token and tokenizer") — an estimate by construction, since the harness carries no provider tokenizer (§4.2).
> - **Overflow policies.** `drop_oldest_summaries` sheds lexically-first `summary/**` body entries until the body fits (zero-padded names sort oldest-first, §2.7); a residue with no summaries left rides. `truncate` fills in declared order and cuts the first overflowing entry to the remaining allowance; `drop` stops filling at the first entry that does not fit. **`drop_oldest_steps` is subtracted from the vocabulary** (bl-7846), on the same argument and by the same means as `summarize` below: a policy names an act assembly performs *on material the tree can hold*, and step records live outside every worktree (§2.2, §2.3), so the policy named material that cannot be present and its arm could only ever be a no-op. Its stated warrant — backward compatibility with pre-v0.3.1 manifests — is void twice over: §10's pre-v1 clean break already *refuses* every pre-substrate workspace outright rather than migrating it, so no such manifest is reachable to be compatible with; and pre-0.0.1 no released config exists to carry (config schema `version` is still `1`, §10). Worse, riding it was compatibility in name only — it kept the word and silently dropped the behaviour, leaving an operator who asked for shedding with an unshed, over-budget body and no signal. So a manifest naming `drop_oldest_steps` is declined at parse, and the decline's expected-variant list names `drop_oldest_summaries` — the successor an over-budget body actually wants. **`summarize` is subtracted from the vocabulary** (bl-a1a1): every policy the manifest may name is an act assembly can perform on the tree it holds, and summarizing is not one — assembly is a pure function of the tree (§5.1) and cannot invoke a model. It is not re-homed as a trigger either, on three counts: model-driven shedding is already declared, once, in `workflow.yaml` `compaction:` (§6), and a second per-role home for the same fact would drift; firing it from assembly would require a pure function to emit an out-of-band signal into the step-boundary interpreter, new mechanism for a capability the checkpoint clock already has; and `budget_tokens` is the wrong gauge for it, since the budget deliberately excludes the transcript (above) and the transcript is what a compactor sheds. A manifest naming `summarize` is declined at parse, not silently ridden. Should budget pressure ever warrant driving the clock, its home is a further `compaction: trigger:` variant beside `every_n_commits` / `every_t_seconds` / `on_flush` (§6) — one place, config rather than a second policy vocabulary.
> - **A role absent from the manifest's `roles:` map** assembles transcript-only — the general path with empty inputs, since a manifest entry is not part of role validity (§4.3). The manifest *file* itself is a required control file like its siblings (§2.2).
>
> The §6 warrant derivation deliberately composes the transcript tail alone: body material must not read as user-side mail warranting a model call, and a no-op hop stays config-free (lazy resolution).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-c867) — the marker guard.** Assembly enforces the §2.6 marker-freedom promise at read time: a selected `summary/**` entry carrying a line that *begins* `<<<<<<< ` or `>>>>>>> ` — the labelled markers git writes on a content conflict — refuses composition as a hard error naming the path (`assembler::body::marked_summary`, `Error::SummaryConflictMarkers`), the same refuse-loudly discipline as the compaction-merge decline, and fires for `pinned` and `order` selection alike (the guard lives in the one shared `select`). Without it, a tree already carrying markers — written pre-guard, by an operator hand-edit, or by a `write_summary` payload quoting the literal strings — would compose into every subsequent model call on the branch, and §2.7's "can never happen" would be load-bearing on the write path alone. Two decisions the guard pins:
>
> - **Scope is `summary/**` alone, not every composed file.** `summary/**` is the one composed category under a marker-freedom *promise*: its only sanctioned writer is `write_summary` and the compaction landing declines any content conflict during the replay (§2.6), so a marked summary is a violated invariant however it arrived — context that is not what it claims to be. Every other category (`descriptions/**`, `skills/**`, anything a deployment adds) is authored content under no such promise, and a read-path refusal is costlier than the write-path decline: the decline loses one compaction, a composition refusal fires on every subsequent model call until the file is repaired. Refusing a skill asset that legitimately documents merge conflicts would hold the branch hostage to a stray asset — the same argument that keeps non-UTF-8 composition lossy rather than fatal (§5.2 shipped-state, bl-e0cb; `assembler::body::select`).
> - **A bare `=======` line never trips the guard.** Git never writes the separator without the flanking labelled pair, so matching it alone adds no detection power over git-written corruption — and it is a legitimate setext heading underline (markdown's title-then-`===` heading form) in model-authored markdown, so matching it would refuse honest summaries. Only a line-initial labelled marker (`<<<<<<< <label>` / `>>>>>>> <label>`, seven characters then a space) is git's; quoted or indented marker text is prose about markers, not markup.
>
> Recovery is §5.4 deletion: repair or delete the marked file and the next assembly composes clean. Pinned by `assembler/body/tests/markers.rs`.

The manifest (`manifest.yaml` in the governing config commit, §2.2; role-keyed) declares ordering, pinning, budget, and overflow policy:

```yaml
roles:
  worker:
    pinned:
      - goal.md
      - soul.md
      - descriptions/**
    order:
      - summary/**
      - skills/**
    # The transcript (messages/**) is not an order category: it always
    # assembles last, in sequence order (§2.3, §5.5).
    budget_tokens: 150000
    overflow: drop_oldest_summaries
  compactor:
    pinned:
      - goal.md
      - soul.md
    order:
      - summary/**
    # The compactor's inherited worktree — forked off the checkpoint
    # commit — carries the dispatching branch's transcript, summaries,
    # and work products (§2.3, §2.6, §2.7); this entry selects the
    # summary chain, and the transcript tail rides unconditionally.
    # Together they are the branch's history, which is the compactor's
    # whole subject (§2.7). The work products in that tree compose
    # nowhere (§5.1), deliberately. Selection is per-role policy; a
    # deployment that wants more in view adds the category here.
    budget_tokens: 50000
    overflow: truncate
```

Paths are interpreted relative to the branch's worktree, and the manifest can reach nothing else by construction (§5.1). Between them the two lists are the inclusion filter: **a worktree path neither `pinned` nor `order` matches composes into nothing** (§5.1). Pinned paths are always included regardless of budget; `order` entries fill the body in declared order until overflow policy kicks in. The transcript (§2.3) is not an `order` category: it always assembles after the body, in sequence order, so the raw recent history sits nearest the next model call and the assembled prompt stays append-only between compactions (§5.5).

**Step records are not context.** `worker.order` (and any other role's manifest entries) MUST NOT reference `steps/**` — step records live at `<workspace>/steps/<agent-id>/NNN/`, outside every worktree (§2.2, §2.3), and are diagnostic-only (§2.3 Diagnostic-only contract). They are physically excluded from context assembly by their location: a worktree-relative path cannot resolve to them. Examples in this doc and the shipped template manifest reflect that constraint.

This corresponds to the LangChain write/select/compress/isolate taxonomy (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §3): **write** = commits; **select** = manifest inclusion; **compress** (here: compact) = compactor; **isolate** = child-agent branches.

### 5.3 File path as hint

File paths are preserved in the assembled context as structural hints to the model. The path itself carries information (`summary/003.md`, `skills/git-ops/SKILL.md`, `descriptions/tools/bash.json`) that is cheaper than explicit metadata and often sufficient.

### 5.4 Removal by deletion

Agents reduce their own context by deleting files from the worktree — work products and transcript entries alike (§2.3). An agent that no longer needs a 5k-token file `rm`s it; the next context assembly excludes it naturally (per §5.1 — if it's not in the worktree, it's not in the prompt). Deleted files remain recoverable from git history until compaction squashes them — the landing's compaction base drops the span's commits from the branch's reachable history (§2.6), which is what keeps a transcript-bearing repo from bloating under large committed tool outputs; the squashed history stays on the compactor's ref until retention (§9.2). Deletion is priced: removing a file invalidates the provider's cached prefix from that entry's position onward (§5.5) — legal, paid for at the wire, and the reason bulk curation belongs to compaction. The price is position, not mechanism: rebase-forward changes compaction's *availability* (zero downtime, §2.6), never this price — any compaction truncates the cached prefix at the compaction point, and the replayed tail sits after the truncation, re-sent like any fresh bytes.

### 5.5 Append-only assembly and the prompt cache

Provider prompt caching is prefix-based (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §8): a request whose leading bytes match a previous request's re-reads the cached prefix at a fraction of the cost. The assembly rules are shaped so that, between compactions, each step's request is byte-prefixed by the last. The assembled context has three parts, in order:

1. **Pinned head, frozen at dispatch.** `goal.md`, `soul.md`, `name`, `descriptions/**` — the system slot's files plus the manifest's `pinned` paths — are written or inherited at the dispatch commit (§2.3 step 2) and do not change on the branch. The head of every request on the branch is identical.
2. **Body, in manifest category order.** The non-transcript context — `summary/**`, `skills/**` — assembles in the manifest's declared `order` (§5.2). The body changes only at **rebuild points**: dispatch (the branch's first assembly) and the compaction landing (§2.7), the sole sanctioned reorganization. Between rebuild points the body is stable. (A mid-run skill load, §3.3, inserts into the body and pays the flush from its position — rare, heavy, and priced.)
3. **Transcript tail, in sequence order.** After the body, the transcript (§2.3) in filename-sequence order — *not* manifest category order. Every ordinary step event — assistant output, tool results, delivered messages — is an append at the tail, so the previous request is a byte prefix of the next and the provider's cache hits.

Deletion (an agent's `rm`, §5.4; the compactor's `mark_for_deletion`, §2.7) truncates the cached prefix from the deleted entry's position; edit-in-place is banned outright (§2.3). Compaction is where wholesale reorganization happens and the cache is deliberately flushed — it is the rebase-forward landing (§2.6): the compaction base deletes superseded transcript entries and lands the new summary at the compaction point, the live tail replays on top, and the next assembly rebuilds from the new tree. One flush, planned, at the moment the context is worth re-paying for; the flush runs from the compaction point, so a retained tail (§6 `keep_recent`) is re-sent, not cache-preserved — rebase-forward buys availability, never a cheaper flush (§5.4).

There is no history walk in any of this: the assembled order is a pure function of the read-state tree — pinned paths, category-sorted body, sequence-sorted tail — so the same readdir+sort produces the same request during execution, after a crash, and at replay (§2.3, §2.10).

---

## 6. Workflow as Configuration

Workflows are declared as event-to-action bindings in the config's `workflow.yaml` (§2.2), authored from a named workflow template at `<config-root>/workflows/<name>.yaml` — a pool `litany prime` seeds with the shipped default, `basic-agentic-loop.yaml` (§2.2), so the named default is there to read, copy or fork a variant from. An agent's workflow follows its governing lineage's current tip like every other control fact (§2.2 *Fork chooses the lineage*, bl-403b), and it is the one control fact with a per-agent override besides: the **workflow mark** (below, bl-f928), a standing pin that wins over the followed tip until cleared. "Workflow" here is the Anthropic sense (`docs/TAXONOMY.md` §1): predetermined code paths, as contrasted with LLM-driven agent control flow. Subordinate routines invoked by the workflow (compaction, verification, auto-dispatch on large tool output) are **procedures**.

```yaml
events:
  user_message:
    - dispatch(worker)
  worker_return:
    - dispatch(verifier)
    - gate_return_on(verifier.approve)
  verifier_approve:
    - deliver_result            # result message + work-product transfer (§2.6)
  verifier_reject:
    - "dispatch(worker, with: verifier.feedback)"
  worker_flush:
    - dispatch(compactor)
  compactor_return:
    - land_compaction           # the compaction landing (§2.6)
  branch_stopped:
    - mark_abandoned
    - notify_ui

  # Per-step hooks, fire on every branch.
  pre_step:
    - <actions run before a step's model call is issued>
  post_step:
    - <actions run after the step's model call returns, before tool execution>
  on_tool_return:
    - <actions run each time a tool call resolves>

compaction:
  intermediate:
    trigger: every_n_commits   # or: every_t_seconds, on_flush
    n: 10
    keep_recent: 3             # optional retained tail: the compaction point
                               # is HEAD~keep_recent (§2.6); must be < n
                               # under every_n_commits

retry:
  max_attempts: 3              # attempt cap per model call (§2.10, §4.4)
  backoff: exponential         # schedule; a provider `Retry-After` floors it (§4.4)

budgets:                       # v0.7 — spend limits (see "Budgets" below);
  max_total_tokens: 2000000    # omit a limit, or the whole block, and that
  max_wall_seconds: 3600       # axis is unbounded. Nothing ships bounded —
  max_depth: 4                 # the template omits this block entirely.

tool_output:                   # bounded transcript projection (§3.3)
  head_bytes: 16384            # per-stream head allowance, bytes
  tail_bytes: 16384            # per-stream tail allowance, bytes

tool_control:                  # the tool-control seam (§3.3) — omit it
  command: /path/to/control    # and no control sits in the tool path
```

Action strings that contain `: ` (named arguments) must be quoted, since YAML otherwise parses them as map entries. Bare actions (no named args) need no quotes.

Actions are implemented in the harness; the workflow declares which run when. The `flush` action emitted by a running agent triggers a compaction checkpoint without terminating the branch (§2.6, §2.7). This is the primary surface for experimentation.

**The workflow mark — the per-agent workflow override (bl-f928; operator ruling 2026-08-31, `docs/DESIGN_WORKFLOW_SWITCH.md`; composed with follow-the-tip by bl-403b, `docs/DESIGN_CONFIG_FOLLOW.md`).** The engine operates by workflows — named declarations of what happens at every step: the basic agentic loop, compaction, when to do what — and because a workflow only determines the next step, it must be switchable mid-conversation, with no migration and no restart, so alternative workflows can be tested against each other (§9.3's experiments are exactly workflow-config variants). The mechanism is **`refs/litany/workflow/<agent-id>`** — a **standing** per-agent ref naming the config commit whose `workflow.yaml` governs the agent from its next step boundary on, written by **`litany workflow <workspace> <agent> [--config <name>]`** (§3.4) at the named lineage's head and deleted by its `--clear` (removing the mark deletes config, never code — `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` severability). Resolution answers the workflow question with one derivation: *the `workflow.yaml` of the nearest workflow mark on the agent's descent — its own id first, then each ancestor — else of the followed config commit* (§2.2, bl-403b), which is the whole path every unmarked agent takes. Because there is no resident interpreter (below) and resolution runs fresh at every hop, writing the ref *is* the switch, effective at the next step boundary — retarget's own timing rule ("a config governs steps, never mid-step") with none of its machinery: no re-derived dispatch commit, no rebase-forward, no branch written at all. Five properties are load-bearing:

- **One home for the fact.** The mark stores no workflow content; content lives only in config commits, authored only by `litany config` (§2.2). The mark selects *which commit answers*, and "which workflow governs agent X" stays a pure disk query — "workflow position is derivable from disk" (below) is untouched, since the mark is policy selection, not position.
- **Standing, not consumed.** The retarget mark is a one-shot request, consumed at the boundary; the workflow choice is standing policy — a consumed mark would re-freeze at the next boundary — so it persists until re-marked or cleared, joining `abandoned` as a non-derivable per-agent policy assertion in the shared mark namespace (reaped by `litany delete`, archived with the refs, §9.2).
- **Inheritance by descent.** Nearest mark wins, so marking the root switches its whole tree — children dispatched before or after alike — and a child's own mark overrides its ancestors': the same nearest-wins shape as governing-config ancestry (§2.2), derived from the id string (§2.3) plus refs. The dispatch budget gate (below) reads the same derivation off the dispatching branch, so the ceiling a fork is refused under and the ceiling the child's own steps check are one answer.
- **The workflow fact alone.** Soul, `providers.yaml`, `manifest.yaml`, `descriptions/**`, grants and `version` still resolve from the governing config commit; the mark moves exactly the contents of `workflow.yaml` — the bindings, the `compaction:` clock, `retry:`, `budgets:`, `tool_output:`, `tool_control:`. A standing mark also *pins*: a later retarget moves the governing lineage and the mark still overrides it, the more specific assertion winning until cleared.
- **Validity precedes the mark; resolution declines the rest.** The verb refuses before writing (workspace, agent and lineage exist; the target head's `version` (§10) and `workflow.yaml` parse), and marking is otherwise unconditional — last write wins, and a mark naming the commit already answering is behaviorally identical to no mark, so no no-op arm exists. A marked commit gets its own §10 version guard at resolution, the `dispatch(<role>)` cross-check runs against the governing `providers.yaml` exactly as today (§4.3), and a mark aimed at a commit the resolver cannot read declines loudly rather than falling back.

**The basic agentic loop** is the shipped default workflow, named: `template/workflow.yaml`'s declaration — the one every workspace's `config/default` freezes at `litany new`, and the one `litany prime` seeds into the `<config-root>/workflows/` template pool as `basic-agentic-loop.yaml` (§2.2, bl-fcd8) — dispatch → step loop → deliver results, compact every 20 commits, retry 3, bounded tool output, nothing budget-bounded, no tool control. An unmarked agent runs it through the identical pre-mark code path, which is what pins "does exactly what the engine does today"; an A/B experiment is two config lineages and a mark. One shipped-reality note: the in-process root loop (`litany prompt`) resolves once per exchange, so a mark written mid-exchange takes effect from the next advance-driven step — the same latency retarget has there, dissolved by the prompt→advance collapse (the open simplification in the shipped-state note below; bl-e580).

Per-step hooks (`pre_step`, `post_step`, `on_tool_return`) fire on every branch and are the primary extension points for cross-cutting behavior — observability, budget enforcement, cache maintenance, scheduled compaction-checkpoint triggers. Their handlers typically dispatch children or emit log entries rather than modifying the in-flight branch's tree directly; any write still goes through the harness-assigned write-path machinery (§2.5).

**Budgets (v0.7).** `budgets:` declares per-agent-tree spend limits: `max_total_tokens`, `max_wall_seconds`, `max_depth`. The harness checks them at every model-call boundary, before invoking the adapter. Spend is derived at check time from the `Usage` events already on disk in `response.json` across the agent tree — no running counter is stored (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Single source of truth"). **Every attempt segment counts:** the derivation sums `Usage` across *all* segments of every step, not only the authoritative last one, because a failed or superseded attempt still consumed provider tokens and real money — the last-segment-authoritative rule (§4.4) governs which segment supplies *context*, never which segments are *billed* (§8). **The cached slice is billed once:** a step's tokens are `max(input, cache_read + cache_write) + output`, **not** the four `Usage` counters summed. brazen's canonical `Usage` reports each provider's own counters unaltered (brazen `specs/architecture.md` §3.2), and the providers disagree about overlap — Anthropic's prompt counters are disjoint slices (`input_tokens` beside `cache_read_input_tokens`/`cache_creation_input_tokens`), while the OpenAI-shaped and Google decoders map a prompt counter that **contains** the cached one (`prompt_tokens` ⊇ `prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens`, `input_tokens` ⊇ `input_tokens_details.cached_tokens`, `promptTokenCount` ⊇ `cachedContentTokenCount`). Summing the four bills the cached slice twice on three of the five protocols, and the inflation grows with the cache hit rate — worst exactly where a long conversation is cheapest; measured at +25% over one real agent tree (2,248,984 derived against 1,796,888 corrected), enough to exhaust a declared 2,000,000-token ceiling one model call short of the answer. Nothing on the `Usage` event names the shape and a step record carries no protocol (§2.3), so the derivation takes the larger of the two readings of the prompt rather than their sum: **exact** where the slice is contained, a **floor** where the counters are disjoint (it drops whichever of the uncached and cached halves is smaller), and plain `input + output` where no cache counter is reported. A floor and not a ceiling on purpose — spend is what was really consumed, and a phantom token ends a conversation before the limit its operator declared, which is the failure this rule exists to prevent. **Which counters overlap is the adapter's fact, not the harness's**: the fold collapses back to the plain sum the day brazen guarantees disjoint slices (brazen bl-d192), and until then it is the honest reading of an unnormalized contract. `max_wall_seconds` is likewise wall-clock: it counts the backoff sleeps between attempts (§2.10), since wall time elapses whether the harness is streaming or sleeping — derived by summing each step's `started_at`→`ended_at` span (one span already covers that step's attempts and backoff, §4.4 "Fd held open for the whole model call"). Tokens and wall are **whole-tree** consumables: they derive over the *root* agent and its entire descent (`steps/<root>/` plus every `steps/<root>-*/`, the §2.9 stop-cascade walk over the one shared `steps/` tree, §2.2/§2.3) — so any driver, root or child, reads the same whole-tree total by deriving against the root id (the agent id's root prefix, §2.3). They exhaust at `derived ≥ limit` (stopping before the next model call overspends). `max_depth` is instead **positional** — derived from the driving agent's own id, never from spend (the depth boundary is spelled out below). There is **no per-dispatch inheritance**: because `steps/` is one shared tree written live by the whole agent tree and never merged (§2.2/§2.3), a child reads the same whole-tree total its parent would, so nothing is handed down or snapshotted at dispatch and no parent-minus-child quantity is ever computed. (A future per-subtree cap — a `--token-cap`-style knob checked against a subtree's own spend — would sit *beside* this whole-tree ceiling, not inherit through it; it is not built.) Exhaustion is a harness-issued stop (§2.9) plus a `refs/litany/budget-exhausted/<branch>` ref — the same git-native marking pattern as the declined-transfer ref (§2.6). Because exhaustion is voluntary and harness-issued, the executor deposits the branch's result message with a `budget-exhausted` epitaph (§2.6, §2.3 step 5) before exiting — the parent is revived and told how the child ended, on the same channel as any return. Exhaustion is an ordinary terminal state, never a special transcript shape.

**Nothing ships bounded (operator ruling 2026-08-16, bl-8dea).** `template/workflow.yaml` declares **no `budgets:` block at all**, so a workspace born from the shipped template is unbounded on every axis — tokens, wall, *and* depth. The ruling is about **scope, not size**: a whole-tree ceiling is met far earlier than its number reads, because a root and its entire descent spend one shared allowance and a dispatch inherits no fresh budget (above), so the numbers that shipped — 2,000,000 tokens, 3600 wall seconds, depth 4 — ended ordinary agent trees that were working correctly, `max_wall_seconds` sharpest of all.
