The remedy is off, not generous: raising the numbers moves the same cliff rather than removing it, and an operator who wants a ceiling declares one. `max_depth` goes with the other two despite not being a spend ceiling — it is the tree's only prohibition on growth (below), so its absence is the one that can produce a runaway rather than merely an expensive tree, and the ruling removes it with the rest rather than holding it back. **This deleted config and touched no code path** (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` severability): an absent block and an absent key both read as unbounded, with no compiled-in fallback number anywhere — `Budgets` is three `Option`s under `#[serde(default)]` with a derived `Default`, and the check skips any axis whose limit is `None`. The bounded shape stays fully supported and fully tested; it is simply not declared.

**A workspace keeps its declared ceilings, and there is still no migration.** A workspace commits its own copy of `workflow.yaml` at creation (§2.2), and every agent reads the block from its *followed config commit*, never from the template. So this template change governs **newly created workspaces only**: every workspace already on disk keeps whatever ceilings its config lineage carries. No path re-reads the template into an existing workspace, deliberately. The exit is one ordinary config act, and since bl-403b it reaches running agents too: `litany config` authors a new commit on the lineage that drops the block, and every agent following that lineage reads it at its next step boundary — no re-fork, no per-agent act (a retarget is only for a change of lineage, §2.2).

**Tool output (bl-d5fa).** `tool_output:` declares the §3.3 *bounded transcript projection* — per-stream `head_bytes` / `tail_bytes` byte allowances applied to every tool result's stdout and stderr before the result envelope is rendered, with an honest marker naming what was cut and where the full record lives. Semantics, marker shape, and the never-cap-the-envelope rule are §3.3's; this block is only the policy's config home, read from the governing config commit like every other workflow fact (§2.2). Byte counts, never tokens (litany has no tokenizer). Omitted → unbounded, the pre-policy behavior; the shipped default (16 KiB + 16 KiB per stream) is `template/workflow.yaml`'s, severable without touching code.

**Tool control (bl-de6d).** `tool_control:` declares the §3.3 *tool control* — the adjudicator consulted before every granted tool invocation executes, answering pass, refuse, or hold. This block is only the seam's config home: one field, `command`, naming the control executable (an absolute path or a PATH-resolved name — the §4.2 `adapter:` idiom). Semantics, the verdict wire contract, the hold mark, and the fail-closed rule are all §3.3's. Deliberately no other knobs: per-tool or per-role scoping is the control's own business (it is told the tool and the role on stdin), so policy lives in the control and the harness carries none. Omitted → no control is consulted and the tool window is unchanged; `template/workflow.yaml` ships **no** control (the re-scoped bl-de6d ruling: wire the capability, not an implementation), so wiring one is config and removing one deletes config, never code.

**The depth boundary.** Depth is a property of the agent id, not of anything it spent. An id is a hyphenated dispatch descent (§2.3): the root agent is `<a>` and every dispatch appends one `-<ts>-<short>` segment, so **depth counts dispatches from the root agent, which is depth 0** — `<a>` is depth 0, `<a>-<b>` is depth 1, `<a>-<b>-<c>` is depth 2, and depth is the id's hyphen count divided by two. `max_depth` is the deepest **allowed** depth, so the comparison that exhausts is **strict**: an agent is depth-exhausted iff `depth(agent) > max_depth`. Three consequences follow and are the whole boundary. The root is never depth-exhausted, for any declared `max_depth` — depth 0 exceeds nothing. `max_depth: 0` is therefore not "no agents" but "the root only, no children". And an agent at depth `max_depth + 1` is exhausted at its *first* model-call boundary, before the adapter is invoked, so it makes no model call at all.

**The depth ceiling is the tree's only prohibition on growth.** An agent is an agent (§2.1 **Agent**; `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §3 "Conversation, thread, session, chat history", which deletes "subagent" as a *category*): a child holds the same powers as the agent that dispatched it — it is addressable by `message` (§2.11) and it may dispatch children of its own. `max_depth` is the one sanctioned circumscription on that, so how far a tree may grow downward is settled here and nowhere else. It bounds **depth only**: nothing caps how many children one agent dispatches at a single depth, and breadth is held instead by the whole-tree `max_total_tokens` and `max_wall_seconds` above.

**One gate, every dispatch.** The model-call boundary is where a budget *retires* an agent; it is not where a budget is *enforced against growth*. Growth has exactly one act — the dispatch that forks a child — and it happens on the **parent's** side, so checking only at the child's first model call lets the branch, its worktree and its inbox exist before anything declines them. Every dispatch therefore evaluates the declared `budgets:` against the child's own prospective id **before the fork**, and a breach refuses: no ref, no worktree, no inbox, no launch. The child id determines its depth positionally (§2.3), so the check is exact for a branch that does not yet exist. It is the same predicate as the boundary above, applied to the prospective child: a child that would land at `max_depth + 1` is refused before the fork, so **the deepest agent a dispatch can create sits at exactly `max_depth`**. The model-call check is not made redundant by that — it is the same rule at the other boundary, and it does not depend on the fork gate having run.

This gate is one function at one fork, and it makes no distinction between a dispatch the *model* asked for (the `dispatch` built-in, `litany dispatch`, §3.4) and one the *workflow* ran (`worker_flush: dispatch(compactor)`, the verifier gate and its reject re-dispatch, §6). A ceiling enforced against one kind and not the other is not a ceiling. The two differ only in whose failure a refusal is: a model that asked for a child it cannot have is told, in-band, as its `tool_result`; a harness procedure that cannot have one has met the operator's declared limit, so the refusal is reported and the dispatching branch steps on — uncompacted or ungated, which is already the §2.7 outcome for a compaction that does not land.

**No resident interpreter.** Nothing parses `workflow.yaml` resident-style and drives the state machine from memory. The chain is driven by a single CLI subcommand, **`litany advance <workspace> <agent>`** — the driver every §2.11 launch seam spawns (the writer's post-deposit probe, the `litany scan` flush, an exiting executor's self-directed launch), and the same verb an operator runs by hand. One invocation is one **hop**: a fresh subprocess that

1. **takes the lease** — adopts the lock fd its predecessor published (`LITANY_LOCK_FD`, below) or try-acquires the §2.11 executor lock; losing the acquire, it exits as a clean no-op (Writer/driver totality, §2.11).
2. **settles any crashed window, then delivers** — first, strictly before any mail moves: a markless unpaired *trailing* window is a window whose executor died (a live window's executor holds its lease for the window's whole life, and a parked window carries the §3.3 mark, adjudicated before this), and the hop settles it with one in-band `is_error` `tool_result` per unanswered id — the §2.9 settlement shape with the crash sentence: the executor died before recording an outcome, whether the invocation ran is unknown, any side effects stand (bl-4187). Pre-delivery ordering is the point: §2.3's pairing is positional, so a settlement appended after delivered mail could never compose wire-legal. Then delivery: rematerializes the worktree if quiescence tore it down (§2.3 step 6), recovers any renamed-but-uncommitted stray, and drains pending inbox messages as delivery commits (§2.11). An empty inbox over a torn-down worktree is the §2.11 pin-1 silent exit.
3. **derives warrant from the tree** — no launcher told it anything (§2.11: warrant is decided by the launched driver under the lock; the fd adoption changes how the lease is obtained, never what is decided). The derivation is the wire alternation itself (§2.3): assemble the transcript, judge the §2.3 pairing over the **whole alternation**, then read the tail. Any `tool_use` unmatched by a committed `tool_result` — *wherever it sits* — is **declined loudly** (below, *the one non-replayable state*): step 2 delivers before this step derives, so an orphaned window would be buried user-side by the time it is read, and the tail's role cannot answer for it — sent anyway, the provider rejects the orphan `tool_use` forever, which the operator experiences as a conversation that silently stopped answering (bl-15f0). Step 2's crash settlement means a fresh crash never reaches this decline — it is settled while still trailing — so what declines here is pre-settlement debris: a window mail got behind before the settlement existed (bl-4187). Only a fully paired history lets the tail speak: ends **user-side** (delivered mail, committed tool results) → a model call is due → run one step; ends **assistant-side without `tool_use`**, or empty → nothing is due → exit silently (§2.11 pin 1).
4. **runs the step** — the ordinary §2.3 step-loop body: stop check (§2.9), budget check (this section), context assembly from the tree (§5), the model call through the retry loop (§2.10, §4.4), the transcript commit, and the step's tool calls (§3.3).
5. **hands off** — a step that emitted `tool_use` runs its tools and **exec's the successor `advance`**: the exec baton, the lock fd deliberately inherited (§2.11, mechanics below). The successor *executable* is injected at the binding, not resolved by name (§3.4): the entry prepares and returns the successor command and the binding execs it — the `litany` binary re-execs its own image, while a linked host that carries the driver loop execs its own re-exec target (or a PATH `litany`), so a linked litany never depends on a `litany` binary being on PATH mid-chain. A terminal event instead ends the chain through the §2.11 exit protocol (deposit → release → epitaph-valued launch → exit); a `final-response` end's self-directed launch spawns a driver whose warrant derivation finds nothing due, which is what terminates the recursion (§2.11 pin 1).

The currently-executing `litany advance` subprocess *is* the interpreter while it runs — a baton passed forward by exec, not a daemon. There is no watcher noticing completions; each hop hands off by exec. Combined with disk-as-bus (§3.1), this keeps the system stateless across process boundaries: a crashed subprocess leaves nothing in memory to reconstruct, and the same `litany advance` invocation re-enters the chain by reading the repo and running the next action — recovery is not a separate operation, it is the same operation as advancing. This is the concrete mechanism behind §1's Regenerability property. (Warrant derivation deliberately never consults who launched the driver or why: a launch after a crash, a launch after a deposit, and a hand-run invocation are indistinguishable, which is exactly the collapse of "advance" and "resume" below.)

**The exec baton carries the lease.** The §2.11 lock fd is deliberately inherited across the advance→advance exec — and inheritance is deliberate work, not ambient behavior: the harness opens fds close-on-exec by default (Rust's stdlib sets `O_CLOEXEC`), so a naive exec would drop the flock and reacquire it on the other side, opening exactly the gap the inheritance clause closes. The handoff is the standard fd-passing shape (systemd socket-activation): immediately before exec — and only then — the hop clears the close-on-exec flag on the lock fd (`fcntl(fd, F_SETFD, 0)`) and publishes its number as **`LITANY_LOCK_FD`** in the successor's environment, and only there — every other spawn (the detached launch, tool subprocesses, adapter subprocesses) scrubs the variable. The `flock` lease lives on the open file description, which survives `execve`, so the lease spans the hop boundary with **no release-reacquire gap**: no rival driver can wedge between two hops of one chain. The successor **adopts** the fd instead of reacquiring: it validates it by `fstat`, requiring the same device and inode as the agent's inbox directory — a mismatched or unusable fd is **declined loudly** (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`), never silently papered over with a fresh acquire, because a wrong fd means a defective launcher and a fresh acquire would mask it — re-asserts the `flock` (idempotent on the same open file description), and restores close-on-exec so the lease never rides into the *exec'd images* of the tool and adapter subprocesses the hop spawns. Close-on-exec cannot keep it out of those subprocesses' pre-`exec` window — the fd table is copied at the fork and the flag fires only at the exec — which is why §2.11 makes release an explicit `LOCK_UN` on the description rather than a close of one fd naming it: the lease a spawn transiently duplicates is one the holder can still give up exactly. Because exec replaces the image in the **same process**, the pid and process group also ride the baton: a §2.9 stop aimed at the chain's process group lands on whichever hop is current, and within a chain there is no between-hop window in which neither a process nor a terminal event exists.

**The image reload is the point, not the price.** exec does not fork and does not change the pid: it replaces the image in place, so a whole `litany advance` chain is one pid, one process group, one `ps` start time, one command line, from the first step to the epitaph — the fork happens once, at chain start, and kill and stop bind to that group exactly as they would a plain while-loop process's (stop discovers the lease by lock fd regardless, §2.9). Externally the chain is **indistinguishable from a single loop process**; the *only* observable difference is whether process memory can carry state across a step boundary — and that difference is the entire reason to prefer exec. A loop makes per-step statelessness **disciplinary**: nothing forbids a cached transcript, a held-open writer, or a parsed config from accreting across steps, so the property survives only as long as every future edit is reviewed for leaks and none drifts. exec makes it **structural**: the address space is destroyed and rebuilt at every hop, so nothing *can* survive — the guarantee holds with no one maintaining it. This is the same move as deletion-only compaction (§2.6 — worst case is lost, never corrupted, information) and disk-as-bus plus CLI-as-control-plane foreclosing back-channels (§3.4): a class of failure deleted by construction rather than policed by convention (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Structure over discipline"). The reload is therefore not overhead to apologize for; it is an extremely strict state-safety mechanism, and naming it as a cost gets the sign backwards.

**Cold entry never becomes a mode.** Because the image is rebuilt every hop, the crash-recovery path and the steady-state path are literally one code path, exercised at every step boundary forever — not a cold-start branch that runs once per process and rots between uses. Re-entry after a crash reads exactly what a mid-chain hop reads (the repo and the transcript tail) and runs exactly the same action, so "resume" is not a distinct operation from "advance" (warrant derivation, above) and "bootstrap" is not distinct from steady state. This is the deliberate family resemblance to §2.3's *"replay" stops being a mode*: the same input drives the same code whether the process is fresh or continuing, so the rare path can never diverge from the common one — because it *is* the common one. Every crash recovery is thus rehearsed continuously by normal operation, which is the strongest test coverage a recovery path can have.

**The delta is a warm reload, and exec is reversible for free.** The disk re-derivation an in-process loop would supposedly save is paid in *both* models: workflow position is a function of disk either way ("Workflow position is derivable from disk", below), so a loop re-reads the same refs and transcript tail a fresh image does. The true delta of exec over a loop is therefore one page-cache-warm reload of an already-resident binary — single-digit milliseconds against a model call measured in seconds. And if measurement ever finds even that too dear, the escape is already sanctioned and severable: §3.4 fixes only the *interface* (the CLI), leaving exec-versus-in-process re-entry a per-procedure implementation choice, so swapping a hot loop in for the exec chain changes zero interface and zero spec. The asymmetry is the whole case: choosing exec costs nothing to reverse, while choosing an in-process loop *deletes the structural guarantee* and cannot recover it without standing review discipline. Prefer the structural default; reach for the loop only where a concrete measurement demands it. The same restraint governs single-step operation — "advance one step" versus "run to the epitaph" is one verb (`litany advance`) differing only in whether the current hop hands off, a continuation already decided from disk (step 3) — so no `--one-step` operator flag is built until a concrete operation asks for one; the signal lives in the tree, not in a new argument.

**The one non-replayable state.** §2.3's crash recovery covers death mid-model-call: nothing committed, the read state is unchanged, and the re-entered hop re-runs the step — replay and normal advance are one code path. Death mid-*tools* is different: the assistant entry committed (the tip moved) but some of its `tool_use` blocks have no committed `tool_result`, and tool side effects are not idempotent, so re-running them is not replay. The harness never re-runs them and never forges an answer for them; what changed at bl-4187 is *who judges the replay*. The next drive **settles** the trailing crashed window (hop step 2, above): one in-band `is_error` `tool_result` per unanswered id, saying the executor died before recording an outcome and whether the invocation ran is unknown. That is a truthful record, not a forged result — the same wording discipline as a refusal (§3.3) — and it puts the re-issue judgement where the context is: the model reads in band that the window died and decides whether the call is still wanted, instead of the branch waiting on an absent human's fork-from-history. An ordinary deposit then revives the branch (the §2.9 resume path, now uniform across stop and crash — and the §8 sweep's `died` deposit into the parent means a crashed *child's* obituary flows even while its own branch heals on its next touch). What remains **declined loudly** is the buried form — an unpaired window with delivered mail already behind it, composable only by pre-settlement histories (bl-15f0): appending a settlement behind the mail could never compose wire-legal under §2.3's positional pairing, so recovery there is still fork-from-history. **One unpaired tail is legitimate and not this state: a parked hold.** The §3.3 hold mark asserts the frontier invocation was held *before* execution — nothing at or past it ran — so a hop finding the mark re-enters the window (skipping committed results, re-adjudicating the held block) instead of settling or declining. **And a stop never produces this state**: the stopped exit settles its own tool window before it deposits (§2.9), committing an in-band interrupted `tool_result` for every unanswered id — the crash settlement is that same act performed late, by the drive that discovers the corpse, because a dead executor cannot perform it on the way out.

**Workflow position is derivable from disk.** The workflow's current position is a function of repo state — refs, step records, merge history, sentinel files explicitly named in `workflow.yaml` — never of a `workflow_state.json` or analogous sidecar that mirrors the position. Implementations may not introduce such a mirror. This is the invariant that makes the unification of "advance" and "resume" load-bearing rather than aspirational: if position were stored separately from disk state, then re-entry after crash would require reconciling the mirror against disk, and "advance" and "resume" would need to be different operations to handle the divergence. Holding the line on derivability collapses them.

**Procedures are idempotent under replay.** §2.10 commits to replay-from-recorded-sha for model calls. The corresponding requirement at the workflow level is that every procedure invoked by `litany advance` must be idempotent under replay from its recorded read-state, or detect already-completed work and no-op. Without this, two `litany advance` invocations against the same disk state could produce divergent effects, and the position-from-disk invariant above would not be sufficient to make recovery safe. With it, any number of `litany advance` invocations against the same state converge. Idempotence covers *sequential* re-entry; *simultaneous* drivers against one branch are resolved by the executor lock (§2.11 "Writer/driver totality") — one becomes the executor, the other exits as a clean no-op. (The mid-tools crash state is the pinned exception, settled rather than replayed — and its buried pre-settlement form declined loudly — *the one non-replayable state*, above.)

> **Shipped-state note (bl-4684).** `litany advance <workspace> <agent>` is shipped (`src/prompt/dispatch/advance/`): lease adoption-or-acquisition (`src/prompt/inbox/baton.rs` — `LITANY_LOCK_FD` parse, fstat validation against the inbox directory, flock re-assertion, close-on-exec restore), delivery through the real drain (`dispatch::driver::deliver`, the bl-5846 seam), the tail-of-transcript warrant derivation, the single-step hop (`advance::hop`, the same step machinery `run_exchange` drives — assembler, model-call retry loop, transcript writer, tool orchestration, §6 budget check, §2.9 stop check points), and the exec baton (the successor `Command` is prepared in the library — args, `LITANY_LOCK_FD`, cleared close-on-exec — and exec'd by the thin CLI shim). Every §2.11 launch seam now really spawns: `AdvanceLauncher` is the detached spawn (§2.11 — `setsid`, stdin/stdout to null and stderr to `steps/<agent-id>/driver.log`, `LITANY_LOCK_FD` scrubbed, fire-and-forget), wired at the writer's post-deposit probe, the `litany scan` flush, and the exit protocol's self-directed launch. One shipped-reality divergence from this section's fuller wording: a *fresh root* is still driven end-to-end by `litany prompt`'s in-process loop (`dispatch::run_exchange`) — §3.4 explicitly leaves exec-vs-in-process as a per-procedure choice, and both drivers share one step machinery; collapsing prompt onto "spawn + deposit + first hop" is an open simplification, not a contradiction.

**The binding interpreter (v0.7).** The event bindings above are *interpreted* by the hop, not merely parsed. This is the single reconciliation the ball proposed and the user confirmed ("matches one path, through the front door"): **`advance` is the only driver, and it is the interpreter.** The two families of binding in `workflow.yaml` are one evaluation seen at two positions of one hop, never two mechanisms:

- **Per-step hooks** (`pre_step`, `post_step`, `on_tool_return`) are **advance-native** — evaluated *inside* the step (§2.3 step-loop body): `pre_step` before the model call is issued, `post_step` after it returns and before tool execution, `on_tool_return` after each tool call resolves. They fire on every branch, every step, and are the primary cross-cutting surface (observability, scheduled compaction-checkpoint triggers, budget shaping).
- **Lifecycle events** (`user_message`, `worker_return`, `verifier_approve`, `verifier_reject`, `worker_flush`, `compactor_return`, `branch_stopped`) are **the same evaluation when the step reached a terminal or delivered a child's result**. A lifecycle event is not a second driver waking on a callback; it is a *circumstance the hop derives from disk* and matches against the flat binding list, exactly as the appendix's one rule is matched (§2.5 "The workflow appendix"). The agent holds no cursor: every hop re-derives its circumstance and runs what matches, so the workflow has no position and crash-replay re-runs the identical match.

**Circumstance is derived from disk, never dispatched to.** A hop names its fired lifecycle event by reading the tree, not by a launcher telling it what happened (§6 warrant derivation — the same discipline):

- The branch's **own terminal** names an event by its epitaph value (§2.6 — code branches on the value, never message shape): `stopped` → `branch_stopped`. (`final-response` and `budget-exhausted` have no distinct lifecycle binding; they terminate through the ordinary exit protocol.)
- A **delivered child result** (a drained inbox message carrying `terminal_ref:`, §2.6) names an event by the **child's role**, and the role is itself disk-derived — read from the child's **dispatch commit subject** (`dispatch: <role> [<child-branch>]`, §2.5), the one authoritative record already on disk (no sidecar role table, `docs/PRINCIPLES.md` Single source of truth). Role `worker` → `worker_return`; `compactor` → `compactor_return`; `verifier` → the approve/reject split below.

**verifier_approve / verifier_reject derivation.** A verifier is an ordinary child agent (§2.5) whose goal is to judge the work it was handed and whose *terminal response is its verdict*. The parent does not read a sidecar approval flag — none exists; it derives the verdict from the verifier's result message (§2.6), which is already on disk: the **epitaph** must be `final-response` (a `stopped`/`budget-exhausted`/`died` verifier reached no verdict — it re-dispatches or surfaces, never silently approves), and the **terminal response** carries the verdict token the binding's predicate names. `gate_return_on(verifier.approve)` reads `verifier.approve` as "the gating verifier's result message epitaph is `final-response` and its terminal response affirms." The affirmation predicate is a total classification of the response text (a leading `APPROVE`/`REJECT` verdict line the verifier's soul instructs it to emit), so approve and reject partition every finished-verifier outcome with no third state — a reframe of the special case into the general path, not an answer apiece (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`).

**gate_return_on — the delivery-hold (hardest, designed first).** `gate_return_on(<predicate>)` is what makes a worker's return *wait* for a verifier. It is a **hold on the worker result's delivery commit**, not a lock or a resident wait-state. When a worker result is drained (§2.11) under a workflow that binds `worker_return: [dispatch(verifier), gate_return_on(verifier.approve)]`, the hop does **not** apply the worker's work-product transfer and delivery commit (§2.6) yet; it instead dispatches the verifier off the worker's terminal ref and **leaves the worker result undelivered in the inbox**. The held state is therefore *entirely disk-derived* — "a worker result is present in the inbox, a verifier for it has been dispatched, and that verifier has not yet approved" is a query over the inbox and the verifier's branch, never a stored flag (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`). The gate lifts on the *verifier's* return: `verifier_approve` runs `deliver_result` — the once-held worker result now drains normally (transfer + delivery commit, §2.6) — while `verifier_reject` runs `dispatch(worker, with: verifier.feedback)`, re-dispatching the worker with the verdict as steering and discarding the rejected result. The latch is single by construction: the worker result is delivered *exactly once*, when a verifier approves, and "already delivered" is the ordinary transcript record, not a second fact. Because the gate is a deferral of one commit and its state is a disk query, no code path branches on "am I gating" held in memory — the hold survives a crash and re-derives identically on the next hop.

**The closed action set (v0.7).** Actions are a **closed set**: `dispatch(role[, with:][, mode:])`, `gate_return_on(predicate)`, `deliver_result`, `land_compaction`, `mark_abandoned`, `notify_ui`. Adding an action is a **code** change; a binding is **config**. The set is closed *both ways*: `spawn_root_agent` and `spawn_exchange` were subtracted (bl-0e79) because §2.4 leaves no circumstance a hop could fire them from — a user message *resumes the agent's own branch* ("Reprompt is a message"), a new root agent is forked explicitly off a config branch's head (§2.4, §3.4 CLI as control plane), and an exchange "owns no branch, no merge, no lifecycle", so there is nothing to spawn. They are **declined at parse** with that reason rather than accepted and ignored, and no config ships them; the interpreter's executor match is correspondingly exhaustive over this set, so vocabulary the interpreter can never reach cannot re-enter it (`src/prompt/tests/workflow_vocabulary.rs` sweeps every shipped config through parse and the interpreter, and pins the deferred set). This is the severability line (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md`): the closed set is the interpreter's vocabulary, and experiments recombine that vocabulary without touching the harness. Two actions are git-native **ref marks**, the same pattern as `budget-exhausted`/`declined-transfer` (§2.6, §3.5) and read by the frontend as orthogonal state, never pushed to it (§3.5 — the UI is a pure reader, there is no push channel): `mark_abandoned` writes `refs/litany/abandoned/<agent-id>`, a **non-derivable policy assertion** ("this stopped branch will not be retried" — distinct from the derivable `stopped` classification, so it is a real fact with a home, not a mirror of one), and `notify_ui` writes `refs/litany/notify/<agent-id>`, the mark a frontend surfaces as a user-facing notification (distinguishing "the user should look" from ambient state — not every terminal warrants a notification, and the binding chooses which do). `land_compaction` is the compaction landing (§2.6) — the rebase-forward, bound on `compactor_return` and epitaph-gated: only a `final-response` compactor return lands (§2.6/§2.7), any other epitaph delivers instead. The retired spelling `compaction_merge` parses to the same action — the merge-back mechanism it named is gone (bl-bc9c), but configs are frozen commits (§2.2) and existing workspaces' vocabulary must keep resolving. `deliver_result` is the §2.6 result delivery, gated by `gate_return_on`. The bl-c33b workflow appendix (§2.5) composes *after* the governing config's `workflow.yaml` at read time, and its terminal→result-message binding is just one more binding this same interpreter evaluates — the appendix is not a special mechanism, it is data in the same list.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-6a3b).** The interpreter's home and its terminal-lifecycle seam are shipped. The closed action/event set is reconciled to this section (the merge-back-era `gate_merge_on`/`merge` are renamed to the post-§2.6 `gate_return_on`/`compaction_merge` (since re-spelled `land_compaction`, §2.6 bl-bc9c), `deliver_result` is added, the §2.1-era `spawn_root_conversation`/`spawn_exchange` are gone entirely — subtracted by bl-0e79, above — and `compactor_return` joins the event set) in `src/config/action.rs` and `src/config/workflow.rs`, with regenerated `schemas/workflow.json`. The parsed `events` now reach the driver: `resolve::WorkerConfig` carries the typed bindings (previously discarded), and the hop evaluates the `branch_stopped` lifecycle event at the stopped terminal (`src/prompt/workflow_actions.rs` — the interpreter `actions_for(event)` plus the `mark_abandoned`/`notify_ui` ref-mark executors), so a `branch_stopped: [mark_abandoned, notify_ui]` binding takes effect **config-only**, the first end-to-end proof that a binding acts at runtime. Deferred and tracked (children of this epic): the per-step hook firing points (`pre_step`/`post_step`/`on_tool_return`), the `worker_return`→verifier dispatch + `gate_return_on` delivery-hold, the `verifier_approve`/`verifier_reject` derivation-and-execution, and `deliver_result`/`compaction_merge` (now `land_compaction`) execution — the last coordinated with the concurrent real-compaction work (bl-9dbd). The v0.7 success criterion (a verifier gating a worker's return, config-only) is **designed in full above** and demonstrated in part (the interpreter drives a lifecycle binding at runtime); its verifier-gate end-to-end lands with those tracked children.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-2d5a; the landing this note describes is the retired merge — the mechanism is now rebase-forward and the action's spelling `land_compaction`, §2.6 bl-bc9c).** The compaction bindings are **live**, and the delivered-child-result circumstance is now a real seam of the interpreter. `src/prompt/dispatch/child_result.rs` derives a returning child's role from its dispatch commit subject (`dispatch: <role> [<id>]`, the single authoritative home — `src/prompt/role.rs`, shared with role-aware resolution below), names the lifecycle event (`compactor` → `compactor_return`, else `worker_return`), and runs the bound actions against the child's context: **`compaction_merge`** lands the returning compactor's branch via `compactor::merge` and consumes the trigger message (the merge commit is the record, never a transcript entry); **`deliver_result`** applies the work-product transfer and delivers the result message to the transcript. An unbound event falls back to its §2.6 baseline (a compactor result merges, every other result delivers), and the merge is epitaph-gated wherever it is bound (§2.6/§2.7, bl-1c94): a non-`final-response` compactor return delivers instead of merging. The step-boundary drain (`src/prompt/dispatch/drain.rs`) now *leaves* result messages in the inbox for this interpreter rather than transferring them itself, and `litany advance` resolves the workflow and interprets them before deriving warrant (`src/prompt/dispatch/advance.rs`). The **checkpoint** is wired at the `advance` step boundary (`hop.rs`): `compactor::checkpoint::state`+`due` reads the `compaction:` clock and, when due, runs the `worker_flush` bindings (`dispatch(compactor)`, forking a compactor off the tip C). **Role-aware resolution** is landed: `resolve::resolve_worker` derives the agent's own role from its dispatch commit subject and reads `souls/<role>.md` and the role's `providers.yaml` assignment (a dispatched compactor resolves `souls/compactor.md` + haiku, not the worker default), and the step injects the built-in compactor toolset (`write_summary`/`mark_for_deletion` schemas, `compactor::builtin_tool_schemas`) for the compactor role alone. Still deferred to bl-bd8a (the verifier gate): the per-step hook firing points, `worker_return`→verifier dispatch + the `gate_return_on` delivery-hold, `verifier_approve`/`verifier_reject`, and the collapse of lifecycle-binding evaluation onto the root `run_exchange` path (today only `advance` interprets the child-result and checkpoint seams).

> **Shipped-state note (bl-bd8a).** The **v0.7 success criterion is met**: a verifier gating a worker's return runs end-to-end, config-only, on the stub-adapter harness (`src/prompt/tests/verifier_gate.rs`). (1) **The delivery-hold.** `worker_return: [dispatch(verifier), gate_return_on(...)]` leaves the worker result undelivered in the inbox and forks a verifier off the **worker's terminal ref** (`src/prompt/dispatch/child_result/verifier.rs`; the fork point is threaded through `subagent::SpawnRequest`/`ChildDispatchRequest` as `fork_point`, defaulting to the parent tip) — so the verifier inherits the work to judge while its id, and thus its return address, stays `<parent>-<sub>`. `gate_return_on` is a no-op executor: the hold *is* the disk state ("worker result present + a verifier dispatched for it + not yet approved"), queried by ancestry (`already_gated`), never flagged; re-interpreting a held return is idempotent. (2) **The verdict.** A returning verifier's event is derived from its own result message — epitaph `final-response` + a leading `APPROVE` line ⇒ `verifier_approve`, else `verifier_reject` (a total partition). `verifier_approve` drains the held worker result through the ordinary `deliver_result`; `verifier_reject` re-dispatches the worker with `with: verifier.feedback` and discards the rejected result. The gated worker result is found by ancestry (the verifier forked off it), no sidecar. Interpretation is **two-pass** (verdicts before worker/compactor results) so an approving verifier drains its worker within the same hop. (3) **Per-step hooks.** `pre_step` / `post_step` / `on_tool_return` fire inside `hop::step` (advance-native, bracketing the model call); the shipped executor runs the git-native ref marks (`notify_ui` / `mark_abandoned`), a `dispatch`-in-hook executor being a tracked follow-on. (4) **The prompt→advance collapse.** `run_exchange` now evaluates the child-result interpretation, the compaction checkpoint, and the terminal-lifecycle bindings at the same seams `litany advance` does (the workflow threads through `dispatch::Resolved`), so both drivers run one evaluation.

---

## 7. UI

### 7.1 Live view

The UI watches the workspace filesystem (§3.5) and re-renders from what it observes. Top of the interface shows the workspace's git tree: config branches and the agent tree forked from them. Clicking a commit navigates to that point. Clicking a branch navigates to that agent.

Live indicators:

- Line-by-line streaming text for model calls in flight.
- Pulsing indicators for tool calls.
- Arrows to child branches for active children.
- Distinct patterns for model-call states: queued, in flight, streaming, terminal.
- Agent-state markers — live, quiescent, stopped (§3.5) — with declined-transfer and budget-exhausted ref marks rendered alongside (§2.6, §6).
- **Agents are labelled by their name where they have one** (§2.1, §2.3) — read from `agents/<id>:name`, the fact's one home, and falling back to the id, which every agent has. A frontend keeps no naming table of its own: labelling is a read of the same committed fact `litany message` resolves against, so what an operator sees named is exactly what they can address by that name.
- Pending-message indicators for agents with a non-empty inbox (§2.11); delivered messages appear as ordinary delivery commits in the tree, and a child's return appears as its result message plus, when files came back, the work-product transfer commit (§2.6).

An exchange (§2.4) is a rendering span, not a structure: the UI groups a user message with the steps and terminal response that answer it on the root agent's linear history. The grouping is presentational; nothing structural corresponds to it.

### 7.2 History view

Clicking an old commit is read-only by default. Forking from history — starting a new agent off an old commit to explore a counterfactual — is the ordinary fork with a historical ref argument (§2.3): no special branch prefix, no distinct operation. Provenance is the ancestry — the new branch's fork point is visible in the graph, which is what accounting and replay read — so the UI distinguishes forked-from-history agents visually, never structurally. The argument is spelled `--from <ref>`, on `litany prompt` (a new root off any commit) and on `litany dispatch` (a child off a ref other than its parent's tip) alike — §2.3 shipped-state note; a frontend issues it like any other verb invocation (§3.5).

### 7.3 Concurrent exchanges

Root agents multiply freely: the user may open a second piece of work while the first runs, or speak twice to the same one. Two intents, two existing primitives (§2.4): a *new question* is a new root agent — `litany prompt`, its own branch forked off a config branch's head (§2.3), running independently; a *course correction or follow-up* is `litany message` into the existing agent's inbox, delivered at its next step boundary — and if that agent is quiescent, the deposit itself starts the driver (§2.11). Steering a running exchange and reprompting a finished one are the same verb. No mechanism is required beyond the branch invariants (§2.3) and the inbox.

---

## 8. Metrics and Observability

First-class metrics, written to commit trailers and event log. All counts are reported along three scope axes, which are not interchangeable:

- Tokens per step, per agent, per workspace — summed across *every* attempt segment of each step's `response.json`, not only the contributing ones (§4.4 segment authority); failed and superseded attempts are billed (§4.4, §6 budgets), each step's `Usage` folded by §6's overlap rule so a cached slice contained in the prompt counter is billed once. This is the **spend** axis and `response.json` is its only source. A *committed output's own* token cost is a different fact with a different home: the `usage` the transcript entry carries (§2.3 *Usage rides the entry*), which a transcript reader states without opening `steps/` at all.
- Model calls per step (always 1), tool calls per step, attempts per model call (≡ API calls, §2.1; derived as the segment count of the step's `response.json`, §4.4 — never stored).
- Cost per step, per agent, per workspace (derived from the all-segments token sum above).
- Silent-death sweep and count per workspace. This is a critical health metric: a ballooning count indicates failure somewhere in the return pipeline. With merge-back deleted (§2.6), branch count itself is not a health axis — every agent branch persists by design until GC (§9.2) — so the signals are liveness and return: candidates are enumerated by `git branch --list 'agents/*'`, filtered to branches with *no live executor* (the §2.11 lock probe) that either died mid-work (the latest step never settled complete, §2.3: `response.json` closed without a terminal `end`, §2.9, or its final segment terminated in an `Error` — retries exhausted or a non-retryable error, §2.10) or, for a child, never deposited a result message (§2.6). **"For a child" is a query, not string arithmetic**: the parent's *address* is derived from the id (§2.11), but whether that address names an agent is asked of the `agents/*` registry (§2.3) — the same intersection §2.11 states for the flush's inbox listing, applied to the sweep's half. A branch whose derived address holds no ref (an id outside the two-token descent grammar, or one whose parent ref was deleted) is nobody's child: no parent inbox, so it is treated exactly as a root and nothing is asked of git. Every candidate is **named** in the scan report, not merely counted: a root gets no `died` deposit (no parent inbox), so its name in the report is the one place an operator learns which branch went quiet. A long-lived child still working (legitimate per §2.5, §2.11) holds its lock and is never counted; a quiescent root agent awaiting its next message (§2.4) has a terminal `end` and is never counted. The sweep is not only a counter: for each **child** in the candidate set that never deposited — a hard crash (SIGKILL, OOM, panic) that ran no handler, so neither the stop path (§2.9) nor the budget path (§6) deposited for it — it **deposits on the child's behalf**, a result message with a `died` epitaph (§2.6), so the return path is total even across uncatchable death and the parent is revived rather than stalled. Its trigger is the operator verb `litany scan` (§2.11): run by hand, or by cron if an operator wants a heartbeat — a transient process, no watcher, no schedule, and never wired into any driver's hot path (the events it compensates for happen at crash rate, not step rate; §2.11 "Crashes are a failure class"). The deposit's sender is the **child**, not the harness: the parent's inbox is sender-namespaced (§2.11) and the "a message from the child exists" derivation depends on it, so the sweep writes as the child — the sweep is the scribe, not the author. No watcher process is added; §2.11 forbids one and none is needed, because stop is SIGTERM-catchable and budget exhaustion is voluntary (those deposit through the executor, §2.9, §6), so only hard crash reaches the sweep, and the sweep already enumerates exactly that candidate set. Same query, one added action, zero new mechanism. **Shipped (bl-d148, bl-5846, `src/prompt/inbox/scan/`):** the sweep runs behind `litany scan` (§2.11 Shipped-state note), and the candidate set is the `agents/*` enumeration exactly as written above — the workspace substrate landed the ref prefix and flipped this seam in the same change (`workspace::agent_ids`). The died-mid-work read is the framing-tail `response.json` classification (`segment::classify`, §4.4), and the never-deposited derivation reads the parent's inbox and transcript by sender, so the sweep's own `died` deposit is idempotent across re-scans. **The derivation's durable home is the returned mark (bl-2c06):** every result deposit — the executor's terminal paths and the sweep's own `died` deposit alike, all through the one `inbox::deposit_result` seam — writes `refs/litany/returned/<child-id>` at the child's terminal ref, immediately after the message file lands. The mark exists because the deposit's downstream traces are consumable: a compactor return is consumed by `land_compaction` with no transcript entry (the base commit is the record, §2.6 — and a `NoOp` landing mints no base at all), and even a delivered `messages/<NNN>-<child>.md` can be squashed away by a later compaction — so "no evidence" cannot be read as "never returned" without fabricating a death for a child that returned cleanly (a false `died` epitaph delivered into the parent's transcript, the observed bl-2c06 defect). The sweep's returned read is therefore mark-first, with the inbox and transcript reads kept as the deposit-crash-window belt (the file lands before the mark, so the gap between the two is covered by the inbox read) and for workspaces predating the mark. The mark shares `workspace::MARK_REF_ROOT`, so `litany delete` recycles it with the agent (§9.2), and it stays an assertion the sweep reads by name — never rendered as an agent state (§3.5).
- Undelivered-message count per workspace: files under `inbox/<agent-id>/` (§2.11) whose agent has no live executor (the §2.11 lock probe). Derived from the inbox listing plus the liveness observation — no flag, no sidecar (§2.11 "Undelivered is derived"); transient under normal operation, since a deposit into a quiescent agent starts a driver (§2.11).
- Step duration, tool call duration, compaction duration.
- Compaction ratio (tokens before / tokens after).

---

## 9. Testing and Replay

**A test bound counts silence, not time (bl-2bf0).** The end-to-end tests observe a detached driver the only way a frontend can — by watching the workspace on disk (§3.5) — and their pass path is satisfied by observable state *however slowly it arrives*. A wall-clock deadline over that is therefore not a property of the code under test but of the machine, the same objection §2.9 raises against a deadline on the leader-pgid re-read ("a deadline measured under load reports the load") and answers with a retry *count*. The e2e analogue counts **consecutive probes that observed no change anywhere in the workspace tree** (`src/e2e/poll.rs`, the one primitive every e2e poll waits on): a live driver writes continuously — delivery commits, transcript entries, streamed `response.json` event lines, worktree churn — and a wedged or absent one writes nothing at all. A slow box only makes the pass path slower, never redder; the only verdict left is genuine silence, which is the hang the bound was always there to catch, and it is reported as such — *nothing is driving this workspace* — rather than as an expired stopwatch. The distinction is not academic: the 120s deadline this replaced hid a real stranded-deposit defect behind a timeout that read like machine load.

### 9.1 Task suite

A task suite of 50–200 manually constructed tasks with machine-checkable success criteria. Target baseline pass rate: approximately 40%, with failure modes distributed across categories:

- Early termination.
- Scope reduction.
- Skipped tests.
- Hallucinated APIs or facts.
- Error recovery failure.
- Fabricated success claims.
- Context hygiene failures (needle-in-haystack, compaction ratio, prompt injection resistance).

Each category has sufficient tasks (≥10) for statistical movement to be detectable. Each task runs N≥5 times per evaluation.

**Primary metric: pass@1** — the mean per-task pass rate. For each task, compute `#passes / N`; then take the mean across tasks (mean-of-means, with N fixed per task). Report 95% Wilson score intervals on the mean. This captures **reliability**: can the harness solve tasks consistently?

**Secondary metric: pass@5 (any-of-5)** — for each task, did any of 5 runs pass? Report fraction of tasks. This captures **ceiling capability**: what is the harness ever capable of solving? A workflow variant that lifts pass@1 without lifting pass@5 is noise reduction; a variant that lifts both is a real capability gain.

Optimization target is pass@1; pass@5 is tracked to distinguish capability shifts from reliability shifts.

> **Shipped-state note.** The suite is authored as data under `tests/suite/` (bl-8094): one YAML file per failure category, each task carrying an `id`, a `prompt`, an optional `setup`, a machine-checkable `check` (shell; exit 0 is the sole pass signal, so success is observable state, never the agent's own claim), and category tags — format in `tests/suite/README.md`. It holds 50 tasks with ≥10 tasks per §9.1 category, reached via secondary tags (a task provoking two failure modes carries both) because seven categories cannot each hold ten of fifty disjointly; `tests/suite.rs` enforces the count, the seven-tag closed set, uniqueness, and file-placement-is-primary-tag. The ~40% baseline pass@1 (v0.9 criterion) is a property of *running* the suite against a live model; the runner that would measure it has now landed (§9.3 shipped-state note), but the crate itself asserts only its own correctness, never a model score.

### 9.2 Replay and archival

A workspace is long-lived; "a run" is an agent subtree within it. The archival unit follows the agent, not the workspace (the v0.3 tarball-per-run died with the repo-per-root layout — there is no per-run directory to tar):

- **Archive.** One `git bundle` of the agent's branch, its descendants' branches (`agents/<id>`, `agents/<id>-*`), and the subtree's **governing lineage** — every `config/*` ref whose history reaches it (§2.2). A full bundle is self-contained in both senses the replay needs: the ancestry carries the governing config commit as an *object*, and the lineage refs carry the thing that commit is *derived from*. Governance is a query, not a field — the nearest ancestor of the branch reachable from a `config/*` ref — so an archive that drops the refs keeps the bytes and loses the derivation, and the replayed workspace has nothing to take the merge-base against. A config branch that advanced past the fork is no longer an ancestor of the agent, yet it is the ref the merge-base is taken *against*, so the lineage travels at its heads and the replay re-derives over the same candidate set rather than an approximation of it. Still no sidecar: the refs are the single source. Alongside the bundle ride the matching diagnostic slices — `steps/<id>*/` and `inbox/<id>*/` (plain directories outside git, §2.2). One bundle plus two slices is the whole run.
- **Replay.** Clone the bundle into a scratch workspace (`LITANY_HOME` keeps it isolated, §2.2) and restore the slices. Every §2.10 guarantee then holds verbatim: each step's read state is a commit in the bundle, assembly re-derives the wire input from it, and `response.json` framing classifies the outcome. Inspection is the ordinary frontend over the scratch workspace (§3.5); replay is not a mode (§2.3) — and *drivable* is the test of that claim: the scratch takes a fresh `litany prompt` off the config head that rode the bundle, and the replayed agent advances on its own governing config commit. What stays machine-local is what a config only names: the global `models.yaml` and the brazen provider *rows* (§4.2, §4.4 "Row names are the portability contract").
- **Retention and GC.** Deleting an expired or archived agent is deleting its branches; `git gc` prunes whatever objects nothing else reaches. Reachability *is* the retention policy: an ancestor shared with a config branch or a still-live sibling survives automatically, and nothing needs to compute what is safe to drop. The `steps/` and `inbox/` slices are plain directories, removed with the branches. The act is the verb **`litany delete <workspace> <agent> [--children] [--dry-run]`**, and `bundle` composes in front of it: bundle-then-delete is the archive path, delete outright is the other, and neither verb carries a flag for the other (§3.4 one obvious path). *When* to delete is the operator's policy, not the harness's: nothing expires on a timer, and no default retention window exists to be silently enforced.

A whole-workspace snapshot stays trivially available — `tar` the workspace directory (repo, steps, inboxes) — and remains the right unit for machine migration. It is not the archival unit, because a workspace never ends; agents do.

> **Shipped-state note.** `litany bundle <workspace> <agent> <out-dir>` and `litany replay <archive>` ship the archival unit (bl-8094, `src/archive/`; re-based onto the workspace substrate by bl-a51c, given its governing lineage by bl-aabb). `bundle` enumerates `agents/<agent>` and its `agents/<agent>-*` hyphen-descendant branches (§2.3) with `git branch --list` against the bare `repo.git`, adds the subtree's governing lineage (`workspace::config_lineage` — the `config/*` refs a merge-base against the subtree root resolves; a fresh orphan config lineage shares no ancestor and does not ride), writes one `git bundle` of them all, and copies the matching `steps/<id>*` and `inbox/<id>*` slices beside it — one bundle plus two slices. `replay` derives the subtree-root (**primary**) id as the shortest agent id in the bundle — the config refs are not agents and are not counted — fetches every branch into a fresh bare `repo.git` at `replays/<primary-id>/` under `LITANY_HOME`'s data root (isolation, §2.2), materializes the primary's worktree under `agents/`, restores the slices, and prints the scratch-workspace path; the ordinary frontend (§3.5) then inspects it — replay is not a mode.
>
> **`litany delete` ships the retention half (bl-0d9e).** `src/archive/delete.rs`, behind the verb above. **The target set is the union of the id's homes, not the ref list.** An agent is five things — an `agents/<id>` ref, a worktree at `agents/<id>/`, `steps/<id>/`, `inbox/<id>/`, and any `refs/litany/<kind>/<id>` marks — so the subtree (`<id>` plus its `<id>-*` hyphen-descendants, §2.3) is derived by asking all five who is present, and the removal covers whatever each remembers. Two consequences: nothing is left half-dead for a frontend to render (§3.5), and a delete that died half-way is *completed by re-running it* — the survivors are still enumerated, and a run against an agent nothing remembers is a quiet success over an empty set. Convergence is therefore the general path with empty inputs, not a resume mode, which is also why delete does not run the existence guard the other id-taking verbs run (§2.11): those decline an absent id because their act would silently do nothing, while delete's postcondition is exactly that absence. **Two refusals, both fail-closed and both checked over the whole target set before anything is removed**: a bare delete of an agent with descendants is declined naming them (`--children` is the explicit subtree request, mirroring `stop --stop-children`, §2.9), and an agent whose executor holds the §2.11 lock is declined naming the lock — reaping the substrate beneath a live driver is a race with a running process, so `litany stop` comes first. **`--dry-run` is the plan, and the plan is the receipt**: the verb's one product (§3.4) is the same line either way — `would delete <id>; descendants: N (…); pending deposits: M` against `deleted <id>; …` — so a frontend's confirmation enumerates what dies in the sentence the receipt will later confirm, rather than parsing it out of a refusal. The alternative shape — refuse-first, then `--force` — was rejected: it spends a second refuse-then-opt-in axis on a verb that already spends one on `--children`, and it puts the census on the failure channel. The **pending deposits** count is what the confirmation needs beyond the descendant names: mail addressed *to* the subtree dies with its inboxes, while a deposit one of them *sent* already lives in the recipient's inbox and is untouched. Marks are reaped by enumerating their shared root `refs/litany/` (`workspace::MARK_REF_ROOT`) rather than the four kind-prefixes, so a fifth mark namespace is covered the day it lands.

> **The lineage refs are the fix for a void archival story (bl-aabb).** Shipping the agent refs alone produced a replay no verb could drive: `litany prompt` died at `rev-parse refs/heads/config/default` and `litany advance` at “no config/* ancestor”, because the governing commit is derived against the `config/*` heads and the archive named none. The regression is now a verb-level test (`src/e2e/replay_drive.rs`): a bundle→replay→`prompt` round-trip drives to a landed step, and a replayed agent's `governing_config` equals the source workspace's — both over the shared `httpmock` / real-`bz` wire fixture.
>
> **Delivery ordering is unchanged, deliberately.** The same finding observed a hop that committed its delivery and *then* failed to resolve config, leaving the branch delivered-but-unanswered. A hop delivers before it resolves (§6, lazy resolution) and continues to: a delivered message is a committed transcript entry, and warrant is derived from the transcript tail (§2.3, §6), so the tail reads user-side and the next hop finds a model call due — the message is landed-and-pending, never consumed-and-lost. Resolving first would make every no-op hop pay a config read (deleting the pin-1 terminator's “costs nothing but the probe” property, §2.11) and would add a second guard for a fact the bundle now establishes at its source.

### 9.3 Experiments

Experiments are workflow config variants:

```
experiments/
├── baseline/
│   └── workflow.yaml -> ../../template/workflow.yaml
├── strict-verifier/
│   └── workflow.yaml
└── parallel-workers/
    └── workflow.yaml
```

An **evaluation run** — **eval** for short — is (experiment × suite × N), producing per-task pass@1 and per-category failure breakdowns. It evaluates harness *configuration*, holding tasks and models fixed; `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §7 (*litany's stance*) pins the term against the field's other "eval" senses (it is not model benchmarking, not LLM-as-judge grading, not §8's production monitoring). A new experiment is a config diff; no code changes.

**The baseline is the template (bl-aa9a).** A variant is a diff against the shipped default (§2.2's `template/`), so the baseline's diff is empty — and an empty diff has nothing to store. `experiments/baseline/workflow.yaml` is a symlink to `template/workflow.yaml`, the same one-fact-one-home idiom as this repo's `CLAUDE.md -> AGENTS.md`, so the resolver needs no fallback and the baseline cannot drift from the default. It had already begun to: the hand-copy this replaced carried the dead `spawn_root_agent` verb §6 subtracted, in duplicate. Editing the baseline is editing the default — a `template/` change.

> **Shipped-state note.** The runner (`agent-eval run --config/--suite/--runs`; the run verb gained its `compare` sibling with bl-36fa, below) shipped in the separate binary crate `crates/agent-eval` (bl-25fc, v0.10) — kept out of the litany core so experiment-driver concerns never leak into the harness. Its layout: `suite` loads the task set (§9.1), `experiment` resolves `experiments/<name>/workflow.yaml`, `runner` executes experiment × suite × N (per run: an isolated `LITANY_HOME` + working directory, the task `setup`, the agent invocation, then the task `check` — exit 0 the sole pass signal, §9.1), `stats` aggregates the outcomes into pass@1 with 95% Wilson intervals and pass@5 (overall and per §9.1 category), and `report` renders them. The **agent invocation is behind a trait** (`agent::Agent`): the production `CommandAgent` shells out to an external harness-driver binary, but tests substitute a fake, so the whole runner is exercised without live model traffic (the 100% coverage gate forces this). A failing run is optionally bundled for triage (§9.2) through a symmetric `agent::Bundler` seam over the shipped `litany bundle`. bl-8094 shipped the two prerequisites — bundle/replay (§9.2) and the task suite (§9.1) — and the `experiments/baseline/` variant (the v0.9 default workflow, linked rather than copied — above) ships alongside the runner; the ~40% ± 5% baseline pass@1 measurement (§9.1, v0.9) is a property of *running* the suite against a live model and is not asserted by the crate.
>
> **The driver shipped; the seam is closed at both ends (bl-2e28).** The far side is **`litany-eval-agent`** (`crates/litany-eval-agent`, workspace-internal `publish = false` like the runner): an integration and therefore an external binary (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Integrations are external binaries"), never a `litany` verb, and it drives the harness exclusively through the front door by exec'ing `litany` from `PATH`. Per run it seeds the isolated `LITANY_HOME` with the machine's wire configuration — `models.yaml` plus the `template/` config-root override, the two existing front doors; the wire is machine-local by design (§4.2, §9.2), so it must travel into every isolated run home or no run can reach the operator's provider rows — then `litany new`s the run's workspace, applies the experiment, and `litany prompt`s the task prompt grounded in the run's shared working directory, reporting workspace path and agent id back through `LITANY_EVAL_REPORT` (the two-line file `litany bundle` needs, §9.2). `--agent` stays required with no default, deliberately: which driver runs the agent under test is an experiment-defining input, so it is named at every invocation. The driver contract stays documented in the repo README ("Run the suite"); `LITANY_EXPERIMENT` arrives canonicalized, since the driver's cwd is the per-run working directory, not the runner's.
>
> The second end — the quiet one — closes the same way it dangled: `CommandAgent` sets **`LITANY_EXPERIMENT`** per run and still **nothing under `src/` reads it** — the harness resolves `workflow.yaml` from the workspace's config commit (§2.2), never from the environment. *Applying* the experiment is the driver's own act: `litany-eval-agent` runs `litany config` with `$EDITOR` set to lay the experiment over the authoring checkout's `workflow.yaml`, so the experiment lands as an ordinary config commit — the only place the harness reads workflow policy from. "A new experiment is a config diff; no code changes" is thereby exercised end to end, through the ordinary authoring verb rather than any bespoke hook (for `baseline` the copy changes nothing and the pass declines — an empty diff is already in force; `experiments/single-attempt/` ships as the smallest non-empty diff, the retry loop disabled).
>
> **The eval reports quality, efficiency, and its own reproducibility inputs (bl-36fa).** An evaluation is only half a measurement until two of them can be compared, so the runner's product is now an **evaluation record** — `agent-eval run --record <out.json>`, one JSON document holding the reproducibility inputs plus every task's per-run observations — and **`agent-eval compare <baseline.json> <candidate.json>`** renders per-task, per-category, and total **baseline → candidate** deltas from two saved records (deltas over the shared task set only; tasks on one side alone are named, never silently dropped; `compare` invokes no driver and no model). Quality stays exactly §9.1 — pass@1 with pooled Wilson intervals and pass@5 — and efficiency rides beside it at the same grains: **outer wall time** (the runner's own measurement around the driver invocation, as distinct from §8's inner per-step spans), model **attempts** (attempt-segment count, §4.4), **tool invocations** (`tool_use` blocks in each step's authoritative segment, §4.4), and the four canonical usage counters (brazen `Usage`), each summed across every attempt segment (§6 billing). The derived metrics come from the workspace the driver disclosed through `LITANY_EVAL_REPORT` — the same two-line report §9.2 bundling consumes, read off the workspace's `steps/` slice with the same descent walk as the §6 budget derivation — so a driver that reports no workspace yields **missing metrics, never zeros** (an unreported counter renders `—`; no price is inferred and no token count fabricated — the runner, like the harness, has no tokenizer). The **reproducibility inputs** printed and recorded are: suite path and git revision (`+dirty` when touched), **starting fixture identity** (sha256 over the suite's task files — their `setup`/`check` scripts are the starting state), experiment name and resolved workflow, driver command plus its self-reported `--version` line (the one non-run probe in the README driver contract; the shipped `litany-eval-agent` answers it), run count, and the observed model/provider sets (models off `message_start` events, provider rows resolved through the run home's `models.yaml`; each `unreported` when unknowable). Everything is exercised by fake drivers and fabricated `steps/` slices — deterministic tests make no network or model call, there is no central telemetry, and a live-model eval remains an explicit operator command (`make eval`, run count stated, money spent), never CI.
>
> **One invocation is the comparison unit (bl-f838).** The point of the switchable workflow (`docs/DESIGN_WORKFLOW_SWITCH.md`, operator ruling 2026-08-31) is testing workflows against each other, and the way that is done is `--config` made **repeatable**: `agent-eval run --config baseline --config <variant> … ` runs the same suite under each named experiment in one invocation — the first is the baseline — and prints the bl-36fa baseline → candidate comparison per later variant. What the one-invocation shape buys is the **controls**: every reproducibility input other than the experiment itself — suite, suite revision, starting fixture, driver, driver version, runs per task — is given and probed exactly once, so the variants differ in precisely the workflow, *held equal by construction* rather than checked after the fact (a repeated experiment name is refused; a deliberate noise measurement names the same content twice via a second, e.g. symlinked, variant directory). Per-variant run directories and failing-run archives are namespaced by experiment name so the arms share nothing on disk, and `--record <path>` becomes a directory of per-variant `<experiment>.json` records, each an ordinary `compare` input on its own. `compare` meanwhile derives the same question for records from *separate* invocations — a `controls:` line naming any control (or observed-model set, both sides reporting) on which the two records differ — because cross-time and cross-harness comparison remains its charter, so a differing control is a fact stated loudly, never a refusal. One `--config` is byte-for-byte the prior contract; no new verb, no new instrumentation, no new stored fact. Filed follow-ups: significance of the pass@1 delta — runs cluster within tasks, so a pooled two-proportion test would be dishonest and the honest shape is paired per task (bl-a35e); a mid-run workflow switch (a workflow-mark schedule) as an experiment axis, a driver-contract extension (bl-0003); interleaving the (experiment, task, run) execution order so provider drift lands evenly across variants of a long live eval (bl-b653). Deliberately *not* filed: model-graded judgment of which output is better — the §9.1 checker is a script, and `docs/TAXONOMY.md` §7 pins the evaluation run against LLM-as-judge grading.

---

## 10. Schema Versioning

Every config declares its schema version in its `version` file (a control file in the config commit, §2.2), and every agent inherits its config's version through ancestry. Old versions remain readable by the harness; migration code is written when a version bumps. Archived bundles and snapshots from any prior version (§9.2) must remain inspectable.

> **Shipped-state note (bl-9e2d).** The `version` file is now **read and enforced** on the one config-load path every step-driving verb goes through (`src/prompt/resolve.rs`, `resolve_worker`). It is the **first** control read from the config commit — before `providers.yaml`, `workflow.yaml`, or the soul — because a commit authored by a newer harness may carry shapes those parsers would misread; declining first means the error names the version, not a downstream parse failure. The supported version is one constant, `config::version::SUPPORTED` (currently `1`); a config declaring a **higher** one is declined loudly with "upgrade litany to read this config" (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Decline illegal operations"), and lower ones stay readable — that is the promise migration code is written against on a bump. Constructing a `Version` **is** the check (`Version::parse(raw, origin)`, the §2.2 content-in-hand seam): there is no separate step a caller can forget and no representable `Version` this build cannot read. An absent `version` is a loud control-read failure, never a defaulted-to-1 guess.

> **Pre-v1 clean break (workspace substrate).** The readability promise above starts at the workspace substrate (§2.2–§2.3). The retired pre-substrate layout — a per-conversation repo with a `root/` primary worktree on `main` and loose control files at the repo root — is **refused, not migrated**: every verb's layout guard declines it with an actionable error naming what was found and what the current layout is (`litany new` creates a fresh workspace). Pre-v1, with no deployed installations to carry forward, a migration path would be mechanism without a user; the version discipline above governs from the substrate onward.

---

## 11. Deferred to Future Versions

Named explicitly so they are not rediscovered later:

- Tool sandboxing and capability restriction — now a named milestone (v1.1, §12) with its spec at §3.6: a WASM/WASI capability clamp over the unchanged §3.3 stdio contract.
- Tool artifact provenance and signing — the v1.1 sandbox (§3.6) bounds a tool's *authority*, not the trustworthiness of its bytes; verifying artifact origin is deferred.
- Multi-user and multi-tenant isolation.
- Distributed execution across machines.
- Secrets management beyond env var injection.
- Sophisticated compaction (rewriting compactors, semantic merging).
- Adversarial compactor defense.
- Adversarial sender defense — front-door messaging (§2.11) lets any sender deposit context into any live branch of the tree, including the root, so a prompt-injected child steering its parent is now one `message` deposit rather than a compaction escape. Provenance is unforgeable and the single-user assumption (§1.1) covers authorization, not influence; the recipient's uniform treatment of every sender (§2.11) is deliberate — uniformity is the feature — so defense is deferred and layered (sender-aware manifest budgets per §5.2, role-scoped send grants) rather than a change to the primitive.
- Cross-conversation memory / shared context.
- Oversized tool-output auto-dispatch (§3.3) — the parsing-subagent handoff lands alongside the subagent dispatch machinery. The §3.3 *bounded transcript projection* (bl-d5fa) is the shipped floor under it: oversized output is head+tail-bounded with an honest marker pointing at the full diagnostic record, not handed to a parser.
- Model Context Protocol (MCP) integration — ruled a **deployment-owned** concern, permanently outside the core (bl-3c76): a bridge binary in the §3.3 `litany-tool-<name>` slot, its tools pinned as ordinary triples into the data-root pools at operator time, credentials and wire protocol the bridge's own (the §4.4 brazen division of labor). No litany code exists or is planned; the design and its refusals are `docs/DESIGN_MCP_BRIDGE.md`.

---

## 12. Milestones

### v0.1 — One model call

**Success criterion:** A single prompt is sent to a provider endpoint, the response is written to disk, and is visible in the conversation repo as a commit. No git branching, no tools, no subagent dispatches.

**Shipped shape.** `litany new <path>` scaffolds a conversation repo from the embedded template; `litany prompt <repo> <message>` loads `providers.yaml` + `agents.yaml`, resolves the `worker` role, invokes `litany-provider-<name>` as a subprocess (§4.1 Historical), writes `exchanges/<ts>-<short-id>.json` with `user_message` / `assistant_response` / `model_id` / `provider` / `usage` / `stop_reason` / `started_at` / `ended_at`, and commits the file to `main`.

**Exceptions to later invariants, historical.** v0.1 committed directly on `main` rather than via an exchange branch merge (§2.3); retired in v0.2, where `litany prompt` spawns an `ex/<ts>-<id>` branch, commits the step snapshot before the model call (§2.10), lands the response as a follow-up commit, dispatches the terminal compactor off the tip (§2.7, stub in v0.2), and `--no-ff` merges the compacted branch back into `main` (§2.6). The earlier `--endpoint` argv pragma was also retired in v0.2: endpoint forwarding now goes through `describe.endpoint_env` per §4.1 Historical.

### v0.2 — Git tree

**Success criterion:** An exchange is a branch, completion is a no-ff merge back to `main`, the repo layout matches the then-current §2.2 (the pre-substrate layout; both the `main`-rooted history and that layout are retired, §2.2/§2.3 Historical). User message → exchange branch → steps as linear commits → compactor (deletion-only, stub is fine) → merge. Unmerged branch count metric available.

**Shipped shape.** `litany prompt <repo> <message>` spawns `ex/<ts>-<short-id>` off `main` in a dedicated worktree under `.litany/worktrees/`, writes `.agent/goal.md` (§2.8) and `exchanges/<ts>-<short-id>/steps/001/request.json`, commits that snapshot before the model call (§2.10), invokes `litany-provider-<name> complete`, lands the normalized response at `steps/001/response.json` as a follow-up commit, then dispatches the terminal compactor by re-entering itself as `litany dispatch compactor <repo> <exchange-branch>` (subprocess per §3.4). The compactor spawns `inv/<ex-id>/<cmp-id>` off the exchange tip, writes its boilerplate `.agent/goal.md` and commits it as the dispatch snapshot (§2.8), then writes `.agent/compactions/001.md` and commits it as the terminal-summary follow-up (stub — no model call; `mark_for_deletion` is a no-op), and `--no-ff` merges back into the exchange branch. Control returns to `litany prompt`, which rebases the exchange onto the current `main` tip and `--no-ff` merges it into `main` (§2.6). The unmerged branch count metric is read directly from `git branch --list 'ex/*' 'inv/*' --no-merged main` — no sidecar file.

### v0.3 — Tools

**Success criterion:** Agent can invoke at least two tools (`bash`, `read_file`). Worktree-modifying tool calls land as commits on the emitting branch (§2.3, §3.3); read-only tool calls produce no commit. Tool contract — binary (`litany tool <name>` in-process or `litany-tool-<name>` external, mirroring §4.4 adapter discovery), JSON schema, `SKILL.md`, the stdio/exit-code shape, and the per-tool-call disk record (`input.json`, `output.json` under `<conv-repo>/steps/<conv-id>/<NNN>/tools/<tool-id>/`, outside every worktree) — pinned in §3.3. Oversized-output auto-dispatch deferred to v0.4+ (§11). Conversation-repo layout migrated from the v0.2 `.agent/`-rooted shape to the v0.3 layout described in §2.2 (control + step records at the conversation-repo root, worktrees as siblings, steps namespaced by conversation id, `merge=ours` on goal/soul/summary, "invocation" retired as a structural term). v0.3.1 follow-on tightens this with the diagnostic-only contract on `request.json` / `response.json` and relocates the step records out of every worktree (§2.3); it also drives `complete` with `stream: true` end-to-end and writes `response.json` as JSONL of §4.4 stream events tail-appended event-by-event (§4.4 "On-disk response shape: JSONL of stream events, always."), with writer-closes-fd as the §3.5 completion signal.

### v0.4 — Subagent dispatch

**Success criterion:** Agent can dispatch a subagent conversation. The subagent runs in its own worktree and branch. Merge-back flow works end-to-end (including the `merge=ours` discipline on `goal.md`/`soul.md`/`summary/**`). Parallel subagent conversations do not corrupt each other's state. Handle-based async works: a dispatch returns immediately with a handle, siblings can be in flight concurrently, and `await(handle)` retrieves the compacted result on a later step (§2.5).

> **Historical.** v0.4's merge-back flow shipped as specced above and is retired by the workspace substrate: children now return results as messages plus a work-product transfer, and the `merge=ours` machinery is **deleted** (bl-4ce8, §2.6 Shipped-state note). This criterion records what v0.4 delivered, not the current contract.

Phase 2 lands the `dispatch` built-in tool — `litany tool dispatch`, input `{role, goal}` — that realizes the §2.5 dispatch primitive on the model-facing side: it reads conversation context from the `LITANY_CONV_REPO` / `LITANY_CONV_BRANCH` env vars (§3.3), spawns the subagent through Phase 1's `litany dispatch <role>` CLI (§3.4), and returns `{status: in_progress, handle}` synchronously. The child's own step loop was the subsequent phase, landed in bl-c33b: `litany dispatch` now deposits the dispatch message through the front door and the launched `litany advance` drives the child (§2.5 Shipped-state note); `worker.rs` is deleted.

Phase 3 lands the `await` built-in tool — `litany tool await`, input `{handle}` — the resolution half of the §2.5 dispatch/await pair. As shipped it blocks until the named subagent reaches a terminal state and emits exactly one of `{status: merged, summary}`, `{status: stopped}`, or `{status: conflicted}` (the merge-era statuses; the substrate dissolves `await` entirely — the child's result arrives as an inbox deposit carrying an epitaph, not a polled status, §2.5, §2.6). Every state read is git-or-fs (`docs/PRINCIPLES.md` "Single source of truth"): merged via `merge-base(handle, parent) == HEAD(handle)`, conflicted via `refs/litany/conflicted/<handle>` (written on rebase failure by the retired merge protocol; the ref name survives as the declined-transfer mark, §2.6), stopped via either of two on-disk signatures — the latest step's `response.json` ending in a §4.4 `error` event (clean failure), or `response.json` having bytes but no terminal `message_stop`/`error` line AND no process holding the fd open (kill-mid-stream §2.9). The kill-mid-stream check reuses the `/proc/<pid>/fd/*` response.json fd-open observation that drives §3.5 `in_flight` classification (single source of truth — the *is-a-model-call-in-flight* fd, distinct from `litany stop`'s executor-lock discovery, §2.9).
